The confession keeps the word and fixes its extent: the power of the keys is "a power or commandment of God, to preach the Gospel, to remit and retain sins, and to administer Sacraments" (AC 1287–1290, per Doc_03), "set forth in its beauty" (AC 912) once the enumeration of sins and the purgatorial arithmetic are removed. The Apology puts the pastoral stake plainly: "if the power of the keys does not console us before God, what, then, will pacify the conscience?" (Ap 4955–4956), and "the keys truly remit sins before God" so that "the voice of the one absolving must be believed not otherwise than we would believe a voice from heaven" (Ap 5148–5152).

**Ecological Function.** G2's authority sense (the community, not Peter alone — Doc_04 §3 G2) and G3/G5's absolution (5.10); refuses 1.3's attrition mechanism and 1.5's reach. Not a generator of its own content — which is why it is Tier 2.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the keys" as a papal or clerical power over people. *World Hearing:* a ministry of the Word held by the whole community, whose only object is sin, and whose whole point is a conscience made sure.

**Key Texts.** v2 2388–2392; v2 8917–8928; Ap 4955–4956, 5141–5152 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R11]; [R12]; [R38] Ap XII. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 1.1 indulgence; 1.3 contrition; 1.5 purgatory; 3.1 the Word; 5.10 confession/absolution; 6.2 we are all priests; 1.2 repentance; 3.3 Scripture against Fathers; 6.7 pope/Antichrist.

#### 1.7 the cross — "Cross, cross"; bearing affliction — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R2, R3, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The affliction a Christian bears — the Theses' answer to preachers of "Peace, peace," the banner of the baptized, and the confession's "true, earnest, and unfeigned mortification" — never a self-chosen discipline.

**World Meaning.** The Ninety-Five Theses end on it: "Blessed be all those prophets who say to the people of Christ, 'Cross, cross,' and there is no cross!" (v1 1506–1510), against those who say "Peace, peace." In baptism a Christian is enrolled under "our Captain, under Whose banner (i. e., the Holy Cross) we continually fight against sin" (v1 2269–2270). The confession claims this teaching as the movement's own kind of mortification: "they have always taught concerning the cross that it behooves Christians to bear afflictions. This is the true, earnest, and unfeigned mortification" (AC 1043–1046) — and the Apology draws the line against the chosen kind: "a true and not a feigned mortification occurs through the cross and afflictions by which God exercises us [when God breaks our will, inflicts the cross and trouble]" (Ap 6939–6942). The phrase "theology of the cross" does not occur in any vendored file; the world's word is the bare noun and the doubled cry.

**Ecological Function.** G5's site under pressure and G12's frame in the body (Doc_05 §8 "embattled"); the affliction-form of 9.2 temptation; the counter-term to 8.1's and 8.5's self-chosen disciplines.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the cross" as a system ("theology of the cross") or as voluntary austerity. *World Hearing:* affliction sent, not chosen; a banner and a battle, not a method.

**Key Texts.** v1 1506–1518; AC 1043–1046; Ap 6938–6944 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R2]; [R37] AC XXVI; [R38] Ap XV. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 5.2 baptism; 9.2 temptation; 8.5 perfection; 4.7 the devil.

### 5.2 Faith, works, and righteousness — the justification cluster

#### 2.1 faith / "faith alone" / "by faith alone" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices, every register. **Registry:** R6, R12, R13, R15, R25, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** Trust that clings to God's promise — the one thing this world says justifies, "without works," and the one thing it calls a "must"; not the knowledge of a story, and not, in this world's own caveat, a slogan that stands on nothing.

**World Meaning.** Faith is a taking hold. "Faith alone is the saving and efficacious use of the Word of God" (v2 11713–11714); "the first and highest, the most precious of all good works is faith in Christ" (v1 6834–6835), and every other good work receives its goodness from it "like a loan" (v1 6841–6842, per Doc_04 §3 G1). The mass "is to be approached, not with any work or strength or merit, but with faith alone. For where there is the word of God Who makes the promise, there must be the faith of man who takes it" (v2 7341–7344). The formula in this world's English is "faith alone" and "by faith alone"; no Latin *sola fide* occurs in any vendored file. The confession says it before the Emperor — "men cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake, through faith" (AC 234–237) — and the Apology says why the word "alone" offends some and must stand: "It is, however, the opinion of merit that we exclude. We do not exclude the Word or Sacraments, as the adversaries falsely charge us… Love also and works must follow faith" (Ap 1097–1101).

What faith is *not* is as carefully said. It is not "merely a knowledge of history… but it is to assent to the promise of God, in which for Christ's sake, the remission of sins and justification are freely offered" — "to wish and to receive the offered promise" (Ap 902–913); it is "confidence which consoles and encourages the terrified mind" (AC 581–583, per Doc_03). It comes from outside: "faith is conceived from the Word" (Ap 1099–1100); "Faith cometh by hearing" (Ap 1045). And its object is always a promise: "Wherever there is a promise faith is required and conversely, wherever faith is required there must be a promise" (Ap 932–935, bracket Bente/Dau's).

In the parish the word is preached as received: the second of the "chief things" is that "whosoever will put his trust in Him, should be free from sin and a child of God… In respect to these two points nothing seems to be lacking or amiss, but they have been rightly preached to you" (v2 14657–14664) — with the immediate warning that "a faith without love is not enough--rather it is not faith at all" (v2 14689–14690). In the sermons faith is the one "must": "The 'must' is that which necessity requires, and which must ever be unyielding; as, for instance, the faith" (v2 14790–14792). And in the household catechism the world states its own caveat against its own formula: "our would-be wise, new spirits assert that faith alone saves, and that works and external things avail nothing… faith must have something which it believes… upon which it stands and rests" (LC 3916–3921). Faith alone — but never faith in nothing: it stands on the Word, the water, the bread, the absolution.

**Ecological Function.** G1's own word, and the term most other entries presuppose: 2.2 (its confessional name), 2.3 (what works receive their goodness from), 2.5 (what it receives), 2.7 (its object), 2.8 (where it is decided), 5.1 (how a sacrament is used — "faith clings to the water," LC 3921–3922), 5.11 (what makes one worthy), 7.5 (the one "must"), 8.1 (why vows cannot merit), 4.4 (trust as the First Commandment's content). Doc_04 §5's G1 row reinforces every gravity but G13, with which it competes ("an ass can almost intone the lessons," v2 14683–14684). A participant who understands this term understands why this world's confession can say "This whole doctrine is to be referred to that conflict of the terrified conscience" (AC 552–553).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* faith as belief-that (assent to propositions), or as a private feeling, or as the slogan "faith alone" heard as license — works do not matter; and, from the other side, as a thing one *does* to be saved. *World Hearing:* trust in a promise spoken from outside, received not produced, standing on Word and sacrament, followed necessarily by love and works that earn nothing — and a formula the world itself had to fence against its own radicals by 1529.

**Key Texts.** v1 6834–6842; v2 7341–7344; v2 11713–11714, 11720–11722; v2 14657–14664, 14689–14690, 14790–14792; LC 3916–3921; AC 234–239, 568–583; Ap 898–935, 1045–1048, 1091–1104, 1114–1122; Hy 1284–1293 (Bacon's English).

**Key Sources.** [R6] *Good Works*; [R12] *Babylonian Captivity*; [R13] *Christian Liberty*; [R15] the Sermons; [R25] LC; [R37] AC IV, XX; [R38] Ap IV, read this pass to 1457 — the article's later reply to the adversaries (1457–4183) unread; [R27] Hymn I. Author Gravity: none — the safest vocabulary in the lexicon (Doc_03 §10.1).

**Related Terms.** 2.2 justification; 2.3 good works; 2.5 grace; 2.7 promise/testament; 2.8 conscience; 2.6 Law and Gospel; 2.10 sin; 3.1 the Word; 5.1 sacrament; 5.11 worthy/examination; 7.5 must and free; 4.4 to have a god; 9.3 assurance; 1.2 repentance; 5.4 for you; 6.8 sects; 8.1 vows; 9.2 temptation.

#### 2.2 justification / to justify / "the righteousness of faith" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1) — [CT], provisional (§2)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [DR] [RT] **[CT]**. **AG:** none by attestation; weighted to Melanchthon's Apology for the technical development. **Registry:** R13, R34, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7); the fourth contest type below concerns present-day usage and characterizes none.

**Quick Meaning.** God's act of accounting a sinner righteous — and, in the same breath, of making one — "for Christ's sake, through faith," which this world's confession calls "the chief topic of Christian doctrine."

**World Meaning.** "This faith God imputes for righteousness in His sight" (AC 238–239). That is the confession's sentence, and the Apology defends it as the matter on which everything turns: "in this controversy the chief topic of Christian doctrine is treated" (Ap 561–562). The founder's own word for the ground is "the merits of another, namely, of Christ alone" (v2 11737–11738), and "since faith alone justifies, it is clear that the inward man cannot be justified, made free and be saved by any outward work" (v2 11744–11745). The Apology's argument runs on the nature of a promise: "Remission of sins is something promised for Christ's sake. Therefore it cannot be received except by faith alone. For a promise cannot be received except by faith alone" (Ap 1186–1188) — "the chief reasoning [a veritable rock] in Paul" (Ap 1202); and "since justification is obtained through the free promise, it follows that we cannot justify ourselves. Otherwise, wherefore would there be need to promise?" (Ap 857–859).

The word does two things at once, and the confessional voice says so on purpose. "We maintain this, that properly and truly, by faith itself, we are for Christ's sake accounted righteous, or are acceptable to God. And because 'to be justified' means that out of unjust men just men are made, or born again, it means also that they are pronounced or accounted just. For Scripture speaks in both ways" (Ap 1080–1085); the bracketed German adds, "The term 'to be justified' is used in two ways: to denote, being converted or regenerated; again, being accounted righteous" (Ap 1085–1087). And so: "by faith alone we are justified, understanding justification as the making of a righteous man out of an unrighteous, or that he be regenerated" (Ap 1120–1122). What is excluded is not works but *confidence* in works: "confidence in the merit of love or of works is excluded in justification" (Ap 1102–1103); what is refused is the *fides formata* by which the schools "do not ascribe justification to faith except on account of love," because then "the remission of sins will always be uncertain, because we never love as much as we ought" (Ap 1384–1391).

The word's purpose, in this world, is certainty. "In this they have sure and firm consolation against the terrors of sin, and against eternal death and against all the gates of hell. [Everything else is a foundation of sand that sinks in trials.]" (Ap 1206–1209). The confession makes the whole doctrine depend on the conscience in conflict, "neither can it be understood apart from that conflict" (AC 552–553). Justification is what a terrified conscience is *given*; the adversaries "bid them doubt whether they obtain remission of sins" and "how will such persons sustain themselves in death?" (Ap 1438–1441). The sharpest single admission in the library is the founder's, to Erasmus: that he alone "saw, what was the [grand] hinge, upon which the [whole] turned" — free will, the ground of merit (Co 15139–15142, OCR as found and recovered per [R34]).

**Ecological Function.** G1's confessional name and the Apology's whole Article IV; presupposes 2.1 (its instrument), 2.10 (its presupposition), 2.7 (its logic), 2.4 (what it excludes), 1.4 (its ground in Christ's satisfaction); lands in 2.8 (its site) and yields 9.3 (its purpose) and 2.3 (what follows it). Doc_05 §9A pattern 1. A participant who understands this term understands why this world's Representative Theological Patterns begin here and why the confession's order puts Article IV before the ministry (V).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a legal fiction ("declared righteous but not changed"), or the reverse, a moral improvement one achieves; or a settled formula whose meaning is obvious. *World Hearing:* one act with two descriptions the confession holds together — accounted and made — received by faith alone, for Christ's sake, whose whole point is that a conscience can be sure; and a word whose exact sense this world's own texts leave two-sided.

**Key Texts.** AC 234–239, 515–516, 546–553; Ap 561–562, 857–888, 1053–1104, 1114–1122, 1186–1223, 1377–1449 (read this pass); v2 11737–11745; Co 15139–15142 (OCR as found).

**Key Sources.** [R37] AC IV, XX; [R38] Ap IV to 1457, read this pass (1457–4183 unread); [R13] *Christian Liberty*; [R34] *Bondage* (OCR-degraded; per-quotation re-check required). Author Gravity: weighted to the Apology for the technical development, the founder attesting the doctrine in every register — the same double classification Doc_03 §10.1 gives.

**CT Contest Type.** *Meaning within its historical context:* the two wordings — "imputes for righteousness" (AC 238) and "the making of a righteous man out of an unrighteous" (Ap 1121) — occur in the world's own texts, and Ap 1085–1087 names them as two uses of one term; how they relate is a specialist question this library does not settle. *Relationship to present-day traditions:* the term is in active use in more than one living tradition that reads these wordings differently; this entry characterizes no present-day reading. **Status: provisional — no secondary source is rowed for the contest (Doc_03 §11 item 4; OG-4); §2 states the leads proposed for the Registry owner and the rule that the tag does not reach a deployment chunk without a row.**

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 2.3 good works; 2.4 merit; 2.5 grace; 2.7 promise/testament; 2.8 conscience; 2.9 free will; 2.10 sin; 1.4 satisfaction; 9.3 assurance; 3.6 Christ alone.

#### 2.3 good works / "works" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R2, R6, R13, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Not the short medieval list of pious acts but everything God has commanded — trade, walking, eating, sleeping — done in faith; necessary because God wills them, never a means of earning anything.

**World Meaning.** "There are no good works except those which God has commanded" (v1 6817–6818). The list is therefore not "praying in church, fasting, and almsgiving" — "they define good works so narrowly" (v1 6866–6870) — but what a Christian does "when they work at their trade, walk, stand, eat, drink, sleep" (v1 6863–6865), all of which are good in faith and none of which are good without it. The confession answers the standing charge: "Our teachers are falsely accused of forbidding good Works" (AC 496); "it is necessary to do good works, not that we should trust to merit grace by them, but because it is the will of God" (AC 585–587, per Doc_05 §9A). The Apology keeps the order fixed: "Love also and works must follow faith. Wherefore, they are not excluded so as not to follow, but confidence in the merit of love or of works is excluded in justification" (Ap 1101–1103). And what the monastic "spiritual" works displaced were "the works of one's own calling, the administration of the state, the management of a family, married life, the bringing up of children" (Ap 6790–6793).

**Ecological Function.** G1's negative (merit refused) and G7's content (the estate as the site of works, Doc_05 §5); lands at 6.4 (the calling) and 8.5 (perfection redefined). The 1517 form: giving to the poor "a better work than buying pardons" (v1 1312–1313).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "works" as charity or ritual, and "faith alone" as their abolition. *World Hearing:* the whole of ordinary life, commanded and therefore good in faith; necessary, and worth nothing as a price.

**Key Texts.** v1 6817–6818, 6863–6870; AC 496, 585–587; Ap 1101–1103, 6788–6793. **Key Sources.** [R6] *Good Works* (opening only read, 6713–6882; the treatise's Decalogue exposition, v1 6883–10599, unread); [R38]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 2.2 justification; 2.4 merit; 6.4 calling; 6.3 office; 8.5 perfection; 4.6 neighbor.

#### 2.4 merit (*meritum congrui* / *meritum condigni*) — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [DR]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R2, R13, R27, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** What this world denies to every human work and reserves to Christ — the scholastic distinctions named in Latin only to be refused.

**World Meaning.** The Theses deny that indulgences are "the merits of Christ and the Saints" (v1 1373–1375, per Doc_03); the confession denies that anyone is justified "by their own strength, merits, or works" (AC 234–235); the Apology names the schools' own vocabulary to reject it — "Their feigning a distinction between *meritum congrui* and *meritum condigni* [due merit and true, complete merit] is only an artifice in order not to appear openly to Pelagianize" (Ap 681–686, bracket Bente/Dau's) — and says what faith brings instead: "This faith brings to God not confidence in one's own merits, but only confidence in the promise" (Ap 868–869). Merit is Christ's alone, "the merits of another, namely, of Christ alone" (v2 11737–11738); the hymn sings "Naught building on my merit" (Hy 1671–1672, Bacon's English). Even the monastic "merits of supererogation" that "these liberal men… sell… to others" are "full of pharisaic vanity" (Ap 9772–9774).

**Ecological Function.** The excluded term of 2.2; the ground of 1.1's refusal and 8.1's; its positive counterpart is 1.4 (b).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "merit" as deserving in general. *World Hearing:* a technical claim that works earn grace — refused in its own Latin, with all merit given to Christ.

**Key Texts.** Ap 681–686, 868–869; AC 234–235; Ap 9762–9774 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R38]; [R37]. Author Gravity: none; the Latin is the adversaries' as Melanchthon quotes it.

**Related Terms.** 2.2 justification; 2.3 good works; 1.4 satisfaction; 1.1 indulgence; 8.1 vows; 8.5 perfection; 2.9 free will.

#### 2.5 grace / "gracious God" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R12, R15, R25, R26, R27, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** God's free favour, given "not because I've earned it or deserved it," which the Creed brings and the sacrament makes a Christian sure of — "I cannot doubt I have a gracious God."

**World Meaning.** In the household the word is learned as a reason: God gives everything "because of His pure, fatherly and divine goodness and His mercy, not because I've earned it or deserved it" (SC 181–183). In the catechism it is the difference between the two parts of the book: the Creed "brings pure grace, and makes us godly and acceptable to God" (LC 2973–2975), where the Commandments only tell "what we ought to do" (LC 2968). God "in free and unmerited mercy makes a beginning and offers us the word of His promise" (v2 7346–7347). And at the altar it becomes the one sentence the world's hope has: "I cannot doubt I have a gracious God" (v2 15723–15724). The hymn sings it as rescue: "God saw, in his eternal grace, / My sorrow out of measure" (Hy 1292–1293, Bacon's English). The confession says baptism offers it (AC 310, per Doc_03).

**Ecological Function.** What 2.1 receives and 2.2 names as free; the Creed's content in 2.6's order; 2.8's comfort in one word. Not an organizer distinct from these.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* grace as a substance infused, or as leniency. *World Hearing:* a favour freely given, received by faith, and known as a *gracious God* — a person, not a quantity.

**Key Texts.** SC 181–183; LC 2967–2975; v2 15723–15724. **Key Sources.** [R26]; [R25]; [R15]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 2.2 justification; 2.6 Law and Gospel; 2.8 conscience; 9.1 comfort.

#### 2.6 Law and Gospel — "commands and promises" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [AS] [TC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices, 1520 and 1529 and 1531. **Registry:** R13, R14, R20, R25, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7). The [AS] tag is Inferential/Thin as a comparative claim (OG-3).

**Quick Meaning.** This world's own way of reading the whole Bible as two things — commands, which show what we ought to do and cannot; and promises, which give what the commands ask — so that a person is first shown his illness and then his remedy.

**World Meaning.** "All the Scriptures of God are divided into two parts--commands and promises… the commands show us what we ought to do, but do not give us the power to do it" (v2 11782–11788); "the promises of God give what the commands of God ask… the promises of God belong to the New Testament, nay, they are the New Testament" (v2 11817–11822). The confessional voice says it as a rule of reading: "All Scripture ought to be distributed into these two principal topics, the Law and the promises" (Ap 579–580, per Doc_04 §2.2). The Law has a work of its own — "to teach men to recognize sin, that they may be made humble unto grace" (v3 12239–12241) — and the Apology states its edge exactly: "The Law worketh wrath… For the Law always accuses and terrifies consciences. Therefore it does not justify, because conscience terrified by the Law flees from the judgment of God" (Ap 820–824). "The Law teaches only the righteousness of the Law"; "the Gospel, which is properly the promise of the remission of sins and of justification for Christ's sake, proclaims the righteousness of faith in Christ, which the Law does not teach" (Ap 861–863).

The distinction is the catechism's own order. The Commandments "teach a man to know his illness," the Creed "teaches him where he may find the remedy" (v2 13202–13210, per Doc_03), and the 1529 book says why the two parts differ: "the Creed is a doctrine quite different from the Ten Commandments; for the latter teaches indeed what we ought to do, but the former tells what God does for us" (LC 2967–2969). It is also the world's shortest rule of politics: "the less law, the more justice; the fewer commandments, the more good works" (v1 10917–10918). It is never taught as a topic of its own; it is the grammar in which faith (2.1), the Word (3.1), the catechism (4.1) and the conscience (2.8) are each spoken — present everywhere it is used, and nowhere as its own subject.

**Ecological Function.** The folded "shared hermeneutic grammar" four gravities state in their own words (Doc_04 §2.2: G1's content, G2's twofold address, G4's order, G5's sequence); Doc_05 §2.4 (ii) makes it the second piece of the world's formation logic, §6C its first interpretive mode, and §8 its affective plot ("terror, then comfort is the world's affective plot, and it is the Law/Gospel fold felt"). Anchors 1.2 (repentance's two parts), 1.3 (the Law's terror), 2.7 (the promise), 4.1 (the catechism's order); presupposed by 3.5 (the literal sense) and 3.4 (what a "doctrine of men" adds beyond either). A central conceptual cluster in the Framework's sense ([121]), which is why it is Tier 1 without being a gravity.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "Law and Gospel" as a labelled theological method, or as Old Testament versus New Testament, or as legalism versus grace in general. *World Hearing:* two kinds of speech found throughout all Scripture — command and promise — read in order on a single person: first the illness, then the remedy; a grammar of reading and of the conscience, not a system, and one this world's texts never name as a doctrine of its own.

**Key Texts.** v2 11782–11788, 11817–11822; v2 13202–13210; LC 2967–2975; Ap 579–580, 820–824, 857–867; v3 12239–12241; v1 10917–10918.

**Key Sources.** [R13] *Christian Liberty*; [R14] the *Kurze Form*; [R25] LC; [R38] Ap IV; [R20] *Secular Authority*. Author Gravity: none; the one-sentence rule is Melanchthon's, the two-part argument Luther's.

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 2.7 promise/testament; 2.8 conscience; 1.2 repentance; 1.3 contrition; 3.1 the Word; 3.2 Gospel; 3.5 letter and spirit; 4.1 catechism; 9.3 assurance; 2.10 sin; 2.5 grace.

#### 2.7 promise / testament — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [AS] (testament) [TC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none — the testament argument is Luther's, "promise" is confessional too. **Registry:** R4, R7, R12, R25, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7). The [AS] tag (for "testament" as this world's name for the Supper) is Inferential/Thin as a comparative claim (OG-3).

**Quick Meaning.** God's word that comes first and gives before any human work — and, at the altar, a *testament*: "a promise made by one about to die," so that the mass is God's promise of forgiveness, not a sacrifice we offer.

**World Meaning.** Nothing begins with us: "not that man begin lay the first stone, but that God alone, without any entreaty or desire of man, must first come and give him a promise" (v1 11044–11047); "This trust and faith is the beginning, middle, and end of all works and righteousness" (v1 11052–11053). The promise is the object faith takes, and the pair cannot be separated: "where there is the word of God Who makes the promise, there must be the faith of man who takes it" (v2 7343–7344). Confession itself rests "only in the most gracious promise of God" (v1 3183–3187, per Doc_03).

At the altar the promise has a name. "A testament, as every one knows, is a promise made by one about to die, in which he designates his bequest and appoints his heirs" (v2 7307–7310); "what we call the mass is the promise of remission of sins made to us by God" (v2 7321–7323, per Doc_04 §3 G3); "both the incarnation and the death of Christ are briefly comprehended in this one word 'testament'" (v2 7336–7337); "the mass, or sacrament of the altar, is Christ's testament" (v2 7292–7294, per Doc_03). A testament is received, not offered — which is the whole difference between this world's mass and the sacrifice it refused (5.6). The confession says the same of every sacrament: "signs and testimonies of the will of God toward us," to which "faith be added to believe the promises" (AC 367–372, per Doc_03), and the Apology makes the pair a rule of everything — "Wherever there is a promise faith is required and conversely" (Ap 932–934, bracket Bente/Dau's) — and the ground of the sacraments' number: "rites which have the command of God and to which the promise of grace has been added" (Ap 6434–6435). The catechism grounds even prayer on it: "besides this command also a promise" (LC 4370–4373, per Doc_03), and comes to the altar "at Thy commandment and promise, which cannot fail or deceive me" (LC 3121–3123, per Doc_05 §2.6).

**Ecological Function.** G3's first pole — "promise and sign" (Doc_04 §3 G3) — and the object of 2.1; the logic of 5.1 (a sacrament is a promise joined to a sign), 5.6 (the mass as testament, not sacrifice), 5.10 (absolution as promise), 9.5 (prayer as command and promise, Doc_05 §2.6) and 9.3 (why the promise makes sure). Doc_05 §2.4 (v): "Every practice is grounded the same way." The second half of 2.6.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "promise" as a hope for the future, and "testament" as a book of the Bible. *World Hearing:* a word God speaks first, now, to be taken by faith; and, as testament, the mass itself — Christ's bequest received by heirs, never a work rendered to God.

**Key Texts.** v1 11044–11053; v2 7292–7294, 7305–7344; AC 367–372; Ap 928–935, 6434–6444 (read this pass); LC 3121–3123, 4370–4373.

**Key Sources.** [R7] *New Testament*; [R12] *Babylonian Captivity*; [R37] AC XIII; [R38] Ap IV, XIII; [R25] LC. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 2.2 justification; 2.6 Law and Gospel; 5.1 sacrament; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 5.6 the mass; 5.10 confession/absolution; 9.5 prayer; 9.3 assurance; 3.1 the Word; 3.2 Gospel; 5.4 for you.

#### 2.8 conscience — "terrified," "anxious," "captive" consciences — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices; the Apology's most repeated adjective ("terrified," 29 times, Doc_04 §9 count). **Registry:** R4, R15, R16, R18, R25, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** The inner court where this world's whole doctrine is decided — the conscience that human laws burden and the pope torments, that is terrified by the Law and comforted by the promise, and for whose sake "many absolutions" exist.

**World Meaning.** "God-fearing and anxious consciences find by experience that it brings the greatest consolation, because consciences cannot be set at rest through any works, but only by faith… This whole doctrine is to be referred to that conflict of the terrified conscience, neither can it be understood apart from that conflict" (AC 546–553). That is the confession's own statement of where its teaching lives. The conscience is a site, and what happens there is a sequence: under the Law it is terrified — "the Law always accuses and terrifies consciences" (Ap 822–823); "terrified consciences waver and hesitate, and then seek and accumulate other works in order to find rest" (Ap 698–699); "in agony of conscience and in conflicts [with Satan] conscience experiences the emptiness of these philosophical speculations" (Ap 818–820) — and under the promise it is comforted: "we must have many absolutions, so that we may strengthen our timid consciences and despairing hearts against the devil and against God" (v2 15694–15695); "I cannot doubt I have a gracious God" (v2 15724).

What binds the conscience wrongly is the world's test for what is wicked. "Consciences are not to be burdened" (AC 396–398); the pope "is a mere tormentor of the conscience" (TT 3083–3084, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim); Christian liberty is preached "only to poor, humble, captive consciences, so that poor children, nuns and monks, who would like to escape from their bondage may… use their freedom" (v2 15915–15920), and "the poor consciences which are by the law of men held in bondage in monasteries and convents" (v2 15902–15904) are its first hearers. The sermons will not "lead astray those of weak conscience" (v2 14960–14961). Even false worship is defined here: it "concerns the conscience alone that seeks in its own works help, consolation, and salvation" (LC 510–511). The two governments are distinguished "for the comforting of men's consciences" (AC 1281–1282). And the deathbed is where the conscience is tested last: "God will call you to account for that soul" of the weak man who "is troubled about it on his deathbed" (v2 14798–14801).

The word is wider in the world's own tongue than in English: the translator records *Gewissen* used once "in the sense of information, intelligence, news" (v3 7180, Steinhaeuser's footnote, OCR-hyphenated in the file — layer). But in every register that matters the conscience is not a compass that guides; it is a court that is terrified and then, by a word from outside, made sure.

**Ecological Function.** G5's word and Doc_05 §10B's *site hub*: "where every lens lands." G1 is referred to it by the confession itself; G3's absolutions exist for it; G8's liberty is preached to it; G6's distinction is drawn for it; the AC's test for a wicked tradition is whether it burdens it (Doc_04 §3 G5). Anchors 9.1 (its comfort), 9.3 (its certainty), 1.2 and 1.3 (its terror as repentance's first part), 5.10 and 5.11 (its instruments), 3.4 (what may not bind it), 7.5 (whom liberty is for). Doc_05 §6C: "every mode above lands in the conscience (G5) and is tested there." (corrected this revision to restore the dropped gravity code, Round 1 finding C2).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* conscience as a moral compass — an inner voice that tells right from wrong, to be followed. *World Hearing:* the place where a person stands accused and is made sure; terrified by the Law, comforted by the promise, burdened wrongly by men's laws; not a guide but a court, and the whole doctrine's home.

**Key Texts.** AC 344–345, 396–398, 546–553, 1281–1282; Ap 698–699, 818–824, 1206–1209; v2 14798–14801, 14960–14961, 15694–15695, 15709–15724, 15902–15904, 15915–15920; LC 510–511; TT 3083–3084 (Bell's English); v3 7180 (Steinhaeuser's footnote — layer).

**Key Sources.** [R37] AC XX, XV, XXVIII; [R38] Ap IV, XII; [R15] the Sermons; [R16] *Doctrines of Men*; [R25] LC; [R31] TT (Contested as verbatim); [R18] *Magnificat* footnote only. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 1.2 repentance; 1.3 contrition; 2.1 faith; 2.2 justification; 2.6 Law and Gospel; 3.4 doctrines of men; 5.10 confession/absolution; 5.11 worthy/examination; 7.1 two governments; 7.5 must and free; 9.1 comfort; 9.3 assurance; 9.4 death; 2.5 grace; 7.3 obedience.

#### 2.9 free will / bondage / "assertion" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [DR]. **AG:** weighted to a single work (*Bondage*) whose OCR requires per-quotation re-check; AC XVIII is the cleaner witness. **Registry:** R12, R27, R34, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** A will that can choose civil righteousness but has "no power, without the Holy Ghost, to work the righteousness of God" — denied against Erasmus in a book that also defends the very posture of *asserting*.

**World Meaning.** The confession grants the will "some liberty to choose civil righteousness" and denies it "power, without the Holy Ghost, to work the righteousness of God" (AC 452–455, per Doc_03). The founder's sustained argument locates the bondage in a war: "there are two kingdoms in the world mutually militating against each other. — That Satan reigns in the one… [I]n the other kingdom Christ reigns" (Co 14747–14759, OCR as found and recovered per Doc_04 §3 G12) — a different "two kingdoms" from 7.1's. And the argument's own manner is part of its content: "Allow us to be assertors, and to study and delight in assertions" (Co 755–756); "The Holy Spirit is not a sceptic" (Co 759–762, OCR-recovered reading per [R34]). The hymn puts the doctrine in a stanza: "Free-will against God's judgment fought, / And dead to good remained" (Hy 1286–1287, Bacon's English).

**Ecological Function.** The ground of 2.4's refusal (the "grand hinge," Co 15139–15142) and of 2.2; the Erasmus debate is the one sustained argument in the library [B]. Not an organizer beyond G1.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "free will" as a philosophical problem about determinism, or as this world denying all human choice. *World Hearing:* a will free for civil things and bound toward God — and a world that will *assert* this rather than hold it as an opinion.

**Key Texts.** AC 452–455; Co 755–762, 14747–14759 (OCR as found). **Key Sources.** [R37] AC XVIII; [R34] *Bondage*, OCR-degraded, "every further quotation needs its own character-level re-check"; [R27] Hymn I. Author Gravity: weighted to one OCR-degraded work — disclosed, not used to demote.

**Related Terms.** 2.2 justification; 2.4 merit; 2.10 sin; 7.1 two governments; 4.7 the devil.

#### 2.10 sin / original sin — "born with sin" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** Melanchthon-only as read (AC II); Luther's own vendored texts use "sin" constantly without a single defining locus found in what was read — a coverage gap, not a finding of silence. **Registry:** R37, R26. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Not a discrete bad act but an inherited condition — "born with sin, that is, without the fear of God, without trust in God, and with concupiscence" — the thing the whole doctrine of faith answers.

**World Meaning.** "Since the fall of Adam all men begotten in the natural way are born with sin, that is, without the fear of God, without trust in God, and with concupiscence; and that this disease, or vice of origin, is truly sin" (AC 192–207, per Doc_03 2.10). It condemns those "who deny that original depravity is sin, and who… argue that man can be justified before God by his own strength and reason." The Apology's justification article assumes it throughout — "the flesh sins even when we do external civil works" (Ap 783–784); "contempt of God, and doubt concerning the Word of God… inhere in human nature" (Ap 795–797) — and in the household the condition has a name and a daily remedy: "the old Adam in us should be drowned by daily sorrow and repentance" (SC 398–400). Sin here is a *state* one is born into, whose two marks are the absence of fear and of trust — exactly the two things the First Commandment asks (4.4, 4.5).

**Ecological Function.** The presupposition of 2.2 and 2.1 (Doc_05 §9A pattern 12); the diagnosis 2.6's Law performs; what 5.2's daily drowning acts on. Coverage: AC II alone as read; Ap II (150–541) unread; the catechisms' own treatment not gathered under this headword (Doc_03 2.10's coverage note stands — §11).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* sin as a discrete wrong act, or as guilt-feeling. *World Hearing:* an inherited disease of origin — no fear, no trust, disordered desire — that only faith, not effort, answers.

**Key Texts.** AC 192–207; Ap 783–803 (read this pass); SC 398–400. **Key Sources.** [R37] AC II; [R38] Ap IV. Author Gravity: Melanchthon-only for the definition as read.

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 2.2 justification; 2.6 Law and Gospel; 2.9 free will; 4.4 to have a god; 5.2 baptism.

### 5.3 The Word, the Scriptures, and the doctrines of men — the interpretation cluster

#### 3.1 the Word / Word of God — "the Word must do it" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices; the best-attested term in the lexicon (Doc_03 3.1), with Doc_04's correction that "every register" means six of the eight coded Luther registers (D and C absent). **Registry:** R11, R13, R15, R20, R25, R26, R29, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** God's own speech, the Gospel of Christ — the one thing necessary; the power that makes a sacrament when joined to water or bread; the mother that bears every Christian; the authority above pope, councils and fathers; and the agent that does the whole work of reform while we sit still: "I did nothing; the Word did it all."

**World Meaning.** "One thing and one only is necessary for Christian life, righteousness and liberty. That one thing is the most holy Word of God, the Gospel of Christ" (v2 11679–11680, per Doc_03). The whole spiritual estate "has been called and instituted only [f]or the ministry of the Word" (v2 11701–11703). The Word is not talk: "God's Word is not like some other silly prattle, as that about Dietrich of Berne, etc., but as St. Paul says, Rom. 1, 16, the power of God" (LC 143–145). It begets: the Church is "a peculiar congregation in the world, which is the mother that begets and bears every Christian through the Word of God" (LC 2800–2802). It makes sacraments: "*Accedat verbum ad elementum et fit sacramentum*… when the Word is joined to the element or natural substance, it becomes a Sacrament" (LC 3861–3864). It is the instrument of the Spirit — "through the Word and Sacraments, as through instruments, the Holy Ghost is given" (AC 247–248) — against any who think the Spirit comes "without the external Word" (AC 253–254). And it is a weapon: "One little word can fell him" (Hy 3695, Bacon's English); "the true holy water and holy sign from which he flees" (LC 137–138).

Its authority stands above every human one. "The Bible has come to lie forgotten in the dust under the bench" (v1 268–269); "it is a wickedly invented fable… that the interpretation of Scripture or the confirmation of its interpretation belongs to the pope alone… the keys were not given to Peter alone, but to the whole community" (v2 2384–2390); "neither St. Peter nor the Church institutes or teaches anything contrary to Christ. And if they did, we must not obey them" (v2 15977–15978); "we must let the Prophets and Apostles sit at the desk, while we sit at their feet" (v1 292–293, per Doc_05 §4.2). Before the Emperor the same rule is said additively — nothing "varies from the Scriptures, or from the Church Catholic, or from the Church of Rome as known from its writers" (AC 631–633, per Doc_03 6.7) — and turned on the bishops: "when they teach or ordain anything against the Gospel, then the congregations have a commandment of God prohibiting obedience" (AC 1342–1344). One rule, two pitches (3.3).

And the Word acts. In the sermons of 1522 it is the reform's only agent. "His word should do the work alone, without our work… We have the *jus verbi*, but not the *executio*; we should preach the Word, but the consequences must be let to God's own good pleasure" (v2 14856–14866; the Latin glossed by the translator's footnotes, per Doc_03); "the Word must do this thing, and not we poor sinners" (v2 14917–14918); "I did nothing; the Word did it all" (v2 14931); "we must let the Word of God alone do the work" (v2 15293–15294). The preacher "can get no farther than to men's ears; their hearts I cannot reach" (v2 14859–14860, per Doc_05 §2.4). The catechism is the Word made small enough to say: "a Short Summary and Epitome of the Entire Holy Scriptures" (LC 46–47, per Doc_04 §3 G2). Everything in this world is done "simply by the Word" (AC 1339–1340) — including, in the confession's account of the ban, its discipline.

**Ecological Function.** G2's own word and Doc_05 §10B's *agent hub*: "the thing that 'must do it' in every lens." G3 (Word joined to element), G4 (the catechism as its epitome), G7 (office as its ministry), G8 (the *jus verbi*/*executio* restraint is G8's warrant), G12 (the weapon) all state their dependence on it (Doc_04 §3 G2). Anchors 3.2 (its content), 3.3 (its authority sense sharpened), 3.4 (its negative), 3.5 (its literal sense), 3.6 (its one Teacher), 4.1 (its epitome), 5.1 (its element), 6.3 (its office), 4.9 (its song), 6.8 (the fence "external"). The G2–G8 competition — "right according to the Scriptures… but what becomes of order?" (v2 14761–14762) — is the ecology's sharpest tension (Doc_04 §5; Doc_05 §10B).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the Word" as the Bible-as-book, a text one reads and interprets for oneself; or as a doctrine of inspiration; or "the Word did it all" as modesty. *World Hearing:* a living, external speech-act of God that begets, makes sacraments, drives the devil off and reforms churches on its own — heard, not merely read; authoritative above every human office; and the reason a preacher may not use force: he has the right to speak, not the power to do.

**Key Texts.** v2 11679–11680, 11701–11703; v2 14856–14866, 14917–14918, 14931, 15293–15294; v2 2384–2390; v2 15977–15978; v1 268–269, 292–294; LC 46–47, 130–138, 143–145, 2800–2802, 3861–3864; AC 246–255, 631–633, 1339–1344; Hy 3695 (Bacon's English).

**Key Sources.** [R13] *Christian Liberty*; [R15] the Sermons (Sermon 2's *jus verbi* passage verified by two passes, Doc_04 §9); [R11] *Christian Nobility*; [R16] *Doctrines of Men*; [R1] the 1539 preface; [R25] LC; [R37] AC V, VII, XXVIII; [R29] Hymn XXVI. Author Gravity: none. Layer: Lambert's footnote glossing *Mein Wort* as "the message contained in the words. Luther does not claim for himself any form of inspiration" (v3 11232, per Doc_03) is editorial.

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 2.6 Law and Gospel; 2.7 promise/testament; 3.2 Gospel; 3.3 Scripture against Fathers; 3.4 doctrines of men; 3.5 letter and spirit; 3.6 Christ alone; 4.1 catechism; 4.7 the devil; 4.9 hymn; 5.1 sacrament; 5.8 congregation; 6.3 office; 6.8 sects; 7.5 must and free; 1.6 the keys; 7.4 insurrection.

#### 3.2 Gospel — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R2, R13, R19, R25, R28, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The good news of Christ — "the true treasure of the Church" — the promise half of Scripture, and the thing this world says "hath again risen" in its own day.

**World Meaning.** "The true treasure of the Church is the Most Holy Gospel of the glory and the grace of God" (v1 1387–1388) — Thesis 62, set against the pope's treasury of pardons. The Gospel is what the second half of Scripture is (2.6): "the Gospel, which is properly the promise of the remission of sins and of justification for Christ's sake" (Ap 861–862). It is what the Church is defined by — "the congregation of saints, in which the Gospel is rightly taught" (AC 277–279) — and what the world believes it has recovered: "the blessed Gospel, which by God's grace hath again risen" (Hy 911–912, Bacon's English); "the blessed light of Christian truth, suppressed for a time by the pope and his followers, has again risen" (v3 10653–10654). Its preaching has two members, "Repent ye and believe the Gospel," and "in the first member He convicts of sins, in the latter He consoles us" (Ap 5180–5183).

**Ecological Function.** G1's content-word and 3.1's specific sense; the promise pole of 2.6; the defining mark of 5.8; the memory of restoration in Doc_05 §6B.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the Gospel" as the four books, or as Christianity in general. *World Hearing:* the promise of forgiveness for Christ's sake specifically — the treasure the indulgence-sellers had buried, and a light this world experienced as risen again.

**Key Texts.** v1 1387–1388; AC 277–279; Ap 861–863; Hy 911–912. **Key Sources.** [R2]; [R37] AC VII; [R28]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 2.6 Law and Gospel; 2.7 promise/testament; 3.1 the Word; 5.8 congregation; 1.1 indulgence.

#### 3.3 Scripture against Fathers, Councils and pope — "the Bible… under the bench" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** Luther-only, cross-register, for the sharp form; Melanchthon attests the milder, additive form (confirmed, Doc_04 §2.3). **Registry:** R1, R11, R16, R23, R31, R34, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The rule that every other writing points to the Scriptures and is judged by them — said sharply by the founder ("under the bench") and additively by the confession ("the Scriptures, or… the Church Catholic").

**World Meaning.** "All other writings should point to the Scriptures, as John pointed to Christ" (v1 283–284, per Doc_05 §6A); "the Bible has come to lie forgotten in the dust under the bench" (v1 268–269); Augustine is praised as "almost the only one of them to subject himself to the Holy Scriptures alone" (v1 320–324, per Doc_03). Against the second wall: "They wish to be the only Masters of the Holy Scriptures" (v2 2352–2357, per Doc_03); "if we are all priests… why should we not also have the power to test and judge what is correct or incorrect in matters of faith?" (v2 2413–2416). Against Emser: "Are not the Scriptures clearer on this point than all the fathers?" (v3 17815–17816, per Doc_03). The confessional voice keeps the rule and changes its pitch — the doctrine has "nothing that varies from the Scriptures, or from the Church Catholic, or from the Church of Rome as known from its writers" (AC 631–633), and the Fathers are cited "lest any one should craftily say that a new interpretation of Paul has been devised by us" (AC 534–537, per Doc_05 §4.2). The English never says "Scripture alone" as a formula; no *sola scriptura* occurs.

**Ecological Function.** G2's authority sense, developed at 3.1; this entry carries the sharp form and the two-pitch difference Doc_05 Discipline 4 requires a Representative to keep (Luther's "under the bench"; the confession's additive rule). Doc_05 §6C mode 4.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "Scripture alone" as a slogan meaning the Fathers and councils are worthless, or as private interpretation. *World Hearing:* the Fathers as witnesses to be judged by Scripture's light and cited as corroboration; a rule stated with two voices — one sharp, one additive — and never as a Latin formula.

**Key Texts.** v1 268–269, 283–294; v2 2384–2390, 2413–2416; AC 534–537, 631–633. **Key Sources.** [R1] the 1539 preface; [R11]; [R37] AC XX–XXI. Author Gravity: Luther-only for the sharp form — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 3.1 the Word; 3.4 doctrines of men; 3.5 letter and spirit; 6.2 we are all priests; 6.7 pope/Antichrist; 1.6 the keys.

#### 3.4 doctrines of men / human traditions (*Menschenlehren*) — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none; Ap XV read entire this pass. **Registry:** R2, R11, R12, R16, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Teaching and commandment "instituted by men" and bound on the conscience — fast-days, orders' rules, compelled confession — rejected on Matthew 15:9; but rites "instituted by men" are wicked *only* when they are made to merit grace or bind the conscience.

**World Meaning.** The treatise's own title carries the German: "THAT DOCTRINES OF MEN ARE TO BE REJECTED… (VON MENSCHENLEHREN ZU MEIDEN)" (v2 15816–15819, the edition's own subtitle). Its rule: "it is wicked to make a necessity and a commandment of that which is free" (v2 15979–15980). The confession draws the line exactly where the conscience is: rites may differ from place to place (AC 282–284, per Doc_03), and "human traditions instituted to propitiate God, to merit grace, and to make satisfaction for sins, are opposed to the Gospel" (AC 400–404). The Apology, defending that article, says the adversaries "appoint another mediator, namely these traditions" (Ap 6639–6640); that "No tradition was instituted by the holy Fathers with the design that it should merit the remission of sins, or righteousness, but they have been instituted for the sake of good order in the Church and for the sake of tranquillity" (Ap 6688–6692); that "traditions are truly snares of consciences" when "exacted as necessary" (Ap 6963–6965); and yet, "we cheerfully maintain the old traditions [as, the three high festivals, the observance of Sunday, and the like]" (Ap 6870–6871) and observe them "without superstition as civil customs" (Ap 6857–6858). Where such doctrines are defended as justifying, "they simply establish the kingdom of Antichrist" (Ap 6724–6725).

**Ecological Function.** The object G2 refuses and G8's "free" thing made "must" (Doc_04 §2.2); Doc_05 §7.1's second boundary ("The Word / doctrines of men"), whose test is conscience-binding (2.8). Anchors 8.6, 8.7, 8.1 (its instances) and 6.7 (its author); presupposes 3.1 and 7.5.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* that this world rejected all tradition and ceremony. *World Hearing:* tradition kept for order and cheerfully — rejected only where it is made a mediator, a merit, or a "must" on the conscience.

**Key Texts.** v2 15816–15819, 15974–15982; AC 400–404; Ap 6613–6643, 6688–6692, 6857–6871, 6962–6965 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R16]; [R37] AC XV; [R38] Ap XV. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 3.1 the Word; 3.3 Scripture against Fathers; 2.8 conscience; 7.5 must and free; 8.6 fasting; 8.1 vows; 8.7 saints; 6.7 pope/Antichrist; 8.8 images.

#### 3.5 the letter and the spirit / the literal sense — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC]. **AG:** Luther-only, single-register (the *Answer to Emser*). **Registry:** R23, R63. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Against a secret "spiritual" sense laid over the plain one, the rule that the literal sense is "the sense accepted by Christ, God the Holy Spirit, and all the angels and saints."

**World Meaning.** Emser had an "external sense and a secret sense, which he calls the literal and the spiritual" (v3 17758–17762, per Doc_03). The answer: the literal sense is "the sense accepted by Christ, God the Holy Spirit, and all the angels and saints," (v3 17784–17789), and "In this way we must interpret all the Scriptures, even the ancient types" (v3 17824–17825). A "clear text" is what a Christian must stand on alone — "You must rest upon a strong and clear text of Scripture if you would stand the test" (v2 14999–15000) — and the devil is a reader too, which is why the text must be clear (Doc_05 §6C mode 3). The translator's phrase, "the foundation principles of Protestant exegesis" (v3 14586–14594, Steimle, per [R23]), is the editor's, 1930.

**Ecological Function.** 3.1's interpretive rule; Doc_05 §6C mode 3; the complement of 2.6 (two *kinds of speech*, not two *senses*).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the letter and the spirit" as legalism versus freedom. *World Hearing:* a rule of reading: the plain sense *is* the spiritual sense; no hidden layer.

**Key Texts.** v3 17755–17834; v2 14998–15002. **Key Sources.** [R23] *Answer to Emser*, "The Letter and the Spirit" only. Author Gravity: single work, single register — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 3.1 the Word; 2.6 Law and Gospel; 3.3 Scripture against Fathers; 3.6 Christ alone.

#### 3.6 Christ alone — "Hear ye Him" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R13, R16, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The one Teacher the Father appointed — "Hear ye Him, Him, Him" — and the one Mediator, so that all Scripture points to Christ alone.

**World Meaning.** Christ "alone has been made our Teacher by the Father… He did not say. Hear ye St. Bernard, St. Gregory, etc., but, Hear ye Him, Him, Him" (v2 16023–16028, per Doc_03); "all the Scriptures point to Christ alone" (v2 15940–15941, per Doc_03). The confession: "the one Christ as the Mediator, Propitiation, High Priest, and Intercessor" (AC 624–626, per Doc_03), and the Apology: "just as it is necessary to maintain this sentence: Christ is Mediator, so is it necessary to defend that faith justifies" (Ap 1054–1056). The tone in which He is heard is a friend's: "Our Lord Christ is a good Friend, and his words are full of love" (Hy 759–761, Walter's report, per [R45]).

**Ecological Function.** 3.1's one voice; 2.2's ground; 8.7's negative (the saints are not mediators); Doc_05 §6C mode 5.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a slogan (*solus Christus*). *World Hearing:* a rule of hearing — one Teacher, one Mediator — and a tone.

**Key Texts.** v2 16023–16028; AC 624–626; Ap 1053–1056. **Key Sources.** [R16]; [R37] AC XXI. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 3.1 the Word; 2.2 justification; 8.7 saints; 3.5 letter and spirit.

### 5.4 Catechesis and the household — the formation cluster

#### 4.1 catechism / "the three parts" / "the five parts" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none for the practice (Ap 6893–6901, Doc_04 §2.3); the household mechanism is 4.2's. **Registry:** R14, R25, R26, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** "Instruction for children, what every Christian must needs know" — the Ten Commandments, the Creed and the Lord's Prayer, which contain everything in the Scriptures, with the two sacraments' words added to make five parts; the book every household is to say word for word, morning, table and night.

**World Meaning.** "Of old it was called in Greek catechism, i.e., instruction for children, what every Christian must needs know" (LC 231–233); the three parts "contain fully and completely everything that is in the Scriptures" (v2 13185–13186) — "These are the three chief things in all the Scriptures" (v2 13214, per Doc_03) — and with the sacraments they are "five parts of the entire Christian doctrine" (LC 372–373), "a Short Summary and Epitome of the Entire Holy Scriptures" (LC 46–47). The three parts are in an order, and the order is the whole teaching: the Commandments show the illness, the Creed the remedy, the Prayer how to ask (v2 13202–13212, per Doc_03; 2.6). "Whoever knows the Ten Commandments perfectly must know all the Scriptures" (LC 194–195), and no one does: "the longer and the more they study the Catechism, the less they know of it, and the more they find yet to learn" (LC 220–221).

It is to be said, not owned. "Every morning, and whenever I have time, I read and say, word for word, the Ten Commandments, the Creed, the Lord's Prayer" — the founder's own account of himself as "a child who is being taught the Catechism" (LC 100–106) who "must remain a child and pupil of the Catechism, and am glad so to remain" (LC 106–107, per Doc_05 §1.1). "He who does not know this could not be numbered with the Christians nor be admitted to any Sacrament, just as a mechanic who does not understand the rules and customs of his trade is expelled" (LC 233–236). The repetition is not mastery but exercise: "the Holy Ghost is present in such reading and repetition and meditation, and bestows ever new and more light and devoutness" (LC 123–125), and speaking, singing or thinking the commandments is "the true holy water and holy sign from which he flees" (LC 137–138). The form is a question: "What does this mean?" (SC 51 and passim).

The book exists because of a failure the founder reports: "many pastors and preachers are very negligent in this, and slight both their office and this teaching" (LC 51–52); "the common people regard the Gospel altogether too lightly" (LC 80–81); "the shameful vice and secret infection of security and satiety" leads many to "throw the book into a corner" (LC 86–89). So "it is not enough to teach and instruct only, but there is need also of daily exhortation" (LC 4262–4265). The confessional voice claims the practice before the Emperor: "With the adversaries there is no catechization of the children whatever… With us the pastors and ministers of the churches are compelled publicly [and privately] to instruct and hear the youth; and this ceremony produces the best fruits. [And the Catechism is not a mere childish thing, as is the bearing of banners and tapers, but a very profitable instruction.]" (Ap 6896–6901, brackets Bente/Dau's) — and communicants are examined on the three parts before the Supper (Ap 6890–6892). Whether any household or parish kept the book is not in the library; what is in the library is the book, to the hour.

**Ecological Function.** G4's own word — "the household catechism as this world's prescribed formation mechanism," Primary on the Documented (program) side only (Doc_04 §3 G4; every "did" in this entry is a "was to"). The whole 1529 output; 3.1's epitome; 2.6's order; 4.2's content; 5.11's gate (examined on the three parts); 4.8's rhythm; 4.9's songs ("some psalms or hymns, which have been composed on these parts," LC 376–378); G13's stated reason for existing (LC 49–52). Doc_05 §2.4 (iii), §6A (the whole of Scripture made repeatable). The G4–G13 tension — the program and its stated failure — is one of the ecology's two structural tensions (Doc_04 §5).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a catechism as a children's textbook, a doctrinal summary to be learned once, or a denominational identity document. *World Hearing:* the whole of Scripture in three sayable parts, to be recited daily by every Christian for life, examined weekly, sung at work — never finished, the gate to the Sacrament, and written because its author believed the people were not holding what they had heard.

**Key Texts.** LC 43–47, 51–52, 80–90, 100–107, 120–125, 130–138, 194–198, 220–236, 241–243, 372–378, 4262–4265; v2 13182–13214; SC 44–53; Ap 6889–6901; TT 2394–2395 (Bell/Morley heading — layer).

**Key Sources.** [R14] the *Kurze Form* (preface and Commandments; its Creed and Prayer, v2 13342–14402, unread); [R25] LC (preface, First Commandment entire, Creed I and III, Prayer entire, Sacrament and Conclusion — Doc_03 §13, Doc_05 §11, this pass); [R26] SC, whole file; [R38] Ap XV; [R31] TT (Contested as verbatim). Author Gravity: none for the practice; the household-father mechanism Luther-only (4.2). **Confidence:** Documented for the program; Inferential/Thin for any household's or parish's practice; Contested for how reception should be read (Doc_02 §8 item 1, R70–R72).

**Reported-Experience Status.** Reported as the world's own self-understanding — not assessed for historical accuracy; confidence calibration applies to the historical-event layer only. *Why it applies:* the catechism program sits under the same G4 divergence as 4.2 — formationally central, prescriptively documented to the hour, while whether any household or parish actually held it is Inferential/Thin (Doc_04 §6.3); this marker was omitted from the draft though the same reasoning that added it to 4.2 applies here (corrected this revision, Round 1 finding C7).

**Related Terms.** 4.2 household; 4.3 "What does this mean?"; 4.4 to have a god; 4.5 fear and love God; 4.8 daily; 4.9 hymn; 2.6 Law and Gospel; 3.1 the Word; 5.11 worthy/examination; 6.5 pastor; 9.5 prayer; 5.1 sacrament; 4.6 neighbor; 4.7 the devil; 7.5 must and free.

#### 4.2 household / "father of a family" / "head of a household" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [AS] [RT] [DR]. **AG:** Luther-only, cross-register, for the mechanism — confirmed (§4: "household" 0/0 in AC and Ap; "father of a family" at Ap 5739, 10011 is a calling, not the catechetical mechanism). **Registry:** R25, R26, R31. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7). The [AS] tag is Inferential/Thin as a comparative claim (OG-3).

**Quick Meaning.** The formation site the catechisms address — a house of father, wife, children, servants and maids, in which the father is to present each part "the simple way," examine everyone weekly, lead the day's three prayers, and withhold food until the parts are said.

**World Meaning.** Every part of the little book is headed the simple way a father should present it to his household — "The Simple Way a Father Should Present Them to His Household" (SC 45; the heading recurs at 166, 227, 357, 494). "It is the duty of every father of a family to question and examine his children and servants at least once a week and to ascertain what they know of it" (LC 241–243); the parts are to be recited "when they arise in the morning when they sit down to their meals, and when they retire at night; and until they repeat them, they should be given neither food nor drink. Likewise every head of a household is obliged to do the same with respect to his domestics, ma-servants and maid-servants" (LC 331–336, typo as found). The house is stratified and served: the Home Chart lists "all holy orders and statuses in life" (SC 611–612), from bishops and pastors to "Servants, Maids, Hired Hands and Workers" and "the Man and Woman of the House" (SC 609–668, per Doc_05); there is a scripted confession for "a servant, maid, etc." (SC 445–446) and one for "a master or a lady of the house" (SC 454). The father's authority is God's own ordering — "that you obey me as your father, and that I have the supremacy" (LC 1072–1075, per Doc_05 §1.2) — and it is answerable: "it is his duty by the injunction and command of God, to teach these things to his children" (LC 4497–4499, per Doc_05 §2.1); at confession he says, "I have not faithfully led my children, servants or wife to God's glory" (SC 455–456).

Why the house: because "through baptism all of us are consecrated to the priesthood" (v2 2176–2177), so the father is a priest in his house (6.2), and because the three parts must be known by "the ordinary Christian, who cannot read the Scriptures" (v2 13182). The house is the parish's gate and its seedbed — the three parts learned there admit one to the Sacrament there, and "we train the people who are to come after us and succeed us in our office and work, in order that they also may bring up their children" (LC 4493–4495, per Doc_05 §4.1). The household's day is set to the hour (4.8), its table is prayed at, its economy is confessed ("charged him too much, cheated him and sold him badly made goods," SC 458–459), its prince is prayed for in its bread (SC 305–306), and its one reported speech from the woman's side is a complaint about its own coldness in prayer — "we are very cold and careless in praying" (TT 3147–3148, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim). Everything above is the household as it is *set down*; whether any household kept it is not in the library.

**Ecological Function.** G4's site and Doc_05 §10B's third, *institutional* hub "on the prescriptive side only": "the place where the Word is recited, the Sacrament prepared for, the conscience examined, the prince prayed for, the devil driven off." G7 in practice (the father as priest, 6.2); G9's site (8.3); G6's felt form (the prince in the daily bread, 7.1); G12's daily defense (4.7). Anchors 4.1, 4.8, 9.5, 5.11, 6.5 (what hearers owe pastors), 8.3, 4.6. Doc_02 §11's inverted asymmetry lives here: the register with the largest ecological role has the smallest evidential footprint.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "household" as the nuclear family, and the father's examination and food-withholding as domestic abuse; or the program as a fact about how sixteenth-century Saxons lived. *World Hearing:* a stratified house of kin and servants under one head answerable to God, prescribed as the place where every Christian is to be formed — the father as priest, the servant as pupil, the mistress as confessor of her own failures — stated as a duty and a program, and unattested as any house's practice.

**Key Texts.** SC 45, 305–306, 445–459, 543–607, 609–668; LC 241–243, 331–339, 1072–1075, 4493–4503; v2 2176–2177, 13182; TT 2394–2395 (Bell/Morley heading), 3094–3095, 3147–3148 (Bell's English).

**Key Sources.** [R26] SC, whole file; [R25] LC preface, Fourth Commandment opening, Conclusion; [R31] TT (Contested as verbatim). Author Gravity: Luther-only, cross-register, for the mechanism — disclosed; the *practice* of catechization is both voices (4.1). **Confidence:** Documented as prescription; Inferential/Thin as any house's fact (Doc_04 §3 G4's divergence — the build's highest-stakes cross-check).

**Reported-Experience Status.** Reported as the world's own self-understanding — not assessed for historical accuracy; confidence calibration applies to the historical-event layer only. *Why it applies:* the household is formationally central — the movement's own chosen site — while whether any household held it is Inferential/Thin (Doc_04 §6.3); the entry speaks the program as the world set it down and keeps the two axes apart.

**Related Terms.** 4.1 catechism; 4.8 daily; 4.6 neighbor; 6.2 we are all priests; 6.1 estate; 6.4 calling; 8.3 marriage; 9.5 prayer; 5.11 worthy/examination; 6.5 pastor; 7.3 obedience; 7.1 two governments; 8.2 chastity.

#### 4.3 "What does this mean?" — the question-and-answer form — Tier 3 (Doc_03: 2 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT]. **AG:** Luther-only, single-register (catechesis). **Registry:** R25, R26.

**Quick Meaning.** The question that follows every commandment, article and petition in the little book — "What does this mean?" (SC 51 and passim) — the form in which a child or "the simple-minded" is expected to hold doctrine; the English formula is Smith's (SC) and Bente/Dau's (LC).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a quiz. *World Hearing:* "As if you were to ask a little child: My dear, what sort of a God have you?" (LC 2605–2609, per Doc_03) — the father's own voice staged. Described inside 4.1.

#### 4.4 "to have a god is to trust" — god, idol, Mammon — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [AS] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** Luther-only, cross-register — confirmed (§4). **Registry:** R25, R26, R31. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only. The [AS] tag is Inferential/Thin as a comparative claim (OG-3, with the seedbed caveat).

**Quick Meaning.** The Large Catechism's definitional move: a god is "that from which we are to expect all good and to which we are to take refuge in all distress," so that to have a god is nothing else than to trust — and "the confidence and faith of the heart alone make both God and an idol."

**World Meaning.** "A god means that from which we are to expect all good and to which we are to take refuge in all distress, so that to have a God is nothing else than to trust and believe Him from the [whole] heart" (LC 408–416, per Doc_03; bracket Bente/Dau's); "the confidence and faith of the heart alone make both God and an idol" (LC 411–412); Mammon is "the most common idol on earth" (LC 433). The exposition, read now to its end, keeps the definition to the last line: "Ask and examine your heart diligently, and you will find whether it cleaves to God alone or not… If on the contrary, it cleaves to anything else, of which it expects more good and help than of God… then you have an idol, another god" (LC 556–564); God "requires nothing higher of us than confidence from the heart for everything good" (LC 670–671); "where the heart is rightly disposed toward God and this commandment is observed, all the others follow" (LC 679–680). Even the German name for God is read as "from the word good" (LC 530–532). The household version is three verbs: "We must fear, love, and trust God more than anything else" (SC 53). Table Talk keeps it as a saying: "As the Faith is, so is also God" (TT 2401, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim).

**Ecological Function.** The First Commandment's whole exposition (LC 400–680, now read entire) and the form 2.1's trust takes from the commandment's side; the head of 4.5; the diagnosis of 2.10 (no trust). Not an organizer across lenses. The Apology's bracketed "Trusting in our own fulfilment of the Law is sheer idolatry" (Ap 1748–1749) is the confessional echo — the logic, not the definition.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "god" as a being whose existence is at issue; "idol" as a statue. *World Hearing:* whatever the heart cleaves to for good — money most commonly — so that the First Commandment is about trust, not metaphysics, and everyone has some god.

**Key Texts.** LC 408–416, 431–433, 447–448, 556–564, 668–680 (521–680 read this pass); SC 53. **Key Sources.** [R25] LC First Commandment, now entire; [R26]. Author Gravity: Luther-only, cross-register — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 4.1 catechism; 4.5 fear and love God; 2.10 sin; 8.7 saints.

#### 4.5 "fear and love God" / "fear, love, and trust" — Tier 3 (Doc_03: 2 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R25, R26, R37.

**Quick Meaning.** The formula that opens every Small Catechism explanation of the Commandments — "We must fear and love God, so that…" (SC 61 and passim) — anchoring each commandment to the First, whose content is trust (4.4); the confession's "Christian perfection is to fear God from the heart, and yet to conceive great faith" (AC 1233–1234) is 8.5's.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a pious phrase. *World Hearing:* the First Commandment restated ten times, so that every commandment is a question of what the heart trusts.

#### 4.6 neighbor — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT] [DR]. **AG:** none for the word (Ap 2312–2318, 3067, 9908–9909; §4); the 1522 measure-of-liberty sense is Luther's. **Registry:** R14, R15, R25, R26, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The person the second table protects — whose property, reputation, "wife, servant, maid, animals" the commandments guard — and, in 1522, the measure of what liberty may do.

**World Meaning.** The Commandments' second table teaches "what he is in duty bound to do and not to do to other men, that is, to his neighbor" (v2 13264–13265, per Doc_03); the household's explanations guard the neighbor's "money or property," "reputation," "wife, servant, maid, animals" (SC 110–145, per Doc_03). In the sermons the neighbor is the rule of pace: "we must not look upon ourselves… but upon our neighbor" (v2 14713–14714, per Doc_03), and a change made "with offence to your neighbor" (v2 14763) is disordered however right. The Apology's love is "love towards one's neighbor" (Ap 9909), and the fruit of the Sacrament "that we should treat our neighbor even as God has treated us" (v2 15559–15560, per Doc_05 §5). And the neighbor tells you where you are: "look about you and see whether you are also in the world, or if you do not know it, ask your neighbors about it" (LC 4448–4449).

**Ecological Function.** The object of 2.3 and 7.5's restraint; Doc_05 §5's ministry is what each "place in life" does for the neighbour; the boundary felt "as neighbours" (Doc_05 §7.2).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* the person next door, or humanity in general. *World Hearing:* the specific other whom the second table names and whom my liberty must not destroy.

**Key Texts.** v2 14713–14714, 14763; SC 93–145; LC 4448–4449. **Key Sources.** [R26]; [R15]. Author Gravity: none for the word.

**Related Terms.** 2.3 good works; 7.5 must and free; 4.1 catechism; 4.2 household; 8.6 fasting.

#### 4.7 the devil — "the old evil foe," "a liar and murderer" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices, every register; the Apology's 61 occurrences counted, unread in bulk (Doc_04 §9). **Registry:** R1, R15, R25, R26, R29, R31, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** A personal adversary this world names in every register — "the master of a thousand arts," "a liar and murderer," "the old evil foe" — against whom the Word, the sacrament and the catechism are daily defense, and whose stratagem the world reads in its own disorders.

**World Meaning.** "The devil is called the master of a thousand arts. But what shall we call God's Word, which drives away and brings to naught this master of a thousand arts" (LC 151–154). He is "Nothing else than what the Scriptures call him, a liar and murderer" (LC 4460–4463), "A murderer, who cannot bear to see you live one single hour" (LC 4463–4464); "the devil never sleeps, and continually molests" (v2 14698); "the devil always opposes this and every Christian exercise" (LC 4266–4267). The 1529 hymn sings him: "The old evil foe, / Means us deadly woe; / Deep guile and great might / Are his dreaded arms in fight" (Hy 3671–3674, Bacon's English; "Der alt' boese Feind," Hy 3712) — and his end: "One little word can fell him" (Hy 3695). The founder has "eaten salt with him once or twice" (v2 15730–15731); the devil knows Scripture and argues it on the deathbed (v2 14772–14776; Doc_05 §6C mode 3).

The world reads its own troubles through him. Of the disorder of 1522: "here we battle not against pope or bishop, but against the devil, and do you imagine he is asleep? He sleeps not, but sees the true light rising, and to keep it from shining into his eyes he would make a flank attack" (v2 14752–14756). Of the household's coldness: "the devil driveth on his servants continually; they are diligent… but we" (TT 3149–3150, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim). Of temptation's three kinds, the third is his (LC 3633–3634). The defense is daily and bodily: the morning prayer "so that the evil enemy will not gain power over me" (SC 555); "undoubtedly, you will not start a stronger incense or other fumigation against the devil than by being engaged upon God's commandments and words, and speaking, singing, or thinking of them. For this is indeed the true holy water and holy sign from which he flees" (LC 134–138); the Sacrament as food "so as not to fall back in such a battle" (LC 4152–4154, per Doc_04 §5). The confession names those "in the power of the devil" (AC 593–594, per Doc_03); the Apology calls the traditions that justify "doctrines of demons" (Ap 6631–6632). Pope, Turk and sects are his instruments in one frame — "the devil, Pope, bishops, tyrants, and heretics" (LC 3433, per Doc_05) — and the Christ/Satan "two kingdoms" of the argument against Erasmus is where the will's bondage lies (Co 14747–14759, per 2.9).

**Ecological Function.** G12's head noun — "the devil, the pope, and the Turk as one adversary; the Word as the weapon" (Doc_04 §3 G12) — and the ecology's Layer-2 name for [F-pap], [F-turk] and [F-rad]. Doc_05 §2.4 (iv): "Formation is arming"; §8: the "embattled" pattern is the constant frame. Anchors 4.1 (the catechism as defense), 9.2 (temptation's third kind), 9.4 (the deathbed), 6.7 and 6.9 (his named instruments), 6.8 (his "apostles"), 4.9 (the hymn), 9.5 (prayer as shelter). Reinforces every gravity but G8, which it reshapes ("the adversary frame gives G8 its urgency," Doc_04 §5).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the devil" as metaphor, psychology, or a figure of speech for evil in general; and the apocalyptic reading of this world's whole frame as its own self-understanding. *World Hearing:* a literal, daily, personal adversary who reads Scripture, attacks at the deathbed, moves colleagues and popes, and flees from a sung commandment — named identically in sermon, catechism, hymn, table and confession; the apocalyptic *reading* of this frame is a modern reconstruction the world's texts do not state (Doc_04 §3 G12).

**Key Texts.** LC 130–138, 151–154, 3633–3634, 4266–4267, 4459–4463; SC 555; v2 14698, 14752–14756, 14772–14776, 15694–15695, 15730–15731; Hy 3671–3705 (Bacon's English), 3712 (German); TT 3149–3150 (Bell's English); AC 593–594; Ap 6631–6632; Co 14747–14759 (OCR as found).

**Key Sources.** [R25] LC; [R26] SC; [R15] the Sermons; [R29] Hymn XXVI; [R31] TT (Contested as verbatim); [R37]; [R38]; [R34] (OCR-degraded). Author Gravity: none. The apocalyptic frame (Oberman, R82) is Dominant Modern Reconstruction at most and unread; nothing here builds on it.

**Related Terms.** 3.1 the Word; 4.1 catechism; 4.9 hymn; 9.2 temptation; 9.4 death; 9.5 prayer; 6.7 pope/Antichrist; 6.8 sects; 6.9 the Turk; 2.9 free will; 7.1 two governments; 7.5 must and free; 1.7 the cross; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 6.6 Christendom.

#### 4.8 daily — "daily bread," "daily sorrow and repentance," daily reading — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT]. **AG:** Luther-only, single-register (catechesis). **Registry:** R25, R26. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The rhythm the catechisms impose on a life: bread asked for daily, the old Adam drowned daily, forgiveness obtained daily, the three parts said at rising, table and bed.

**World Meaning.** "Daily bread" is "Everything that nourishes our body and meets its needs" — "Food, drink, clothing, shoes, house, yard, fields, cattle, money, possessions, a devout spouse, devout children, devout employees, devout and faithful rulers, good government, good weather, peace, health, discipline, honor, good friends, faithful neighbors" (SC 303–308) — a household economy, and a prince, in one petition.
