Baptism means the old Adam is drowned "by daily sorrow and repentance" and "a new person daily come forth" (SC 398–400); in the Church "we shall daily obtain… nothing but the forgiveness of sin" (LC 2887–2889, per Doc_03). The day itself is set: the sign of the cross, creed and Lord's Prayer at rising, "with joy go about your work" (SC 557), the table's folded hands (SC 583–587), the evening's mirror of the morning and "go to sleep immediately with joy" (SC 575); the three parts "when they arise in the morning when they sit down to their meals, and when they retire at night" (LC 331–333). The founder does it himself, "every morning, and whenever I have time" (LC 100–103). The pastor is to "morning, noon, and evening, read a page or two in the Catechism" (LC 73–74, per Doc_05 §1.1).

**Ecological Function.** The form in which the five Primaries were meant to be met — Doc_05 §10B adds "the daily rhythm (morning, table, evening)" to the ecology's central set; the world's answer to the "Seven Canonical Hours" it calls "babbling" (LC 71–72, per Doc_05). Anchors 4.1, 4.2, 9.5, 5.2; the day and the week are shaped by prescription, the year "un-shaped by principle" (Doc_05 §3). Prescribed, not attested (Doc_04 §3 G4).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "daily bread" as food; "daily" as a devotional habit chosen by the pious. *World Hearing:* the whole household economy and its prince in one petition; a day whose three hours belong to the house by rule, and a baptism that happens every morning.

**Key Texts.** SC 303–308, 398–400, 541–607; LC 100–106, 331–334. **Key Sources.** [R26]; [R25]. Author Gravity: Luther-only, single-register — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 4.1 catechism; 4.2 household; 5.2 baptism; 9.5 prayer; 7.1 two governments; 4.9 hymn; 8.3 marriage.

#### 4.9 hymn / "spiritual songs" / German singing — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 2 ↑)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT] **[DR]** (added, §1.5). **AG:** none — both voices, plus the fullest non-founder attestation in the build (Walter [R45], Spangenberg [R47], and by implication Karsthans [R44]); Ap 6893–6895 is a second confessional attestation (Doc_04 §9 item 2). **Registry:** R25, R26, R27, R28, R37, R38, R45, R47. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** Song in the people's own German, written "to make a good beginning" and "added to teach the people" — the way the Word reached those who could not read, the household's work-song, the memory of old tunes with new words, and the one register whose reach beyond the founder's desk the library shows.

**World Meaning.** The purpose is stated in the first preface: "to make a good beginning and to encourage others who can do it better, I have myself, with some others, put together a few hymns, in order to bring into full play the blessed Gospel" (Hy 909–912, Bacon's English); so that "our young people (who both will and ought to be instructed in music and other sciences)" might "rid themselves of amorous and carnal songs" (Hy 919–922); "I would fain see all the arts, and music in particular, used in the service of Him who hath given and created them" (Hy 926–930); "I am not of opinion that all sciences should be beaten down and made to cease by the Gospel, as some fanatics pretend" (Hy 926–928). The confession says what the songs are for: in the Mass "the parts sung in Latin are interspersed here and there with German hymns, which have been added to teach the people" (AC 789–791); "we retain the Latin language on account of those who are learning and understand Latin, and we mingle with it German hymns, in order that the people also may have something to learn" (Ap 8512–8515); "The children sing psalms in order that they may learn… the people also sing [Latin and German psalms], in order that they may either learn or pray" (Ap 6893–6895, bracket Bente/Dau's). In the house: "with joy go about your work and perhaps sing a song inspired by the Ten Commandments or your own thoughts" (SC 557–558); "some psalms or hymns, which have been composed on these parts" (LC 376–378).

The hymn carries memory. At burial "no dirges nor lamentations, but comforting songs" (Hy 983–984); "we have taken the sweet music or melodies which under popish rule are in use at wakes, funerals and masses for the dead… But we have set other words thereto, such as shall adorn our doctrine of the resurrection, not that of purgatory" (Hy 1009–1016); "The notes and melodies are of great price; it were pity to let them perish; but the words to them were unchristian and uncouth, so let these perish" (Hy 1017–1019); "The main point is the correcting of the words, not of the music" (Hy 1051–1052). Music itself is theologized: it "pleaseth God far more, we know, / Than any joys the world can show; / The Devil's work it doth impede" (Hy 1203–1205, from the verse piece *Lady Musick Speaketh*, in Catharine Winkworth's English, so attributed at Hy 1236); "Cheerful and merry must we be in heart and mind, when we would sing" (Hy 1116–1117). And the hymn is a weapon and a self-image: "Ein' feste Burg ist unser Gott" (Hy 3708) — "Strong tower and refuge is our God, / Right goodly shield and weapon" (Hy 3667–3668).

The hymn is also where the world felt its own medium turn on it. By 1543 "the earliest of our hymns are more perverted the more they are printed," (Hy 1067–1069), so that a hymnal must go out "with name attached" (Hy 1084), "lest strange and unsuitable songs come to be sold under our name" (Hy 1086–1087), and "when it is amended without our knowledge, it be fully understood to be not our book published at Wittenberg" (Hy 1093–1094). "False masters now abound, who songs indite; / Beware of them, and learn to judge them right" (Hy 1249–1250, R. Massie's English). The one participant who describes the singing is a musician forty years on: "He kept me three weeks long at Wittenberg… until the first German Mass was sung in the parish church" (Hy 766–768, Walter, per [R45]); the charity-scholars "should sing from door to door Latin Hymns" (Hy 775–776).

**Ecological Function.** G11's carrier — "German for the people: vernacular teaching and singing, Latin retained for the learned" (Doc_04 §3 G11) — and the hymnody Doc_04 folded into G11 and G4 (§2.2, §10 item 12). Doc_05 §3 finds it organizing worship's formation, memory, belonging and authority; §6B makes it a memory institution; §7.1 makes "our book / not our book" a boundary; §2.5 (d) makes the hymnal the community's guard against the print market [F-print]. Anchors 4.1 (songs "composed on these parts"), 4.8 (the work-song), 9.4 (the burial songs), 4.7 (the hymn as weapon), 5.6 (German hymns inside the Mass), 6.8 ("some fanatics" who would beat down the sciences). Doc_02 §3.1: one of the registers with the widest actual congregational reach.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* hymns as ornament or mood in a service; the German hymn as a break with all Latin; "Ein' feste Burg" as a battle-anthem of a founder at Worms (Bacon's own footnote refuses that legend, Hy 856–866, per [R29]). *World Hearing:* teaching set to old tunes so that the unlearned may learn or pray while the learned keep their Latin; a household work-song, a burial comfort, a weapon against the devil, and a book the movement had to sign to keep its own.

**Key Texts.** Hy 909–930, 957–987, 1009–1019, 1047–1052, 1064–1094, 1116–1117, 1203–1206 (Winkworth, attributed at 1236), 1249–1250 (Massie, attributed at 1254), 3667–3668, 3708; AC 789–794; Ap 6893–6895, 8512–8515; SC 557–558; LC 376–378; Hy 766–777 (Walter, per [R45]).

**Key Sources.** [R27] the hymn texts, Bacon's composite English (tunes absent from the file); [R28] the four hymnal prefaces; [R29] Hymn XXVI; [R45] Walter (late, laudatory, three hands — Widely Accepted); [R47] Spangenberg (partisan); [R37] AC XXIV; [R38] Ap XV, XXIV. Author Gravity: none. **Evidential weakness disclosed, per [148], not used to hold the tier:** no hymn text beyond the openings of I, IV, V and XXVI has been read in this build (Doc_05 §13 item 8); every English line is Bacon's rendering; the tunes are not in the file; what any parish sang is Inferential/Thin (Doc_05 §10A).

**Reported-Experience Status.** Reported as the world's own self-understanding — not assessed for historical accuracy; confidence calibration applies to the historical-event layer only. *Why it applies:* the hymn's formative work is central in the world's own account and in the register's reach, while what was sung, where, and to what tune is thinly attested (one late participant; no tune; no parish record).

**Related Terms.** 3.1 the Word; 4.1 catechism; 4.7 the devil; 4.8 daily; 5.6 the mass; 9.4 death; 6.8 sects; 6.9 the Turk; 9.6 martyr; 6.5 pastor.

### 5.5 Word, sign, and faith — the sacramental cluster

#### 5.1 sacrament — "sign, significance, and faith"; "the Word joined to the element" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices; Ap XIII read entire this pass. **Registry:** R3, R9, R12, R25, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** God's promise joined to a visible sign and received by faith — "when the Word is joined to the element… it becomes a Sacrament" — so that there are two (or three, counting absolution), not seven, and none of them works without faith.

**World Meaning.** In 1519 a sacrament has three parts: "the sacrament, or sign, the second is the significance of this sacrament, the third is the faith required by both" (v2 220–228) — "the sign, the significance thereof, and the faith" (v1 2271–2272). In 1520 the count falls: "I must deny that there are seven sacraments, and hold for the present to but three--baptism, penance and the bread" (v2 6695–6701), and by the treatise's end "but two sacraments in the Church of God — baptism and bread" (per Doc_01 §1, verified there). What makes one is stated in Augustine's Latin and then in German: "*Accedat verbum ad elementum et fit sacramentum*… when the Word is joined to the element or natural substance, it becomes a Sacrament" (LC 3861–3864). The fanatics err because they "regard the Sacraments, aside from the Word of God, as something that we do" (LC 4070–4071, per Doc_03). The confession: sacraments are "signs and testimonies of the will of God toward us… instituted to awaken and confirm faith" (AC 367–372, per Doc_03). The Apology gives the rule of counting — "If we call Sacraments rites which have the command of God and to which the promise of grace has been added, it is easy to decide what are properly Sacraments… Therefore Baptism, the Lord's Supper, and Absolution, which is the Sacrament of Repentance, are truly Sacraments" (Ap 6434–6443) — and the image: "a Sacrament is a visible word, because the rite is received by the eyes, and is, as it were, a picture of the Word" (Ap 6452–6454). It would not even refuse the name to prayer, "which most truly can be called a sacrament" (Ap 6512), and it condemns "the whole crowd of scholastic doctors, who teach that the Sacraments confer grace *ex opere operato*, without a good disposition on the part of the one using them" (Ap 6522–6525).

How a sacrament is used is by faith, and only by faith: "faith clings to the water, and believes that it is Baptism" (LC 3921–3922); the mass "is to be approached, not with any work or strength or merit, but with faith alone" (v2 7341–7343); "in the use of the Sacraments faith ought to be added, which should believe these promises, and receive the promised things, there offered in the Sacrament" (Ap 6532–6534); "This use of the Sacrament consoles godly and alarmed minds" (Ap 6551–6552). The sacraments are the outward marks of a Christian — "We Christians have no other outward sign by which we may be distinguished from others than this sacrament and baptism" (v2 15446–15447, per Doc_05 §3) — and their number is a thing of teaching, not of salvation: "no prudent man will strive greatly concerning the number or the term, if only those objects still be retained which have God's command and promises" (Ap 6517–6519).

**Ecological Function.** G3's definitional term — "the sacrament as God's promise joined to an element and received by faith" (Doc_04 §3 G3) — and the second pole of 2.7. G4's five parts include the sacraments' words; G5's "many absolutions" are G3's instruments; G10 is G3's content at the Supper; G7's office is the administration of "the Word of God and the sacraments" (Doc_04 §3 G3). Anchors 5.2, 5.3, 5.10 (the three), 5.4, 5.6, 5.11 (its use), 5.8 (what it signifies), 5.7 (a question about its mode). Doc_05 §3: worship functions "as the Word's delivery and the promise's sign, in that order." The 1522 crisis was fought over this gravity (Doc_04 §3 G3).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "sacrament" as a ritual that works by being performed, or as a mere symbol; and the number seven as given. *World Hearing:* a promise made visible — Word plus element — that does nothing without the faith that takes it; two (or three) in number by God's command; the marks by which a Christian is known.

**Key Texts.** v2 220–228; v1 2271–2272; v2 6695–6701, 7341–7344, 15446–15447; LC 3861–3864, 3921–3922, 4070–4071; AC 367–372; Ap 6415–6455, 6510–6552 (read this pass).

**Key Sources.** [R9] *Blessed Sacrament* (1519); [R3] *Baptism* (1519); [R12] *Babylonian Captivity*; [R25] LC Baptism and Sacrament; [R37] AC XIII; [R38] Ap XIII, read entire. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 2.1 faith; 2.7 promise/testament; 3.1 the Word; 5.2 baptism; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 5.4 for you; 5.6 the mass; 5.7 transubstantiation; 5.8 congregation; 5.10 confession/absolution; 5.11 worthy/examination; 6.8 sects; 4.1 catechism.

#### 5.2 baptism (*Taufe*) — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓; the closest call, §1.4)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R3, R11, R15, R25, R26, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Water "contained within God's command and united with God's Word"; a sign whose meaning — the old Adam drowned daily — "lasts so long as we live"; a covenant of comfort; and the ground on which all Christians are priests.

**World Meaning.** "Baptism [German, *die Taufe*] is called in the Greek language *baptismos*, in Latin *mersio*" (v1 2247–2249; the German bracket is the translator's, the Greek and Latin the founder's own), and "the word *Taufe* comes undoubtedly from the word *tief*" — deep (v1 2256–2257, the founder's own German in the text). Its significance is a death that lasts: "the life of a Christian, from baptism to the grave, is nothing else than the beginning of a blessed death" (v1 2319–2321, per Doc_03); "the old Adam in us should be drowned by daily sorrow and repentance, and die with all sins and evil lusts, and, in turn, a new person daily come forth" (SC 398–400). It is not plain water: "here stand God's commandment and institution, lest we doubt that Baptism is divine" (LC 3789–3791, per Doc_03); "faith clings to the water, and believes that it is Baptism" (LC 3921–3922). It consecrates: "Through baptism all of us are consecrated to the priesthood" (v2 2176–2177) — "whoever comes out of the water of baptism can boast that he is already consecrated priest, bishop and pope" (v2 2214–2216, per Doc_04 §3 G7). It comforts: among the "many absolutions" the sermons list, "a third is our baptism" (v2 15715–15717, per Doc_03). And it is fenced: against "our new spirits, to mock at Baptism, omit from it God's Word" (LC 3840–3842, per Doc_03), and against those who would set a vow "equal to Baptism" — "It is madness to make human tradition, which has neither God's command nor promise, equal to the ordinance of Christ which has both" (Ap 9732–9736).

**Ecological Function.** G3 at the font, G7's ground (6.2), 1.2's daily form, 4.8's rhythm, the AC's fence against the Anabaptists under [F-rad] (Doc_04 §3 G2). A satellite of 5.1 by the reasoning at §1.4, at the richest Tier 2 depth.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a one-time naming rite, or a personal decision. *World Hearing:* a death begun that is repeated every morning, the ground of every Christian's priesthood, a comfort to be reached for on the deathbed, and the thing a vow may not equal.

**Key Texts.** v1 2247–2257, 2319–2321; SC 398–400; LC 3789–3791, 3921–3922, 3840–3842; v2 2176–2177, 2214–2216, 15715–15717; Ap 9732–9736 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R3] *Baptism* (opening only, v1 2243–2422; the rest, 2423–2855, unread); [R25] LC Baptism (opening only, 3760–3925; 3926–4032 unread); [R26]; [R11]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 5.1 sacrament; 1.2 repentance; 4.8 daily; 6.2 we are all priests; 8.1 vows; 6.8 sects; 9.4 death; 5.10 confession/absolution; 1.7 the cross; 2.10 sin.

#### 5.3 the Sacrament of the Altar / Lord's Supper / "the bread" — "in and under" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [AS] (the wording) [TC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices; the controversy that made the presence a boundary is absent from the library except one word (Co 16090–16092, per [R34]). **Registry:** R9, R12, R25, R26, R37, R38; R56 as unread lead. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7); the doctrine of the presence is in active use in more than one living tradition, none of which this entry characterizes. The [AS] tag (for "in and under" / "truly present") is Inferential/Thin as a comparative claim (OG-3).

**Quick Meaning.** "The true body and blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, in and under the bread and wine," given "for you" — Christ's testament received by faith, a food for the daily battle, to which none are admitted unexamined and none compelled.

**World Meaning.** The household learns it as a sentence: "the true body and blood of our Lord Jesus Christ under bread and wine" (SC 496–500, per Doc_03); the larger book says "in and under the bread and wine which we Christians are commanded by the Word of Christ to eat and to drink" (LC 4075–4077); the confession says "the Body and Blood of Christ are truly present, and are distributed to those who eat the Supper of the Lord; and they reject those that teach otherwise" (AC 322–324). The world does not dispute the how: "I rejoice greatly that the simple faith of this sacrament is still to be found at least among the common people; for as they do not understand, neither do they dispute, whether accidents are present or substance but believe with a simple faith that Christ's body and blood are truly contained in whatever is there" (v2 7143–7148); "I will take my reason captive to the obedience of Christ, and clinging simply to His word, firmly believe not only that the body of Christ is in the bread, but that the bread is the body of Christ" (v2 7185–7188). In 1519 it is "*synaxis* or *communio*, that is, fellowship" (v2 263–266, per Doc_03); in 1520 "Christ's testament which He left behind Him at His death" (v2 7292–7294, per Doc_03).

Its benefit is the words: "the words, 'for you' demand a heart that fully believes" (SC 534–535); "put yourself into this YOU, that He may not speak to you in vain" (LC 4376–4377, capitals Bente/Dau's). It is food for a fight: "a food of souls, which nourishes and strengthens the new man" (LC 4145–4146); "a daily pasture and sustenance" (LC 4152); "a pure wholesome, comforting remedy imparting salvation and comfort, which will cure you and give you life both in soul and body" (LC 4389–4390, per Doc_05 §3); "If you could see how many knives, darts, and arrows are every moment aimed at you, you would be glad to come to the Sacrament as often as possible" (LC 4464–4466). Worthiness is the words believed, not the self prepared (5.11): "I come, not upon any worthiness, but upon Thy Word" (LC 4359–4363, per Doc_03).

Its gate and its freedom are stated together. "None are admitted except they be first examined" (AC 797); "no one should by any means be coerced or compelled, lest we institute a new murdering of souls" (LC 4250–4251) — and yet those who "withdraw from, the Sacrament so long a time are not to be considered Christians" (LC 4252–4254); "If you wish such liberty, you may just as well have the liberty to be no Christian" (LC 4295–4296). It is bound "to no place or appointed time" (LC 4287–4288, per Doc_05), and the pope's Easter compulsion is refused (v2 15468–15470, per Doc_05). The sermons of 1522 were preached about it — the cup, the hands, the pace — and the sacrament's public form was to be settled only after the congregation "had obtained the aid of the authorities" (v2 14765). The world's "outward sign" of being Christian is "this sacrament and baptism" (v2 15446–15447).

**Ecological Function.** G3's content at the Supper and the whole of G10 — "the bodily presence: 'truly present,' 'in and under'" (Doc_04 §3 G10), Supporting within G3 on this library's evidence, with the inverse divergence Doc_04 §6.3 flags: the presence's weight as this world's *boundary* against the Reformed (Doc_01 §8.1) is assigned from the census, not shown by the library, and "Supporting" is not "minor" (Doc_04 §10 item 3). The Supper is where the 1522 crisis was fought (Doc_04 §3 G3), where the examination gate stands (5.11), where the third inhabited passage of Doc_05 (§8) walks, and where belonging is decided (Doc_05 §3). Anchors 5.4, 5.5, 5.6, 5.7, 5.11, 5.8 (*communio*), 2.7 (testament), 9.1, 4.7 (food for the battle), 6.8 (those who "teach otherwise").

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a memorial meal, or a metaphysical theory ("consubstantiation" — a word this library does not use), or a boundary-marker whose whole point is who is excluded. *World Hearing:* Christ's body and blood truly there, "in and under," taken on his word without explanation of the how; a testament received, not a work offered; food for a daily battle; a table with an examination at its gate and no compulsion at its door. The controversy that made the presence a boundary with the Reformed is real (Doc_01 §7) and is not in this library; the Representative states the doctrine as the catechism states it and does not narrate the Colloquy (Doc_05 §9A item 3; R56, R57).

**Key Texts.** SC 496–500, 515–535; LC 4075–4077, 4135–4154, 4250–4254, 4287–4296, 4352–4377, 4389–4393, 4459–4466; AC 322–324, 787–799; v2 263–266, 7143–7149, 7185–7188, 7292–7294, 14765, 15446–15447, 15468–15470; Ap 8490–8494.

**Key Sources.** [R25] LC Sacrament of the Altar, entire; [R26] SC; [R12] *Babylonian Captivity*; [R9] *Blessed Sacrament*; [R15] the Sermons (all eight now read, Doc_05 §11); [R37] AC X, XXIV; [R38] Ap XXIV. Author Gravity: none. **Confidence:** Documented for the doctrine; Untagged (Doc_04 §3 G10) for the organizing role against the Reformed, pending [R56]; Widely Accepted as claimed practice; Inferential/Thin as any parish's.

**Related Terms.** 2.7 promise/testament; 5.1 sacrament; 5.4 for you; 5.5 both kinds; 5.6 the mass; 5.7 transubstantiation; 5.8 congregation; 5.11 worthy/examination; 6.8 sects; 7.5 must and free; 4.7 the devil; 9.1 comfort; 5.10 confession/absolution; 5.9 the ban.

#### 5.4 "given for you" — "for you" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT] [DR]. **AG:** none as quotation of the institution (Ap 5159–5162; §4); the "put yourself into this YOU" development is Luther's. **Registry:** R12, R25, R26, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The two words in the institution that the catechisms make the sacrament's whole benefit: "the words, 'for you' demand a heart that fully believes."

**World Meaning.** "'Given for you' and 'Shed for you to forgive sins.' Namely, that the forgiveness of sins, life and salvation are given to us through these words in the sacrament" (SC 515–518); "anyone who believes these words has what they say" (SC 525–526, per Doc_05 §9A). "These words… are not preached to wood and stone, but to me and you… put yourself into this YOU, that He may not speak to you in vain" (LC 4374–4377, capitals Bente/Dau's). In the testament argument the "you" is the heir: "He appoints the heirs when He says: 'For you, and for many'--i. e., for such as accept and believe the promise" (v2 7317–7319). The confessional voice quotes the words as the promise the sacraments seal: "the words of the Lord's Supper clearly testify… This is My body, which is given for you" (Ap 5159–5161).

**Ecological Function.** 5.3's benefit and 2.7's address; Doc_05 §6C mode 6 (reading as being addressed) and §3 (theology learned by hearing one's own address).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a liturgical formula. *World Hearing:* the moment the promise becomes mine — the whole benefit of the sacrament in two words, demanding a heart that believes them.

**Key Texts.** SC 515–535; LC 4135–4143, 4374–4377; v2 7317–7319. **Key Sources.** [R26]; [R25]. Author Gravity: the development is Luther's — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 2.7 promise/testament; 2.1 faith; 5.11 worthy/examination; 5.1 sacrament.

#### 5.5 both kinds / the cup — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R9, R12, R15, R37; R43 (context). **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Bread *and* cup for the laity — in 1520 the sacrament's "first captivity," in 1522 necessary yet "not… made compulsory," in 1530 "the commandment of the Lord."

**World Meaning.** In 1519 it is something "the Church in a general council should again decree" (v2 244–246, per Doc_03); in 1520 "Drink ye all of it" is "not by way of permission but of command" (v2 6787–6791, per Doc_03), and the withholding of the cup is the sacrament's first captivity (Steinhaeuser's sidenote, v2 6713–6714 — layer). In 1522 the cup is "necessary… nevertheless it must not be made compulsory nor a general law" (v2 15377–15380, per Doc_03) — the congregation had "forced… into a law" (v2 15392, per Doc_05) what should have waited on the Word. In 1530 it is "the commandment of the Lord" (AC 678–681, per Doc_03). The other side reaches the library only as the editor's quotation: "In my opinion, he who partakes only of the bread, sins" (v2 14446 — [R43], context).

**Ecological Function.** 5.3's form and the first of 7.5's 1522 "free" things; the only lay-side change of practice the 1522 sermons address by name.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a minor liturgical detail. *World Hearing:* Christ's command to the laity, and the test case for how a right thing must be done — by the Word and not by law.

**Key Texts.** v2 6787–6791; v2 15377–15380; AC 678–681. **Key Sources.** [R12]; [R15]; [R37] AC XXII. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 7.5 must and free; 5.6 the mass; 6.8 sects; 8.8 images.

#### 5.6 the mass — sacrifice, "good work," private mass, "evangelical mass" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓) — [PV]

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT] **[PV]**. **AG:** none; Ap XXIV's sacrifice argument read this pass. **Registry:** R7, R12, R15, R28, R37, R38; R43 (context); R59 as named absence. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The word this world keeps and re-founds: the mass as God's promise of forgiveness, not a sacrifice or a "good work" we offer; private masses abolished, the "evangelical mass for all the people" retained, with German hymns and an examined congregation.

**World Meaning.** "What we call the mass is the promise of remission of sins made to us by God" (v2 7321–7323); its "third captivity" is the opinion "that the mass is a good work and a sacrifice" (v2 7228–7231, per Doc_03), "an *opus operatum* and a sacrifice" (v2 7289–7290); "to have many masses is not to have the mass" (v1 11017–11018, per Doc_03). The 1522 voice holds the same doctrine and a different pace: "the mass is an evil thing… because it is performed as a sacrifice and work of merit. Therefore it must be abolished" (v2 14846–14848) — "the private mass must be abolished, as I have said in my writings. And I heartily wish it would be abolished everywhere and only the evangelical mass for all the people be retained" (v2 14850–14853) — but "the Word must do this thing, and not we poor sinners" (v2 14917–14918), and "I let it live in God's name" (v2 14893, per Doc_05 §3). The 1530 voice: "the Mass is retained among us, and celebrated with the highest reverence. Nearly all the usual ceremonies are also preserved" (AC 787–789); "Private Masses were discontinued" (AC 816–817, per Doc_03). The Apology draws the division the whole argument turns on: "A Sacrament is a ceremony or work in which God presents to us that which the promise annexed to the ceremony offers… A sacrifice, on the contrary, is a ceremony or work which we render God in order to afford Him honor" (Ap 8635–8642); of sacrifices "there has been only one propitiatory sacrifice in the world, namely, the death of Christ" (Ap 8672–8673), and the rest — "the preaching of the Gospel, faith, prayer, thanksgiving, confession, the afflictions of saints" — are "sacrifices of praise" (Ap 8712–8715) that "avail not *ex opere operato*, but on account of faith" (Ap 8735–8736). The funeral preface counts "masses for the soul" among the horrors abolished (Hy 977).

**Ecological Function.** 5.3's public form, settled by G6's "aid of the authorities" (v2 14765) and defended before the Emperor (AC XXIV); the arc Doc_03 5.6 tagged [PV] and Doc_05 §3 treats as real change — "Worship's function did not change across the arc; its *public form* did, under [F-rad] and [F-imp]." Anchors 4.9 (German hymns interspersed), 5.11 (the examined congregation), 1.4 (the one satisfaction). No service order is vendored (R59).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* that this world abolished the mass, or that "mass" is the wrong word for what it kept; or that the 1520 treatise and the 1530 confession must mean two different things. *World Hearing:* the mass kept as a testament and refused as a sacrifice, its private form abolished and its public form retained "with the highest reverence" — one doctrine, three periods, and a documented change of public form the Representative must carry as change.

**[PV], stated.** The 1520 abolition-logic, the 1522 "I let it live," and the 1530 "retained" are three periods inside one voice and between two voices (Doc_03 5.6); not silence, but a demonstrated plurality across periods, directly quoted at the loci above.

**Key Texts.** v2 7228–7231, 7289–7290, 7321–7323; v2 14846–14853, 14893; AC 787–789, 816–817; Ap 8570–8573, 8635–8652, 8672–8673, 8712–8737 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R12]; [R15]; [R37] AC XXIV; [R38] Ap XXIV (8488–8806 read across three passes; 8806–9560 unread). Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 2.7 promise/testament; 1.4 satisfaction; 4.9 hymn; 5.11 worthy/examination; 7.5 must and free; 7.1 two governments; 5.5 both kinds; 5.1 sacrament.

#### 5.7 transubstantiation — "a monstrous word" — Tier 3 (Doc_03: 3)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC]. **AG:** Luther-only, single-register (*Babylonian Captivity*). **Registry:** R12.

**Quick Meaning.** The scholastic account of the presence, refused as "a monstrous word for a monstrous idea" (v2 7102–7105, per Doc_03) while others are permitted to hold it — "only let them not press us to accept their opinions as articles of faith" (v2 7221–7224).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* that this world's presence-doctrine is a rival theory of the same kind. *World Hearing:* a refusal to theorize the how at all — "the simple faith… that Christ's body and blood are truly contained in whatever is there" (v2 7143–7148). (Doc_03's decline of [PV], re-read: agreed — toleration of an outside opinion, not an internal plurality.)

#### 5.8 fellowship / communion / congregation of saints (*communio*, *Gemeinde*) — and "brotherhoods" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices; the German glosses are the founder's own, in the text. **Registry:** R8, R9, R10, R12, R25, R37, R87. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** What the sacrament signifies and what the Creed names — not "church" as building or institution but an assembly, a community, a *Gemeinde*: "the congregation of saints, in which the Gospel is rightly taught and the Sacraments are rightly administered," and the mother that bears every Christian through the Word.

**World Meaning.** The sacrament's significance "is the fellowship of all saints, whence it derives its common name *synaxis* or *communio*" (v2 263–266, per Doc_03) — "a sacrament of love" pictured as a city whose citizens share "the name, honor, freedom, trade" and "all the danger of fire and flood" (v2 291–297, per Doc_03); to excommunicate "means to put out of the community… and that is called in our language 'putting one under the ban'" (v2 274–277, per Doc_03). The Creed's article is glossed in the world's own German inside the text: *ecclesia* "properly means in German eine Versammlung, an assembly"; *Kirche* is "not German by idiom, but Greek"; the article means "eine christliche Gemeinde oder Sammlung… or, best of all and most clearly, holy Christendom (eine heilige Christenheit)" (LC 2822–2858, per Doc_03) — "a little holy group and congregation of pure saints, under one head, even Christ" (LC 2860–2864, per Doc_03). This body is "a peculiar congregation in the world, which is the mother that begets and bears every Christian through the Word of God" (LC 2800–2802), and in it "we shall daily obtain… nothing but the forgiveness of sin" (LC 2887–2889, per Doc_03). The confession defines it by its marks: "The Church is the congregation of saints, in which the Gospel is rightly taught and the Sacraments are rightly administered" (AC 277–279), and locates it where those marks are: "we know that the Church is among those who teach the Word of God aright, and administer the Sacraments aright" (Ap 6592–6594).

The word is also a sanction and a scandal. The "brotherhoods" of the 1519 title are the guild-confraternities counted among sacramental "wares" — "participations, brotherhoods, intercessions, merits, anniversaries" (v2 7235–7236, per Doc_03); "brotherhoods, pilgrimages" (AC 502, per Doc_03). Membership is knowing the three parts and coming to the table (LC 233–234; 4252–4254); belonging is "to partake of the Sacrament together" (AC 795–797, per Doc_05 §3); its negative is the ban (5.9), pronounced "before the whole assembly" (v2 15646, per Doc_05) and "without human force, simply by the Word" (AC 1339–1340); and mutual correction by Matthew 18 is prescribed and, by the founder's own admission, absent — "Of this confession there is no longer even a trace to be found" (v2 15629–15631, per Doc_05 §2.1). The translator notes that the founder "carefully avoids the use of the word 'Church' (*Kirche*)" (v1 14739–14740, Steimle's footnote — layer) and that *Gemeinde* "means sometimes 'community,' sometimes 'congregation,' sometimes even 'the Church'" (v1 14791–14792, Steimle's footnote — layer).

**Ecological Function.** The name of the body G2 begets and G3 signifies (Doc_04 §2.2, its second-closest fold); Doc_05 §3's belonging ("Membership is knowing the three parts and being admitted") and §7.1's inner boundaries (in the Sacrament / not; the ban; "our book"). Anchors 6.6 (its inclusive extent), 5.9 (its exclusion), 5.11 (its gate), 6.5 and 6.3 (its offices), 6.7 (what it is not reducible to — "one man," v2 2407–2411, per Doc_03), 3.1 (its mother). The one place the world's texts define the Church by marks rather than by hierarchy.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "church" as a building, a denomination, or an institution with officers; "communion" as a private act. *World Hearing:* an assembly of people under Christ, known by Word and sacrament, entered by knowing three parts, kept by coming to the table together, sanctioned by the Word alone — a *Gemeinde*, in the founder's own preferred German, with "Church" avoided.

**Key Texts.** v2 263–297; LC 2800–2802, 2822–2864, 2887–2889; AC 277–279, 795–797, 1339–1340; Ap 6592–6594 (read this pass); v2 7235–7236; v2 15629–15631, 15646; v1 14739–14745, 14789–14793 (Steimle — layer).

**Key Sources.** [R9] *Blessed Sacrament*; [R10] *The Ban*; [R25] LC Creed, Third Article; [R37] AC VII; [R38] Ap XIV; [R8] *Papacy at Rome* footnotes; [R87] the translators' glossing footnotes. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 5.1 sacrament; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 5.9 the ban; 5.11 worthy/examination; 6.6 Christendom; 6.5 pastor; 6.3 office; 6.7 pope/Antichrist; 3.1 the Word; 3.2 Gospel; 8.7 saints.

#### 5.9 the ban / excommunication — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [DR]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R10, R15, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Exclusion from the outward fellowship of the sacrament only — never from the inner fellowship "which no ban can touch" — used for correction, stopping short of death, by the Word alone.

**World Meaning.** The ban is "exclusion from this fellowship" (v2 1156–1157) — the visible one; the inner, spiritual fellowship is one that no ban can touch (v2 1165–1169). There is "the lesser ban" and "the greater ban" (v2 1187–1190, per Doc_03), and the spiritual estate's "sword is not to be of iron, but the sword of the Spirit" (v2 1195–1199); the ban is for "the correction of our neighbor," and "the penalty should stop short of his death" (v2 1222–1226). In 1522 the pastor is to hear "Dear pastor, this man has done this and that" and "exclude him and put him under the ban before the whole assembly" (v2 15639–15646, per Doc_05 §1.1) "for the sake of the congregation, until he comes to himself and is received back again" (v2 15646–15648, per Doc_05 §5). The confession condemns "ruthless excommunications" (AC 1277–1278, per Doc_03) and keeps the power "without human force, simply by the Word" (AC 1338–1340).

**Ecological Function.** 5.8's negative and Doc_05 §3's belonging; G2's rule (by the Word) applied to discipline; the sword's counter-term (7.2).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* excommunication as damnation, or as a political weapon. *World Hearing:* a fence around the visible table only, for correction, without force — and an ordinance the founder himself said was no longer practiced (v2 15629–15631).

**Key Texts.** v2 1154–1226; v2 15639–15648; AC 1338–1340. **Key Sources.** [R10] *The Ban* (opening only); [R15]; [R37] AC XXVIII. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 5.8 congregation; 7.2 the sword; 6.5 pastor; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar.

#### 5.10 confession / absolution — "many absolutions" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R4, R15, R26, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Two parts — admitting one's sin, and receiving forgiveness "from the confessor, as if from God Himself" — kept as a treasure ("I will let no man take private confession away from me"), never compelled, never a catalogue of every sin.

**World Meaning.** "Confession has two parts: First, a person admits his sin Second, a person receives absolution or forgiveness from the confessor, as if from God Himself, without doubting it" (SC 414–417); the confessor asks, "Do you also believe that the forgiveness I give is God's forgiveness?" and the answer is "Yes, dear sir" (SC 480–483, per Doc_05 §2.1). One should "not look for other sins or invent them, because that would turn confession into torture" (SC 464–466). The founder refuses compulsion — "I refuse to go to confession just because the pope wishes it" (v2 15675–15676, per Doc_05) — and refuses to give it up: "I will let no man take private confession away from me, and I would not give it up for all the treasures in the world, since I know what comfort and strength it has given me… the devil would have slain me long ago, if the confession had not sustained me" (v2 15678–15683). There are three confessions — before the congregation, to God, to a brother (v2 15619–15690, per Doc_03) — and "we must have many absolutions, so that we may strengthen our timid consciences and despairing hearts" (v2 15694–15695). Confession is "an occasion by which God is called to the fulfilment of His own promise" (v1 3195–3197, per Doc_03). The confession before the Emperor: "Private Absolution ought to be retained" (AC 331–334, per Doc_03), enumeration of sins "not necessary," and absolution prized "as being the voice of God" (AC 906–912, per Doc_03). The Apology names absolution "that blessed word of comfort" and "a sacrament of repentance" (Ap 5152–5154), and puts the whole matter in one question: "if the power of the keys does not console us before God, what, then, will pacify the conscience?" (Ap 4955–4956).

**Ecological Function.** Where G3 and G5 meet (Doc_04 §2.2); Doc_05 §2.1's confessor → penitent relationship and its brother → brother form; the practice the movement kept when it abolished the Easter compulsion (Doc_05 §2.5 c). Anchors 1.6, 1.2, 5.11 ("examined and absolved," Ap 8494). Not a generator — Tier 2.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* that this world abolished confession, or that "absolution" is a Catholic word. *World Hearing:* private confession kept as a comfort, freed from compulsion and enumeration, with absolution heard "as if from God Himself" — the most-loved of the "many absolutions."

**Key Texts.** SC 410–491; v2 15619–15724; AC 331–334, 906–912; Ap 4955–4956, 5141–5154 (read this pass); v1 3180–3197. **Key Sources.** [R26]; [R15] Sermon 8; [R4] *Confession* (opening only, v1 3165–3324); [R37] AC XI, XXV; [R38] Ap XII. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 1.2 repentance; 1.6 the keys; 2.8 conscience; 5.1 sacrament; 5.2 baptism; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 5.11 worthy/examination; 9.1 comfort; 6.5 pastor; 2.7 promise/testament.

#### 5.11 worthy / unworthy — examination before the sacrament — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R25, R26, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Worthiness redefined as believing the words "given for you," so that the unworthy are "those alone… who neither feel their infirmities nor wish to be considered sinners"; and the practice that "none are admitted except they be first examined."

**World Meaning.** "Anyone who believes these words, 'Given for you,'… is really worthy and well prepared" (SC 528–535, per Doc_03); "I come, not upon any worthiness, but upon Thy Word, because Thou hast commanded it" (LC 4359–4362); "those alone are called unworthy who neither feel their infirmities nor wish to be considered sinners" (LC 4419–4421). The old scruple is named and refused: "the old way under the Pope, in which a person tortured himself to be so perfectly pure that God could not find the least blemish in us… every one was instantly thrown into consternation and said to himself: Alas! you are unworthy!… one week trails another, and one half year the other" (LC 4327–4336, per Doc_05 §1.4). And the gate is kept: "none are admitted except they be first examined" (AC 797); "examined [whether they know and understand anything of the Lord's Prayer, the Creed, and the Ten Commandments], and absolved" (Ap 6890–6892, bracket Bente/Dau's) — with the other "examine," the father's weekly one (LC 241–243), a different sense of the same English word. Those who stay away too long "are not to be considered Christians" (LC 4252–4254), and yet "no one should by any means be coerced or compelled" (LC 4250–4251).

**Ecological Function.** The parish's gate and the household's test joined (Doc_05 §3: "examination before the Supper, which makes the household's weekly test the parish's gate"); G5's answer to the "Alas! you are unworthy!" scruple (LC 4330–4331); G13's stated occasion (LC 4240–4248, the founder's testimony, Discipline 5). Anchors 5.3, 5.4, 4.1, 5.10.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* worthiness as moral fitness, examination as a test of purity. *World Hearing:* worthiness is believing the words; examination is of the three parts; the unworthy are the untroubled.

**Key Texts.** SC 528–535; LC 4327–4338, 4352–4363, 4414–4421; AC 797; Ap 6889–6892. **Key Sources.** [R25] LC Sacrament, exhortation; [R37] AC XXIV; [R38] Ap XV. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 5.4 for you; 4.1 catechism; 5.10 confession/absolution; 2.8 conscience; 2.1 faith; 5.8 congregation; 4.2 household; 5.1 sacrament; 5.6 the mass; 8.6 fasting.

### 5.6 Priest, office, estate, calling — the priesthood-of-all-believers cluster

#### 6.1 spiritual estate / temporal estate — "the Christian Estate" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [AS] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** Luther-only, cross-register, for the spiritual/temporal pairing — confirmed (§4: Melanchthon's words are "states," "estates of life," "orders"). **Registry:** R10, R11, R14, R19, R24, R25, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only. The [AS] tag is Inferential/Thin as a comparative claim (OG-3, seedbed caveat).

**Quick Meaning.** The division of Christians into a "spiritual" estate (pope, bishops, priests, monks) and a "temporal" one — called "pure invention," then kept as a word for every holy station: marriage "the most common and noblest estate," fatherhood and motherhood an estate too.

**World Meaning.** "It is pure invention that pope, bishops, priests and monks are to be called the 'spiritual estate'… all Christians are truly of the 'spiritual estate,' and there is among them no difference at all but that of office" (v2 2159–2164); "there is really no difference between laymen and priests, princes and bishops, 'spirituals' and 'temporals,' as they call them, except that of office and work, but not of 'estate'" (v2 2233–2236). The temporal power "has become a member of the body of Christendom, and is of the 'spiritual estate,' though its work is of a temporal nature" (v2 2298–2300, per Doc_03); the rulers "are priests and bishops, and count their office one which has a proper and a useful place in the Christian community" (v2 2211–2214). Then the word is reused for the stations that remain: marriage is "the most common and noblest estate, which pervades all Christendom" (LC 1714–1716); "this estate of fatherhood and motherhood" (LC 1051–1052, per Doc_03); "both ecclesiastical and civil estates must humble themselves and all be found in this estate" (LC 1713–1716, per Doc_03). The Teutonic Order is the case where the two estates were confused in one body: "must therefore wield the worldly sword and be a secular order at the same time that it is to be a spiritual order" (v3 20897–20899). The Apology's "states of perfection, i.e., holier and higher states than the rest, such as marriage, rulership" (Ap 9765–9767, bracket Bente/Dau's) is the refused claim in the confessional voice's own word. The translator's footnote shows *geistlich* in its church-property sense (v1 14770 — layer; not the estate sense).

**Ecological Function.** G7's refused pairing and its kept vocabulary (Doc_04 §3 G7); Doc_05 §4.4: rank is "a place in life one confesses from." Anchors 8.3 (the married estate), 8.4 ("spiritual" in scare-quotes), 6.3 (the difference that remains). Developed under 6.2.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "estate" as property; "spiritual" as inward or pious. *World Hearing:* a *station* — and the refusal that clergy are a higher one, with the word kept for marriage, parenthood, rule and trade as holy stations of equal standing.

**Key Texts.** v2 2159–2164, 2211–2214, 2233–2238, 2298–2300; LC 1051–1052, 1713–1716; v3 20897–20900; Ap 9765–9767. **Key Sources.** [R11]; [R25]; [R24]; [R38]. Author Gravity: Luther-only for the pairing — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 6.2 we are all priests; 6.3 office; 6.4 calling; 8.3 marriage; 8.4 spiritual/Geysterey; 7.1 two governments; 4.2 household.

#### 6.2 "we are all priests" — the royal priesthood — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none, weighted to Luther (§4: Ap 6463–6477 and 8719–8724 attest the universal priesthood in the Apology's sacrifice argument; the formula "we are all priests" is Luther's). **Registry:** R11, R12, R30, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7); "the priesthood of all believers" is a later phrasing this world's own texts do not carry (Doc_03 §10.3).

**Quick Meaning.** Through baptism every Christian is consecrated a priest — so that there is "no difference at all but that of office"; the father is a priest in his house, the prince a priest in his; but no one may exercise the public office unless called.

**World Meaning.** "Through baptism all of us are consecrated to the priesthood" (v2 2176–2177); "whoever comes out of the water of baptism can boast that he is already consecrated priest, bishop and pope, though it is not seemly that every one should exercise the office" (v2 2214–2216). "If we are all priests, as was said above, and all have one faith, one Gospel, one sacrament, why should we not also have the power to test and judge what is correct or incorrect in matters of faith?" (v2 2413–2416). What remains among priests is office, not rank: "a priest in Christendom is nothing else than an office-holder. While he is in office, he has precedence; … when deposed, he is a peasant or a townsman like the rest" (v2 2223–2226, per Doc_03). "Christ hath not ordained principalities or powers or lordships, but ministries, in the Church" (v2 8908–8909). The martyrs' ballad sings it as a climax: "Their monkish garb from them they take… True priests of God's own making" (Hy 1789–1800, Bacon's English).

The priesthood is exercised where one stands. The father is a priest in his house: every part of the little book is his to present (SC 45), and "it is his duty by the injunction and command of God" (LC 4497–4498). The temporal rulers "are priests and bishops" (v2 2211–2214). But the public office has a fence, and the world builds it in its own voice: "no one must put himself forward… without our consent and election" (v2 2216–2219, per Doc_04 §5); "I was regularly called by the Council to preach in this place" (v2 14778–14779); "no one should publicly teach in the Church or administer the Sacraments unless he be regularly called" (AC 383–384). The Apology reads the New Testament priesthood as the whole reconciled people offering "spiritual sacrifices" — "An holy priesthood, to offer up spiritual sacrifices" (Ap 8720–8721) — and the ministers as "called to teach the Gospel and administer the Sacraments to the people" and not "to make any sacrifices for the people" (Ap 6473–6477). The phrase "The Priesthood of Believers" is the editor's sidenote (v2 2171, Jacobs — layer); "the priesthood of all believers" is Neve's introduction (v1 10681 — layer); the founder's own words are "we are all priests."

**Ecological Function.** G7's head — "Estate, office, and calling — 'we are all priests'" (Doc_04 §3 G7) — and the claim from which 6.1 (no estate difference), 6.3 (the office as the only difference) and 6.4 (the call as the fence) each draw. Doc_05 §2.1: the father "is a priest in his house because 'through baptism all of us are consecrated to the priesthood'"; §4.3: "the household father is the ecology's first officer"; §4.4: "the office is not the man." Anchors 4.2, 6.5, 8.3 (the married pastor), 3.3 (all may test and judge), 5.2 (its ground), 9.6 (the martyrs made priests). Fenced under [F-rad] by "regularly called" (Doc_04 §3 G7's forces notation).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the priesthood of all believers" as a slogan for religious individualism, lay preaching without call, or the abolition of ordained ministry; or, from the other side, as a merely spiritual dignity with no consequence. *World Hearing:* a consecration given in baptism to every Christian, exercised in one's own house and station, with the *public* office reserved to those regularly called — one priesthood, many offices, no higher estate.

**Key Texts.** v2 2159–2164, 2176–2180, 2191–2199, 2211–2231, 2413–2416; v2 8908–8909; v2 14778–14779; AC 383–384; Ap 6463–6477, 8712–8724 (read this pass); Hy 1789–1802 (Bacon's English); SC 45; LC 4497–4499.

**Key Sources.** [R11] *Christian Nobility* (the "Three Walls" opening read; the rest, v2 2417–5051, unread); [R12]; [R15]; [R37] AC XIV; [R38] Ap XIII, XXIV; [R30] Hymn V. Author Gravity: weighted to Luther for the formula — disclosed; the Apology attests the substance.

**Related Terms.** 6.1 estate; 6.3 office; 6.4 calling; 6.5 pastor; 5.2 baptism; 4.2 household; 3.3 Scripture against Fathers; 1.6 the keys; 9.6 martyr; 7.1 two governments; 8.3 marriage.

#### 6.3 office / office-holder — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R11, R12, R25, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The only difference among Christians: a priest is "nothing else than an office-holder," charged with Word and sacrament, who "when deposed… is a peasant or a townsman like the rest"; and every trade has its office too.

**World Meaning.** "There is among them no difference at all but that of office" (v2 2163–2164). "A priest in Christendom is nothing else than an office-holder. While he is in office, he has precedence; … when deposed, he is a peasant or a townsman like the rest" (v2 2223–2226, per Doc_03) — against the *characteres indelebiles* (v2 2223–2231, per Doc_03). The clergy are "charged with the administration of the Word of God and the sacraments, which is their work and office" (v2 2246–2249, per Doc_03), and "A cobbler, a smith, a farmer, each has the work and office of his trade" (v2 2252–2257, per Doc_03). The confession keeps the word for the bishop's two powers — the sword is "another office than the ministry of the Gospel" (AC 1329–1330, per Doc_03) — and the catechism rebukes pastors who "slight both their office and this teaching" (LC 51–52). Even God's Spirit has "His office" (LC 2763, per Doc_03). Doc_03 §10.1's note holds: "calling" is the confessional word for what Luther's texts call "office" and "estate."

**Ecological Function.** 6.2's conclusion and G7's criterion; Doc_05 §4.4 ("the office is not the man"); 6.5's definition; 7.2's distinction (the sword as another office). Developed under 6.2.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "office" as a position of rank or a room. *World Hearing:* a function held for a time, by which alone Christians differ — the pastor's, the cobbler's, the prince's — never an indelible character.

**Key Texts.** v2 2163–2164, 2223–2257; AC 1329–1330; LC 51–52. **Key Sources.** [R11]; [R37] AC XXVIII; [R25]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 6.2 we are all priests; 6.1 estate; 6.4 calling; 6.5 pastor; 7.2 the sword; 3.1 the Word; 2.3 good works; 5.8 congregation.

#### 6.4 calling / "regularly called" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓; the closest call in this cluster, §1.4)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none by attestation, weighted to Melanchthon's confessional register (Doc_03 §10.1's double classification). **Registry:** R15, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Every station of life — father, mother, prince, preacher — as a work commanded by God and pleasing to Him, against the monks who "put it far above all other kinds of life"; and, of the ministry, the rule that no one teaches publicly "unless he be regularly called."

**World Meaning.** The confession claims its teachers "have taught to good purpose concerning all estates and duties of life, as to what estates of life and what works in every calling be pleasing to God" (AC 497–500, per Doc_05 §5) — "that the father brought up his offspring, that the mother bore children, that the prince governed the commonwealth" (AC 978–981, per Doc_03) — against the monks who "put it far above all other kinds of life ordained of God." "Christian perfection is to fear God from the heart, and yet to conceive great faith… and meanwhile, to be diligent in outward good works, and to serve our calling" (AC 1233–1239, per Doc_03). The Apology's closing word on perfection: "callings are unlike [one is called to rulership, a second to be father of a family, a third to be a preacher]… Callings are personal… Thus perfection with us is that every one with true faith should obey his own calling. [Not that I should undertake a strange calling for which I have not the commission or command of God.]" (Ap 10009–10022, brackets Bente/Dau's); "all men, in every vocation, ought to seek perfection, that is, to grow in the fear of God in faith, in love towards one's neighbor" (Ap 9907–9909). Of the ministry: "no one should publicly teach in the Church or administer the Sacraments unless he be regularly called" (AC 383–384), which the founder says of himself — "I was regularly called by the Council to preach in this place" (v2 14778–14779) — and treats as his "weapon to brandish in the devil's face" (v2 14776–14779, per Doc_05 §2). Table Talk: "Matrimony proceedeth freely in every state and calling" (TT 2432, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim).

**Ecological Function.** The confessional wording of 6.2/6.3 and G7's fence under [F-rad] (Doc_04 §3 G7); Doc_05 §5's whole Ministry lens ("Ministry is redefined as vocation") and §4.3's criterion of legitimacy. Anchors 8.5 (perfection as obeying one's calling), 2.3 (works in one's calling), 6.5 (the called pastor).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "calling" as a career one chooses or feels drawn to; "vocation" as a religious profession. *World Hearing:* a station commanded — one is *called to* rulership, fatherhood, preaching — personal, not chosen, and holier in no case than another; and, for the pulpit, a *regular* call from the community, the fence against unlicensed preaching.

**Key Texts.** AC 383–384, 497–500, 978–981, 1119–1121, 1233–1239; Ap 9905–9910, 10008–10022 (read this pass); v2 14776–14779; TT 2432 (Bell's English). **Key Sources.** [R37] AC XIV, XX, XXVI, XXVII; [R38] Ap XXVII, read entire; [R15]; [R31]. Author Gravity: weighted to Melanchthon — disclosed; Luther's own texts say "office" and "estate" (aliases: 6.1, 6.3).

**Related Terms.** 6.2 we are all priests; 6.3 office; 6.1 estate; 6.5 pastor; 8.5 perfection; 2.3 good works; 8.3 marriage; 7.1 two governments; 4.2 household; 6.8 sects; 8.4 spiritual/Geysterey.

#### 6.5 pastor / preacher / "care-takers of souls" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT] [DR]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R15, R25, R26, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The office-holder the catechism addresses and rebukes — to read, catechize, examine, absolve, exclude, and be honoured and fed — married, called, and, in the founder's report, often negligent.

**World Meaning.** The Large Catechism is addressed to "All Pastors and Preachers, that They Should Daily Exercise Themselves in the Catechism" (LC 43–47), and rebukes them: "very negligent… some from great and high art… but others from sheer laziness and care for their paunches" (LC 51–54, per Doc_05); "shameful gluttons and servants of their own bellies who ought to be more properly swineherds and dog-tenders than care-takers of souls and pastors" (LC 66–69, per Doc_03). Freed "from the unprofitable and burdensome babbling of the Seven Canonical Hours," they are to "morning, noon, and evening, read a page or two in the Catechism… and pray the Lord's Prayer for themselves and their parishioners" (LC 71–76, per Doc_05). They are to instruct and hear the youth (Ap 6897–6899), examine and absolve before the Supper (Ap 8493–8494), and exclude "before the whole assembly" (v2 15646). They are owed honour and living — "What Hearers owe their Pastors" (SC 619); "Shepherds of Souls are worthy of double honour" (TT 2424, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim) — and they starve where nobles "let the parishes decay… and pastors and preachers to suffer distress and hunger a plenty" (LC 95–97). They are married: "our priests were desirous to avoid these open scandals, they married wives" (AC 713–715), and "it is to be expected that the churches shall at some time lack pastors if marriage is any longer forbidden" (AC 754–756). "The ministry is the highest office in the Church" (Ap 6908–6909, bracket Bente/Dau's).

**Ecological Function.** 6.3's office in the parish and 6.4's called man; Doc_05 §1.1 ("Addressed, rebuked and prescribed for, never heard") and §10A (the under-weighted dimension: "the office that ran the parishes has no voice of its own in the library"); G13's first named culprit (LC 51–52) — as the founder's testimony, not as fact about any pastor (Discipline 5).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* the pastor as a professional clergyman with a settled role. *World Hearing:* a called office-holder, married, poor where nobles are stingy, told daily to read a page and rebuked in print for not doing so — the one office in this world that never speaks in its own voice in the library.

**Key Texts.** LC 43–76, 92–97; SC 615–623; v2 15639–15648; AC 713–715, 754–756; Ap 6897–6899, 6908–6909; TT 2424 (Bell's English). **Key Sources.** [R25] LC preface; [R26] Home Chart; [R37] AC XXIII; [R38] Ap XV. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 6.3 office; 6.4 calling; 6.2 we are all priests; 4.1 catechism; 5.9 the ban; 5.10 confession/absolution; 8.3 marriage; 4.2 household; 4.9 hymn; 5.8 congregation; 7.3 obedience.

#### 6.6 Christendom (*Christenheit*) — the one holy Christian Church — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none, weighted to Luther (Ap 5626 read this pass — one confessional use, in the bracketed German text; §4). **Registry:** R6, R8, R11, R25, R38, R87. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The founder's preferred word for the whole body of Christians — wider than Rome's confirmation, including "Muscovites, Russians, Greeks, Bohemians" — the Creed's "one holy Christian Church," which cannot be reduced to one man.

**World Meaning.** Christendom includes those who "merely do not have their priests and bishops confirmed by Rome" (v1 12792–12793; Revision 0 condensed this quotation without ellipsis — Round 1 finding C1) — "Muscovites, Russians, Greeks, Bohemians" (v1 12786–12796, per Doc_05 §7.1) — who "agree with us in holding to the same baptism, Sacrament, Gospel, and all the articles of faith" and are "not heretics and apostates, but perhaps better Christians than we are" (v1 12817–12819, per Doc_03). It cannot be reduced "to one man" (v2 2407–2411, per Doc_03), and the temporal power is "a member of the body of Christendom" (v2 2298–2300). The Creed's article, in the world's own German inside the text, is best rendered "holy Christendom (eine heilige Christenheit)" (LC 2840–2843). The confessional voice says "one holy Church" (AC 277) and "the Church Catholic" (AC 632) and, once, "this cause, which is not only ours, but that of all Christendom" (Ap 5626–5627, bracket Bente/Dau's). The translator records the founder's avoidance of *Kirche* (v1 14739–14745 — layer).

**Ecological Function.** 5.8's inclusive extent and Doc_05 §7.1's first boundary ("Christendom / the papacy") with its inner edge; G12's "embattled Christendom" is this body under siege (4.7).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "Christendom" as a political order or a civilization. *World Hearing:* the whole assembly of Christians, wider than any pope's confirmation, named in preference to "Church."

**Key Texts.** v1 12786–12819; v2 2298–2300, 2407–2411; LC 2840–2843; Ap 5625–5627. **Key Sources.** [R8] *Papacy at Rome*; [R11]; [R25]; [R38]. Author Gravity: weighted to Luther — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 5.8 congregation; 6.7 pope/Antichrist; 4.7 the devil; 7.1 two governments; 6.9 the Turk.

#### 6.7 pope / papacy / papist / Romanist / "Antichrist" — and "the Church of Rome" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓) — [PV]

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT] **[PV]**. **AG:** none — every register, both voices. **Registry:** R1, R8, R11, R12, R25, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only; this entry characterizes no present-day body.

**Quick Meaning.** The adversary named at rising pitch — "the Romanists" behind three walls, "the papacy is the kingdom of Babylon," the pope as "Antichrist," "under the Papacy," "in Popedom" — and, in the confession before the Emperor, "the Church of Rome as known from its writers": one adversary, two registers of naming.

**World Meaning.** The polemical series names him louder each year: "the Romanists" and their "three walls" (v2 2121–2124, per Doc_03); "THE PAPACY IS THE MIGHTY HUNTING OF THE ROMAN BISHOP" (v2 6512–6513); "a game of Anti-christ or a sign that he is close at hand" (v2 2347); "the kingdom of his Vicar, the Antichrist in Rome… is sore beset" (v1 408–409, per Doc_04 §3 G12). The question the Leipzig debate posed is whether papal power "is of divine or of human origin" (v1 12786–12788 with fn. [8], per Doc_03). In the sermons "the pope… the Antichrist" (v2 15024, per Doc_05 §4.2) is the one whose "fools' laws" made fasts and Easter a compulsion (v2 15240, 15468, per Doc_05). In the catechism he is the past: "the old way under the Pope" (LC 4327); "under the Papacy, where faith was entirely put under the bench" (LC 2807–2808). At table: "The Pope is a mere tormentor of the conscience" (TT 3083–3084, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim); "in Popedom" (TT 3063, Bell's English). Before the Emperor the same adversary is "the Church of Rome as known from its writers" (AC 631–633), whose "Pontiffs, emboldened by the power of the Keys" (AC 1276–1280, per Doc_03) have overreached — and the Apology reserves "the kingdom of Antichrist" for the conditional: "the Papacy also will be a part of the kingdom of Antichrist if it thus defends human services as justifying" (Ap 6729–6731). Two registers, one enemy; the frame is the devil's (4.7): "the devil, Pope, bishops, tyrants, and heretics" (LC 3433, per Doc_05).

**Ecological Function.** The Layer-2 name of [F-pap] and the thing G2 refuses and G12 frames (Doc_04 §2.2 — "as the thing the ecology organizes *against*, not around"); Doc_05 §4.2 (one of four authorities), §7.1 (the first boundary), §1.4 and §2.5 c (felt as compulsion: the Easter law, the altar-linen, the fast). Anchors 1.1, 1.6, 3.3, 3.4, 8.6; opposed by 6.6 and 5.8 (Christendom is not "one man").

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "Antichrist" as a fixed doctrine about the papal office, or as mere abuse; "papist" as this world's neutral term; or the confessional courtesy as a retraction. *World Hearing:* one adversary named in two pitches — the polemic's "Antichrist" and the confession's "Church of Rome" — with the plainest test in the Apology: the papacy is Antichrist's kingdom *if* it makes human services justify. Bell's "Popedom" (six of seven occurrences) and Schmauk's one are the translators' English (Doc_03 6.7).

**[PV], stated.** The confessional register (1530) and the polemical register (1520–21, 1539–45) demonstrably differ on the adversary's name — "the Church of Rome" versus "Antichrist" — a plurality of registers inside one world, directly quoted, that a Representative must not flatten into either (Doc_03 6.7; Doc_04 §3 G12's naming shift under [F-imp]).

**Key Texts.** v2 2121–2124, 2347, 6508–6513; v1 327–328, 357, 408–409; v1 12786–12788; v2 15024, 15240, 15468; LC 58, 2807–2808, 4327; TT 3063, 3083–3084 (Bell's English); AC 631–633, 1276–1280; Ap 6722–6731 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R11]; [R12]; [R8]; [R1]; [R15]; [R25]; [R31]; [R37]; [R38] Ap XV. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 4.7 the devil; 6.6 Christendom; 5.8 congregation; 3.3 Scripture against Fathers; 3.4 doctrines of men; 1.1 indulgence; 1.6 the keys; 6.9 the Turk; 6.8 sects; 7.1 two governments.

#### 6.8 sects / "new spirits" / fanatics / enthusiasts / Anabaptists — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R7 (editorial), R25, R28, R34 (one word), R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only; the other side of this vocabulary belongs to VI.3's own record (Doc_01 §8.2).

**Quick Meaning.** The founder's generic names for the radicals — "new spirits," "fanatics," "enthusiasts" — and the confession's named condemnation of "the Anabaptists and others who think that the Holy Ghost comes to men without the external Word."

**World Meaning.** "Our new spirits, to mock at Baptism, omit from it God's Word" (LC 3840–3842, per Doc_03); "sects clamoring that Baptism is an external thing" (LC 3799–3800, per Doc_03); "the prating of nearly all the fanatical spirits" (LC 4069–4070, per Doc_03); those who "assert that faith alone saves, and that works and external things avail nothing" (LC 3916–3917); "some fanatics" who would have "all sciences… beaten down… by the Gospel" (Hy 926–928). The confession names them: "the Anabaptists and others who think that the Holy Ghost comes to men without the external Word, through their own preparations and works" (AC 253–255); the Apology restates the charge in its own words — "fanatical men, who dream that the Holy Ghost is given not through the Word, but because of certain preparations of their own, if they sit unoccupied and silent in obscure places, waiting for illumination, as the Enthusiasts formerly taught, and the Anabaptists now teach" (Ap 6492–6496) — and reports "through such commendations Muentzer was deceived, and thereby many Anabaptists were led astray" (Ap 10005–10006, bracket Bente/Dau's). In 1522 the congregation's own leaders are "the devil… through his apostles" (v2 15173–15174, per Doc_05); "Sacramentarians" is the founder's one in-voice word for the other rival (Co 16090–16091, per [R34]; the v1 occurrences are Neve's — layer).

**Ecological Function.** The Layer-2 name of [F-rad]; the reason G2's Word is defined as "external" and G7 is fenced "regularly called" (Doc_04 §3 G2, G7); Doc_05 §7.1's boundary and §2.5 (a). Anchors 5.2 (baptism mocked), 5.3 ("those that teach otherwise"), 6.4 (the fence), 2.1 (the slogan misused).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "enthusiast" and "fanatic" as temperament words; "Anabaptist" as a neutral label. *World Hearing:* a specific theological charge — the Spirit without the external Word — made in two confessional wordings; and names this world gave to people its own authorities helped suppress, whose own account is not this world's to give.

**Key Texts.** LC 3799–3842, 3916–3921, 4069–4070; AC 253–255; Ap 6492–6496, 10002–10006; Hy 926–928; v2 15173–15174. **Key Sources.** [R25]; [R37] AC V; [R38] Ap XIII, XXVII; [R28]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 3.1 the Word; 5.2 baptism; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 6.4 calling; 2.1 faith; 4.7 the devil; 6.7 pope/Antichrist; 7.4 insurrection; 4.9 hymn; 5.1 sacrament; 5.5 both kinds; 8.8 images.

#### 6.9 the Turk — Tier 3 (Doc_03: 3)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R7, R25, R31, R37.

**Quick Meaning.** The external enemy named with pope and Jews as outside Christianity — "heathen, Turks, Jews, or false Christians and hypocrites" (LC 2959–2961) — the Diet's "most atrocious, hereditary, and ancient enemy" (AC 49–51, per Doc_04 §3 G12), against whom the household prays ("vanquish the Turks and all enemies," LC 3504) and the founder "will pray… as long as I live" (TT 3134–3136, Bell's English).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a political or ethnic enemy. *World Hearing:* one of the devil's three instruments (4.7), met in this library by a petition and nothing bodily (Doc_05 §1.4) — Doc_08's material more than the lexicon's.

### 5.7 The two governments — sword, obedience, liberty

#### 7.1 the two governments / "kingdom of God" and "kingdom of the world" — and *Bondage*'s "two kingdoms" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1) — [CT], provisional (§2)

**Tags:** [AS] [TC] [DR] [RT] **[CT]**. **AG:** none — both voices. **Registry:** R15, R20, R29, R34, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7); the label "two kingdoms" is in active use in more than one living tradition, none of which this entry characterizes. The [AS] tag is Inferential/Thin as a comparative claim (OG-3).

**Quick Meaning.** God's two governments — the spiritual, which by the Word under Christ makes Christians, and the secular, which by the sword restrains the wicked — both God's, neither to be confounded with the other; and, in a different book, "two kingdoms" of Christ and Satan at war, which is not the same pair.

**World Meaning.** "We must divide all the children of Adam into two classes; the first belong to the kingdom of God, the second to the kingdom of the world" (v3 12139–12141, per Doc_03; OCR uneven). Real Christians "need no secular sword or law. And if all the world were composed of real Christians… no prince, king, lord, sword, or law would be needed" (v3 12188–12191) — and since it is not, "God has ordained the two governments; the spiritual, which by the Holy Spirit under Christ makes Christians and pious people, and the secular, which restrains the unchristian and wicked" (v3 12318–12323, per Doc_03). The two "must be sharply distinguished, and both be permitted to remain; the one to produce piety, the other to bring about external peace" (v3 12371–12374, OCR-damaged, recovered per Doc_03). "The kingdom of God consists not in outward things" (v2 15334–15335, per Doc_03). The confession draws the same line and says why: "our teachers, for the comforting of men's consciences, were constrained to show the difference between the power of the Church and the power of the sword… both of them… are to be held in reverence and honor, as the chief blessings of God on earth" (AC 1281–1285); the Church's power is exercised "without human force, simply by the Word" (AC 1339–1340), and the sword's by "bodily punishments in order to preserve civil justice and peace" (AC 1306–1309, per Doc_03). The Apology lists among the sermons' standing topics "the distinction between the kingdom of Christ, or the spiritual kingdom and political affairs" (Ap 6931–6932).

The distinction is felt from within as the Word's own limit: "We have the *jus verbi*, but not the *executio*" (v2 14864–14866), which is why a right reform had to obtain "the aid of the authorities" (v2 14765) and why "I can drive no man to heaven with a club" (v2 14974, per Doc_05 §4.3). In the household it is the fourth petition's line between "the domestic" and "the neighborly or civil relation and government" (LC 3467–3468, per Doc_05), and the prince's loaf on his arms: "by their office we have protection and peace, and that without them we could not eat and retain our daily bread" (LC 3482–3484). Obedience is owed "save only when commanded to sin; for then they ought to obey God rather than men" (AC 426–428).

The same English phrase carries a second referent. In the argument against Erasmus "there are two kingdoms in the world mutually militating against each other. — That Satan reigns in the one… [I]n the other kingdom Christ reigns" (Co 14747–14759, OCR as found and recovered) — a spiritual-warfare pairing, not the church/civil one. The hymn's "The kingdom ours remaineth" (Hy 3705, Bacon's English) belongs to that second sense. The running marginal "The Two Kingdoms" is the 1930 editor's (v3 12123–12127, OCR-broken across lines in the file; per Doc_03 — layer); the founder's words at those loci are "two classes," "two governments," "the kingdom of God… the kingdom of the world."

**Ecological Function.** G6's own word — "the two governments: the temporal sword ordained of God, obeyed 'save only when commanded to sin'; the prince as addressee, protector and administrator" (Doc_04 §3 G6), Supporting, with the coverage divergence that 1525 and 1555 are outside the library (Doc_04 §6.3; nothing here narrates either). Doc_05 §4's whole Organizational lens is "built around" it; §4.3 gives its criterion of legitimacy; §7.1 lists it as a boundary; §9A pattern 6. Anchors 7.2 (its instrument), 7.3 (its obedience), 7.4 (its 1522 refusal of force), 6.1 (rulers as priests), 4.2 and 4.8 (the prince in the daily bread), 5.6 (the sacrament's public form settled by authority), 2.8 (distinguished "for the comforting of men's consciences"), 2.9 (the other "two kingdoms").

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the two kingdoms doctrine" as a settled system — church and state separated, or the state given a free hand; or a theory this world named and taught as such. *World Hearing:* two governments, both God's, distinguished so that the conscience is not ruled by the sword nor the sword by the Word; obedience limited at sin; the prince honoured as the giver of bread and prayed for at every meal; and a second, different "two kingdoms" of Christ and Satan that a reader must not merge with the first. The moments at which this doctrine was tested hardest (1525, 1555) are not in this library.

**Key Texts.** v3 12139–12141, 12188–12191, 12318–12323, 12371–12374; v2 14764–14766, 14864–14866, 14974, 15334–15335; AC 411–428, 1281–1285, 1306–1312, 1339–1344; Ap 6931–6932; LC 3467–3468, 3480–3484; Co 14747–14759 (OCR as found); Hy 3705; v3 12123–12127 (editorial marginal — layer).

**Key Sources.** [R20] *Secular Authority* (Part One only, v3 12100–12440; Parts Two and Three, 13133 to the work's end, unread); [R37] AC XVI, XXVIII; [R38] Ap XV; [R15]; [R25] LC Fourth Petition (per Doc_05); [R34] (OCR-degraded); [R29]. Author Gravity: none. **Coverage:** the treatise's own "main part," on the limits of obedience (Part Two), is unread; Philadelphia vol. IV [R62] and anything post-1546 unvendored.

**CT Contest Type.** *Historical scope:* whether the later systematic label ("two kingdoms doctrine") applies as broadly to the founder's own usage as claimed, given that in this library he says "two classes," "two governments," and "kingdom of God / kingdom of the world" in one work, "two kingdoms" of Christ and Satan in another, and never treats the phrase as one fixed technical term across both — while the confessional voice says "the power of the Church and the power of the sword." **Status: provisional — no secondary source is rowed for the contest (Doc_03 §11 item 4; OG-4); §2 states the leads proposed for the Registry owner and the rule that the tag does not reach a deployment chunk without a row.**

**Related Terms.** 7.2 the sword; 7.3 obedience; 7.4 insurrection; 6.1 estate; 6.2 we are all priests; 6.4 calling; 4.2 household; 4.8 daily; 5.6 the mass; 2.8 conscience; 2.9 free will; 4.7 the devil; 6.6 Christendom; 6.7 pope/Antichrist; 7.5 must and free.

#### 7.2 the sword / temporal sword / secular authority — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [TC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R10, R11, R20, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The power ordained of God that bears "sword and rod with which to punish the evil and to protect the good," reaching pope and monk alike, which the spiritual estate may not wield — its sword being "the sword of the Spirit."

**World Meaning.** The temporal power "bear[s] sword and rod with which to punish the evil and to protect the good" (v2 2250–2251) and "is ordained of God to punish evil-doers" (v2 2265), reaching "pope, bishops, priests, monks, nuns or anybody else." "So Paul interprets the secular sword, Romans xiii, and says it is not a terror to good works, but to the evil" (v3 12323–12326). The spiritual estate's "sword is not to be of iron, but the sword of the Spirit" (v2 1195–1199). The confession: "lawful civil ordinances are good works of God," and Christians may "serve as soldiers… make oath… marry a wife" (AC 411–416); the bishops' sword-power is "another office than the ministry of the Gospel" (AC 1329–1330, per Doc_03), and civil power is to "restrain men with the sword and bodily punishments in order to preserve civil justice and peace" (AC 1306–1309, per Doc_03).
