"Secular" is Schindel's 1930 English; the 1915–16 volumes prefer "temporal" (Doc_03 7.2 — layer).

**Ecological Function.** G6's instrument, developed at 7.1; 5.9's counter-term (the ban has no iron); 7.4's refused alternative (flails and cudgels); Doc_05 §4.2's prince.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "the sword" as violence sanctioned by religion, or "secular" as non-religious. *World Hearing:* God's own ordinance for external peace, held by the temporal ruler and by no cleric, honoured and prayed for, and never the Church's instrument.

**Key Texts.** v2 2250–2269; v3 12300–12326; AC 411–416, 1306–1309. **Key Sources.** [R11]; [R20]; [R37] AC XVI, XXVIII. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 7.1 two governments; 7.3 obedience; 7.4 insurrection; 5.9 the ban; 6.3 office.

#### 7.3 obedience / "obey God rather than men" / honor — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R11, R25, R26, R31, R37. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Owed to parents, masters and magistrates as to God's own ordering — "All that govern are called Fathers" — and limited at exactly one point: "save only when commanded to sin."

**World Meaning.** The Fourth Commandment's "honor" is "a far higher thing… than to love one" (LC 1051–1060, per Doc_03), extended to "All that govern" (TT 2422, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim); parents are to be regarded "as in God's stead… however lowly, poor, frail, and queer they may be" (LC 1066–1069, per Doc_05 §2.1); in the house "among us there must necessarily be such inequality and ordered difference, and therefore God commands it to be observed" (LC 1072–1074, per Doc_05). The Home Chart prescribes "For Earthly Authorities" and "For those Under Authority" (SC 625–633). The confession: "Christians are necessarily bound to obey their own magistrates and laws save only when commanded to sin; for then they ought to obey God rather than men" (AC 426–428), and of bishops, "when they teach or ordain anything against the Gospel, then the congregations have a commandment of God prohibiting obedience" (AC 1342–1344). "It is our duty and obligation to pray if we would be Christians, as much as it is our duty and obligation to obey our parents and the government" (LC 3032–3034, per Doc_05 §2.3).

**Ecological Function.** G6's taught side (Doc_04 §3 G6) and G5's limit on it; Doc_05 §4.1–§4.4; anchors 7.1, 4.2, 6.5 (what hearers owe pastors).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* obedience as servility, or the limit-clause as a general right of resistance. *World Hearing:* honour of a majesty hidden in parents and rulers, limited at the one point where obedience would be sin — with the treatise's own part on the limits of obedience unread in this build (7.1).

**Key Texts.** LC 1051–1075; SC 625–633; AC 426–428, 1342–1344. **Key Sources.** [R25] LC Fourth Commandment (opening); [R37] AC XVI, XXVIII; [R26]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 7.1 two governments; 7.2 the sword; 4.2 household; 6.5 pastor; 2.8 conscience.

#### 7.4 insurrection / rebellion / "the common man" (1522 only) — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2) — [PV]

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT] **[PV]**. **AG:** Luther-only, cross-register, period-bound to 1521–22 — confirmed (§4; Ap 10002–10006 is a 1531 retrospect naming Müntzer, not the 1522 vocabulary). **Registry:** R11, R15, R19, R44 (context); R48, R94 (bars). **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The unrest addressed at the turn of 1522 — the common man who "would indeed have good reason to lay about him with flails and cudgels" — met with the refusal of force and the confidence that "God will watch over His Word."

**World Meaning.** "The common man has been brooding over the injury he has suffered in property, in body and in soul, and has become provoked… and would indeed have good reason to lay about him with flails and cudgels, as the peasants are threatening to do" (v3 10665–10672, OCR hyphenation and a stray "!" as found); yet there is "no fear whatever that there will be an insurrection" because "God will watch over His Word" (v3 10696–10702, per Doc_03). In the sermons: "I could have brought great bloodshed upon Germany, Yea, I could have started such a little game at Worms that even the emperor would not have been safe. But what would it have been? A fool's play. I did nothing; I left it to the Word" (v2 14932–14935). The nobility's "three rods" (v2 2135, per Doc_03) and the peasant of the pamphlet asking "Where is my flail?" (v3 10586, editor's quotation, [R44] — layer) are the frame. In 1529 the Large Catechism remembers "murder, and riot" checked by "the prayer of a few godly men" that "intervened like a wall of iron" (LC 3190–3194, per Doc_05 §1.4) — naming no event.

**Ecological Function.** [F-pop]'s Layer-2 vocabulary as the library has it (Doc_04 §3 G6's forces notation); G2's restraint applied to "the common man" (Doc_04 §3 G8); Doc_05 §1.4. **Bar, stated:** the 1525 tract's own language is not in this library and is barred by [R94]; nothing here characterizes 1525 beyond Doc_02 §12.4's disclosure; [R48]'s named absence (the tract itself, not vendored) is the same bar's other face.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* the 1525 tract's reputation projected onto this vocabulary. *World Hearing:* the 1522 texts' sympathy for "the common man," confidence in the Word, and refusal of force — a period-bound stance the library attests only for 1521–22.

**[PV], stated.** Period-bound: this term is representative of 1521–22 in this library and not of the whole window (Doc_03 7.4; the Framework's own "periods").

**Key Texts.** v3 10641–10672, 10696–10702; v2 14932–14935; LC 3190–3196. **Key Sources.** [R19] *Earnest Exhortation* (opening only; v3 10751–11659 unread); [R15]. The bar on the 1525 material is Ecological Function's own stated sentence and its rows are not repeated here (corrected this revision, Round 1 finding S1; the codes' continued presence in this field was itself Round 2 finding R2-C6). Author Gravity: Luther-only, period-bound — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 7.1 two governments; 7.2 the sword; 7.5 must and free; 3.1 the Word; 6.8 sects; 9.5 prayer.

#### 7.5 "must" and "free" — Christian liberty, the weak in faith, offence — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [AS] (the pairing) [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none for "liberty"; Luther-only, single-register (the Sermons) for the "must"/"free" pairing — confirmed and extended by Doc_05 §11 item 5 (the Third and Fourth Sermons carry it repeatedly). **Registry:** R13, R15, R16, R25, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7). The [AS] tag is Inferential/Thin as a comparative claim (OG-3).

**Quick Meaning.** The 1522 sermons' governing distinction — the "must" is what necessity requires, faith above all; the "free" is what I may use or not, so long as it profits my brother — a liberty preached only to captive consciences, bound by love to the weak, and turned by 1529 against those who called their neglect "liberty."

**World Meaning.** "A Christian man is a perfectly free lord of all, subject to none. A Christian man is a perfectly dutiful servant of all, subject to all" (v2 11618–11621). That paradox is the ground; the sermons of 1522 are its application to one congregation that had "gone too fast" (v2 14727–14728). "Take note of these two things, 'must' and 'free.' The 'must' is that which necessity requires, and which must ever be unyielding; as, for instance, the faith… But 'free' is that in which I have choice, and may use or not, yet in such wise that it profit my brother and not me. Now do not make a 'must' out of what is 'free'" (v2 14790–14796). The free things are named — "the leaving of monasteries by monks, the marriage of priests, and the images… no compulsion or law must be made of them" (v2 15288–15292); meat on Friday; the cup in the hands — and the rule for them is the neighbour: "we must not look upon ourselves… but upon our neighbor… have patience with him for a time, suffer his weakness and help him bear it; we should give him milk-food, too" (v2 14711–14722); "there are also brothers and sisters on the other side who belong to us, and must still be won" (v2 14728–14729); "Why will you not herein also serve those who are weak in the faith and abstain from your liberty?" (v2 15364–15365). "There are some who can run, others must walk, still others can hardly creep" (v2 14737–14738); "the faith must always remain pure and immovable in the heart, never wavering; but love moves and is guided, according as our neighbors may grasp it" (v2 14734–14736). "You say it was right according to the Scriptures. I agree, but what becomes of order?" (v2 14761–14762). "It is wicked to make a necessity and a commandment of that which is free" (v2 15979–15980). And the liberty has an address: "This Christian liberty I would have preached only to poor, humble, captive consciences, so that poor children, nuns and monks, who would like to escape from their bondage may… use their freedom in an orderly and Christian way" (v2 15915–15920).

By 1529 the same word is the charge. Pastors have kept "no more of the Gospel than such a lazy, pernicious, shameful, carnal liberty" (LC 78–80); people "pretend that it is a matter of liberty and not necessary… go so far that they become quite brutish" (LC 4244–4248); "no one should by any means be coerced or compelled, lest we institute a new murdering of souls. Nevertheless… If you wish such liberty, you may just as well have the liberty to be no Christian" (LC 4250–4296). The confession settles the free things as liberty in rites: holy-days and usages "which may be observed without sin… Nevertheless… consciences are not to be burdened, as though such observance was necessary to salvation" (AC 391–398, per Doc_04 §3 G8); "Such liberty in human rites was not unknown to the Fathers" (AC 1068, per Doc_03); and the Apology: "the use of liberty is to be so controlled that the inexperienced may not be offended" (Ap 6978–6979). The "must" never moves; the "free" is governed by love, and love by the weak.

**Ecological Function.** G8 — Tensional: "the persistent counter-force inside the reform against the reform's own logic — the Word licenses; love restrains; then, when restraint has produced sloth, the same word 'liberty' is the charge" (Doc_04 §3 G8). The G2–G8 competition is the ecology's sharpest tension (Doc_04 §5; Doc_05 §10B i); the 1522 refusal to compel and the 1529 refusal to count the absent as Christians are "the same gravity's two directions" (Doc_05 §10B iii). Doc_05 §2.4 (vi): "Liberty bound by love sets the pace"; §7.1: "the boundary *inside* the world"; §1.2: the same line at the table. Anchors 5.5, 5.6, 8.6, 8.8, 8.1, 8.3 (the "free" things), 3.4 (the "must" made of the free), 4.6 (the neighbor as measure), 2.8 (whom liberty is for), 5.3 (the Supper neither compelled nor despised), 4.1 (the one compulsion the program keeps — Doc_04 §5's G4–G8 cell). Tier 1 attaches to this Tensional gravity because it organizes a substantial, bounded portion of the ecology — the pace of everything, and the one documented formation event (§1.1).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* Christian liberty as autonomy — freedom from rules, the right to do what Scripture permits regardless of others — or, from the other side, the 1529 rebuke as a retraction of the 1522 liberty. *World Hearing:* a liberty of captive consciences, whose exercise is measured entirely by the weak brother; a "must" that never yields (faith) and a "free" that is never to be made a law; and one word that restrains the fast in 1522 and goads the slack in 1529 without contradiction.

**Key Texts.** v2 11618–11621, 11848–11851; v2 14711–14740, 14761–14762, 14790–14801, 14838–14841; v2 15288–15292, 15364–15366, 15915–15920, 15979–15980; v2 14945, 15031, 15070 (per Doc_05 §11 item 5); LC 78–80, 4244–4248, 4250–4296; AC 391–398, 1068; Ap 6978–6986 (read this pass).

**Key Sources.** [R13] *Christian Liberty* (opening only; the treatise's own argument, v2 11870–13025, unread — its paradox is quoted from the vendored opening); [R15] all eight Sermons (Doc_05 §11); [R16]; [R25] LC preface and Sacrament; [R37] AC XV, XXVI, XXVIII; [R38] Ap XV. Author Gravity: the pairing is single-register — disclosed; the gravity is both voices. **Confidence:** Documented for the texts; Widely Accepted for the 1522 outcome (Steimle's editorial account, Doc_01 §2.3); reception "one congregation, once, editorial" (Doc_04 §3 G8).

**Related Terms.** 3.1 the Word; 2.1 faith; 2.8 conscience; 4.6 neighbor; 3.4 doctrines of men; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 5.5 both kinds; 5.6 the mass; 8.1 vows; 8.3 marriage; 8.6 fasting; 8.8 images; 7.1 two governments; 7.4 insurrection; 4.7 the devil; 4.1 catechism; 9.7 patience.

### 5.8 Vows, chastity, marriage — the monastic and marital cluster

#### 8.1 vow / monastic vows — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC] [RT]. **AG:** none; Ap XXVII read entire this pass. **Registry:** R12, R16, R24, R25, R37, R38; R61 as named absence. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only. The seedbed question (whether this vocabulary is the Augustinian inheritance's own) is carried unchanged (OG-3; Doc_04 §8).

**Quick Meaning.** The binding promise this world declares void where it is made to merit — once taught to be "equal to Baptism," a "state of perfection" — so that "no man's law, no vow, can annul the commandment and ordinance of God."

**World Meaning.** "No man's law, no vow, can annul the commandment and ordinance of God" (AC 726–727, per Doc_03). The confession recalls what was taught: "They taught that vows were equal to Baptism" (AC 1110–1115), "a state of perfection" (AC 1130–1131) that merited forgiveness — vows "commonly taken have been wicked services, and, consequently, are void" (AC 1204–1206). The founder to the Teutonic knights: "such a vow is nothing at all and is not to be kept, unless a man have God's special grace" (v3 20906–20908, OCR punctuation as found); to his congregation, of "nuns and monks, who would like to escape from their bondage" (v2 15916–15918). The Apology, which "wish[es] here to consider that book as reiterated" — the unvendored *De Votis Monasticis* (Ap 9647–9649; [R61], not vendored — "excluded from this volume because of its size" (v3 20823–20824, the 1930 volume's own introduction — layer)) — says "we hold that lawful vows ought to be observed" (Ap 9619–9620) and then names what makes a vow unlawful: the opinion that "he who vows thinks that he merits the remission of sins before God" (Ap 9651–9653); "It is madness to make human tradition, which has neither God's command nor promise, equal to the ordinance of Christ" (Ap 9732–9734); obedience, poverty and celibacy "are, as exercises, adiaphora" that "the saints can use… without impiety, just as Bernard, Franciscus, and other holy men used them" (Ap 9738–9742); vows of chastity are not lawful "in those who do not have the gift of continence" (Ap 10030–10031); and vows "extorted either from the unwilling or from those who on account of age were not able to judge" are not vows even by the canons (Ap 9627–9630, 10073–10077). The older lay sense survives in the catechism's catalogue: "a vow to St. Sebastian or Rochio" (LC 454–455, per Doc_03).

**Ecological Function.** G9's refusal — "the monastic estate refused, the married household and the married pastor affirmed" (Doc_04 §3 G9) — carried under 8.3; refused as merit-works (2.4, 2.1) and as a "doctrine of men" (3.4); one of 7.5's "free" things (leaving the monastery). The against-the-grain women's traces (nuns deciding by conscience, Doc_05 §1.3) sit here.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* that this world condemned all vows, or all monks. *World Hearing:* lawful vows kept; unlawful ones — those made to merit, made equal to baptism, extorted, or made against the gift one lacks — void; the "exercises" themselves indifferent.

**Key Texts.** AC 726–727, 1110–1115, 1146–1148; v3 20906–20908; v2 15916–15918; Ap 9617–9653, 9732–9760, 10024–10038, 10073–10080 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R37] AC XXVII; [R38] Ap XXVII, entire; [R24]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 8.3 marriage; 8.2 chastity; 8.4 spiritual/Geysterey; 8.5 perfection; 2.4 merit; 3.4 doctrines of men; 5.2 baptism; 7.5 must and free; 2.1 faith; 8.6 fasting.

#### 8.2 chastity — "false chastity," "unchaste chastity," "the true chastity of wedlock" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [AS] (the coinage) [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none for the argument; Luther-only, single-register (the Teutonic Order exhortation) for the coinage. **Registry:** R24, R25, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only. The [AS] tag is Inferential/Thin (OG-3, seedbed caveat).

**Quick Meaning.** The monastic word turned inside out — the knights are to "give up your unchaste chastity and to marry," to "lay aside false chastity and take upon them the true chastity of wedlock"; where nature has its course, chastity outside marriage "is not possible."

**World Meaning.** The exhortation's own title: that the knights "Lay Aside False Chastity and Take Upon Them the True Chastity of Wedlock" (v3 20875–20876, per Doc_03); "give up your unchaste chastity and to marry" (v3 20891); "the abomination of 'spiritual' chastity" (v3 20906). The catechism states the ground as nature: "where nature has its course, as it is implanted by God, it is not possible to remain chaste without marriage. For flesh and blood remain flesh and blood, and the natural inclination and excitement have their course without let or hindrance, as everybody sees and feels" (LC 1726–1729), with "some exceptions (although few)" (LC 1722–1725, per Doc_05). The confession: "it is not unknown to what extent perpetual chastity is in the power of man" (AC 1168–1169, per Doc_03); the Apology: "Of celibacy we do not like to speak, how pure this is in most of those who desire to be continent" (Ap 9698–9699), and "all do not have the gift of continence" (Ap 10027). The monk who cannot keep the vow "should conscientiously examine himself; if heart and conscience are strong… let him marry" (v2 15010–15013, per Doc_05 §1.2).

**Ecological Function.** G9's inverted vocabulary (Doc_04 §3 G9); 8.3's ground and 8.1's refusal in one word; Doc_05 §1.2 (the body as nature) and §1.3 (nuns deciding by conscience). Doc_03's decline of [PV] stands.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "chastity" as celibacy, and the argument as a lowering of standards. *World Hearing:* true chastity *is* marriage for all but the few with the gift; the vow of the rest is "unchaste chastity."

**Key Texts.** v3 20875–20908; LC 1721–1729; AC 1168–1169; Ap 9698–9701, 10024–10031. **Key Sources.** [R24] (opening only); [R25]; [R38]. Author Gravity: the coinage is single-register — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 8.3 marriage; 8.1 vows; 8.4 spiritual/Geysterey; 4.2 household.

#### 8.3 marriage / matrimony / "the married estate" — Tier 1 (Doc_03: 1)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices, catechesis, exhortation, conversation, confession. **Registry:** R9, R24, R25, R26, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only (§0 Discipline 7).

**Quick Meaning.** "The most common and noblest estate, which pervades all Christendom" — ordained by God at creation, "a help against human infirmity," necessary for nearly everyone, forbidden to no priest, and the household in which this world's formation is to happen.

**World Meaning.** God "created man and woman separately (as is evident), not for lewdness, but that they should [legitimately] live together, be fruitful, beget children, and nourish and train them to the honor of God" (LC 1693–1696, bracket Bente/Dau's). Marriage is "not a peculiar estate, but the most common and noblest estate, which pervades all Christendom" (LC 1714–1716), before which "both ecclesiastical and civil estates must humble themselves and all be found in this estate" (LC 1713–1716, per Doc_03); "where nature has its course… it is not possible to remain chaste without marriage" (LC 1726–1727), with "some exceptions (although few) whom God has especially excepted… or whom He has released by a high, supernatural gift" (LC 1722–1725). It is "a divine and blessed estate" (LC 1692) and "the estate of marriage is well-pleasing to Him" (v3 21017–21018) — two separate texts making the same point, not one (corrected this revision: this document had merged both phrases under Doc_03's v3 21016–21018 citation, which carries only the second; Round 1 finding S2, corrected again at Round 2 — R2-S1 — after this note first misattributed the merge to Doc_03 itself). At table: "Matrimony is necessary and commanded" (TT 2432–2438, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim); "Matrimony proceedeth freely in every state and calling" (TT 2432). The Sixth Commandment's household explanation: "so that our words and actions will be clean and decent and so that everyone will love and honor their spouses" (SC 101–102). The Home Chart addresses husbands, wives, widows, the man and woman of the house (SC 635–660, per Doc_05).

The confession makes the pastor's marriage a public cause: "God ordained marriage to be a help against human infirmity" (AC 751); "our priests were desirous to avoid these open scandals, they married wives" (AC 713–715); "it is to be expected that the churches shall at some time lack pastors if marriage is any longer forbidden… men, and that, priests, are cruelly put to death, contrary to the intent of the Canons, for no other cause than marriage. Paul… calls that a doctrine of devils which forbids marriage" (AC 754–767). The Apology: "the law of nature [or of God] in men cannot be removed by vows or enactments" (Ap 10025–10026), and among the sermons' standing subjects it lists "marriage; of the education and instruction of children, of chastity" (Ap 6933). And the founder's own marriage reaches the library at one remove, as patience: "I must have patience with Kate my wife; to conclude, the patiences are so many, that my whole life is nothing but patience" (TT 3013–3015, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim). Marriage is a "free" thing that no law may forbid or compel (v2 15288–15292); the monk who marries does so on "a clear text of Scripture" and his own conscience (v2 15004–15013, per Doc_05 §4.3).

**Ecological Function.** G9's positive head — "vows, 'false chastity,' and marriage: the monastic estate refused, the married household and the married pastor affirmed" (Doc_04 §3 G9), Supporting — and the site of G4 (4.2). Doc_05 §1.2 (sex and marriage stated as nature), §1.3 (the women's traces are almost entirely this gravity's shadow: wives, widows, mistresses, nuns, pastors' wives), §9A pattern 8. Anchors 8.1 (its refused alternative), 8.2 (its true chastity), 6.1 (the estate word), 6.5 (the married pastor), 4.2 (the house), 6.4 (a calling), 4.8 ("a devout spouse" in the daily bread). G9's three empty matrix cells (Doc_04 §5) mark it as organizing a bounded region — the household — not the whole; that region is a hub (4.2), which is why the head term is Tier 1.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* marriage as a private romantic bond, or this world's teaching as merely permitting clergy to marry. *World Hearing:* the noblest and commonest estate of all, ordained at creation, before which the clerical and civil estates bow; the household of formation; a public cause for which priests were killed; and the founder's own life "nothing but patience." No woman's own voice on this term is in the library (Doc_02 §12.1).

**Key Texts.** LC 1687–1729; SC 101–102, 635–660; v3 21017–21018; TT 2432–2438, 3013–3015 (Bell's English); AC 713–715, 751–767, 1136–1144; Ap 6933, 10024–10031 (read this pass); v2 15004–15013, 15288–15292.

**Key Sources.** [R25] LC Sixth Commandment; [R26] SC; [R37] AC XXIII; [R38] Ap XXVII; [R24]; [R31] TT (Contested as verbatim); [R15] Sermons 3 and 5. Editorial-layer traces: Duchess Margaret as dedicatee [R9]; Albert's marriage, "July 1, 1526" [R24]. Author Gravity: none. **Article 20:** the household is documented from the father's side only (Doc_05 §1.3).

**Related Terms.** 8.1 vows; 8.2 chastity; 8.4 spiritual/Geysterey; 6.1 estate; 6.4 calling; 6.5 pastor; 4.2 household; 4.8 daily; 7.5 must and free; 9.7 patience; 6.2 we are all priests.

#### 8.4 "spiritual" / "spirituality" (*Geysterey*) / the orders — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [AS] [DR] [TC]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R11, R16, R24, R25, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only. The [AS] tag is Inferential/Thin (OG-3 — the Augustinian seedbed is the neighbour most likely to share this vocabulary).

**Quick Meaning.** The claimed status of monks and clergy, in scare-quotes in the world's own text — "few people will become monks and 'spiritual,' because the Gospel is beginning to shine, and it reveals that 'spirituality'" (*Geysterey*) — and the orders whose rules "contradict Christ."

**World Meaning.** "Few people will become monks and 'spiritual,' because the Gospel is beginning to shine, and it reveals that 'spirituality'" (v3 20994–20995, per Doc_03), with the translator's footnote giving the founder's own word, *Geysterey* (v3 21027 — the lemma is the text's). "This one saying of Christ mightily condemns all orders and spiritual rules" (v2 16003–16011, per Doc_03); "those who are now called 'spiritual'--priests, bishops or popes" (v2 2246–2247, per Doc_03). The confession names the claim — "the exalted title of being the spiritual life and the perfect life" (AC 976–978, per Doc_03) — and the catechism the works: the Carthusian's works are not "as noble and good as if God should pick up a straw" (LC 3822–3826, per Doc_03). The Apology says such works "arrogate to themselves the title of a perfect and spiritual life, and are far preferred to the works of God's commandments" — "the works of one's own calling, the administration of the state, the management of a family, married life" (Ap 6788–6793), and that many "have gone into cloisters in order to become holy and spiritual" (Ap 6797–6798, bracket Bente/Dau's).

**Ecological Function.** 6.1's refused adjective and 8.1's claim; G7 and G9 in their monastic form (Doc_04 §3 G7, G9); [F-inh] felt as "one's own former vow and cowl" (Doc_05 §1.4). The seedbed question — whether the vocabulary is the inheritance's own — is unanswerable here (Doc_04 §8).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "spiritual" as inward, devout, or non-material. *World Hearing:* a *claimed status* over other Christians — mocked in scare-quotes — against which every calling is equally holy.

**Key Texts.** v3 20994–20995, 21027 (footnote — layer for the English, the text for the German); v2 16003–16020; AC 976–978; Ap 6785–6798. **Key Sources.** [R24]; [R16]; [R37]; [R38] Ap XV. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 6.1 estate; 8.1 vows; 8.5 perfection; 8.2 chastity; 6.4 calling; 8.3 marriage.

#### 8.5 perfection / "evangelical counsels" / "state of perfection" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [TC]. **AG:** weighted to Melanchthon (AC, and now Ap XXVII entire). **Registry:** R20, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The monastic claim to keep "counsels" above the commandments and so to be in "a state of perfection" — refused and redefined: perfection is "to fear God from the heart, and yet to conceive great faith… and to serve our calling."

**World Meaning.** The monks taught that their life "kept not only the precepts, but also the so-called 'evangelical counsels,'" and was "a state of perfection" (AC 1130–1131). The confession's redefinition: "Christian perfection is to fear God from the heart, and yet to conceive great faith… and meanwhile, to be diligent in outward good works, and to serve our calling" (AC 1233–1239); "they who believe and love the most are the perfect ones, whether outwardly they be male or female, prince or peasant, monk or layman" (v3 12133–12136, per Doc_03). The Apology's defence: "perfection is growth in the fear of God, and in confidence in the mercy promised in Christ, and in devotion to one's calling" (Ap 9827–9829); if monasticism is only "a state in which to acquire perfection," then "monasticism will be no more a state of perfection than the life of a farmer or mechanic. For these are also states in which to acquire perfection" (Ap 9905–9907); "Perfection is in that which Christ adds: Follow Me. An example of obedience in one's calling is here presented" (Ap 10008–10009); and the story of Anthony sent to a shoemaker in Alexandria who "early in the morning… prayed in a few words for the entire state, and then attended to his trade" (Ap 9912–9921). Evangelical poverty "does not consist in the abandonment of property, but in not being avaricious" (Ap 9993–9994).

**Ecological Function.** 8.1's claim and 6.4's climax ("perfection with us is that every one with true faith should obey his own calling," Ap 10019–10020); Doc_05 §5's relief ("felt from within as relief"); refuses 8.4's title. Weighted to the confessional voice.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* "perfection" as moral flawlessness or as a spiritual elite's achievement. *World Hearing:* growth in fear, faith and one's calling — the farmer's as much as the monk's — with no counsels above the commandments.

**Key Texts.** AC 1113–1115, 1130–1131, 1233–1247; v3 12133–12136; Ap 9819–9836, 9897–9924, 10008–10022 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R37] AC XXVII; [R38] Ap XXVII, entire; [R20]. Author Gravity: weighted to Melanchthon — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 8.1 vows; 8.4 spiritual/Geysterey; 6.4 calling; 2.3 good works; 4.5 fear and love God; 2.4 merit; 1.7 the cross..

#### 8.6 fasting / meats / fast-days / "butter-letters" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 3 ↑)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT] **[DR]** (added, §1.5). **AG:** none. **Registry:** R16, R25, R26, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Bodily discipline kept as "free and voluntary, both as to the day and as to the food" — "excellent disciplines for the body" — and refused as commanded: "it is allowable to eat milk, butter, eggs, cheese and meat every day, whether it be Sunday or Friday, Lent or Advent."

**World Meaning.** "It is allowable to eat milk, butter, eggs, cheese and meat every day, whether it be Sunday or Friday, Lent or Advent; and no one needs to pay butter-money or buy butter-letters" (v2 15963–15969); it is "sheer devil's knavery for the pope to sell letters and grant permission to eat butter, meat" (v2 15986–15989, per Doc_03). But the discipline itself is kept: "fasting and other physical preparations are excellent disciplines for the body" (SC 530–531) — they do not make one worthy; "we do not condemn fasting in itself, but the traditions which prescribe certain days and certain meats" (AC 1060–1062, per Doc_03). The Apology: bodily exercises "are to be undertaken not because they are services that justify, but in order to curb the flesh, lest satiety may overpower us, and render us secure and indifferent" (Ap 6950–6953) — and "this prescribed form of certain meats and times does nothing [as experience shows] towards curbing the flesh. For it is more luxurious and sumptuous than other feasts" (Ap 6955–6957). At the table the line is drawn by the neighbour: "If you cannot give up meat without harm to yourself, or if you are sick, you may eat whatever you like, and if any one takes offence, let him be offended" (v2 15229–15231, per Doc_05 §1.2) — but "if you should be pressed to eat fish instead of meat on Friday… I will eat meat in spite of you" (v2 15238–15244, per Doc_05), and toward the weak, "we must bear patiently with them and not use our liberty" (v2 15253–15259, per Doc_05).

**Ecological Function.** 7.5's "free" thing at the table (Doc_05 §1.2: "The body's food is the 'must'/'free' distinction (G8) applied at the table"); 3.4's instance; the year "un-shaped by principle" (Doc_05 §3). Promoted for its paired Distortion Risk ([144]).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* that this world abolished fasting, or made eating meat a badge. *World Hearing:* fasting kept as a free bodily discipline against satiety, the calendar of meats refused as a "must," and the use of the freedom governed by the weak neighbour.

**Key Texts.** v2 15963–15969, 15986–15999; SC 530–532; AC 1048–1062; Ap 6938–6960 (read this pass); v2 15229–15259. **Key Sources.** [R16]; [R26]; [R37] AC XXVI; [R38] Ap XV; [R15] Sermon 4. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 7.5 must and free; 3.4 doctrines of men; 4.6 neighbor; 5.11 worthy/examination; 8.1 vows.

#### 8.7 saints — invocation, "helper in need," "the memory of saints" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R2, R25, R30, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The saints as helpers in need — "if he was afraid of fire, he chose St. Lawrence as his helper in need" — refused as invocation while "the memory of saints may be set before us, that we may follow their faith and good works."

**World Meaning.** The catechism's catalogue of the old way: "if he was afraid of fire, he chose St. Lawrence as his helper in need; if he dreaded pestilence, he made a vow to St. Sebastian or Rochio" (LC 453–455) — the trust that makes an idol (4.4). The confession refuses the invocation and keeps the memory: "the Scripture teaches not the invocation of saints," but "the memory of saints may be set before us, that we may follow their faith and good works" (AC 620–626, per Doc_03), with Christ alone as Mediator (3.6). The Apology traces the decline: "mention of the saints seems to have been admitted with a design that is endurable, as in the ancient prayers. Afterwards invocation followed, and abuses that are prodigious and more than heathenish followed invocation. From invocation the next step was to images" (Ap 7606–7610). The Bavarian who said "Fie on thee, Leonard" (TT 3045–3050, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim) is the table's exemplum; the Theses had denied that indulgences are "the merits of Christ and the Saints" (v1 1373–1375, per Doc_03). The other "saints" — the Creed's *communio sanctorum* — is 5.8.

**Ecological Function.** [F-inh]'s functional protections, refused and replaced by the Word as "holy water" (Doc_04 §3 G12's forces notation; Doc_05 §1.4); memory, not intercession (Doc_05 §3); 4.4's idol in its most common form.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* that this world rejected the saints. *World Hearing:* the saints remembered and imitated, not invoked; the "helper in need" replaced by the one Mediator.

**Key Texts.** LC 451–457; AC 620–626; Ap 7605–7612; TT 3045–3050 (Bell's English). **Key Sources.** [R25]; [R37] AC XXI; [R38] Ap XXI (grep-located, 7605–7616 read in context). Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 4.4 to have a god; 3.6 Christ alone; 5.8 congregation; 8.8 images; 9.6 martyr; 3.4 doctrines of men.

#### 8.8 images — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 3 ↑)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT] **[DR]** (added, §1.5). **AG:** none — both voices (Ap 7610–7616, 8946, 9001; §4; Doc_03 had "Luther-only, cross-register, Third Sermon unread"). **Registry:** R15, R16, R28, R38; R43 (context). **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** One of the 1522 "unnecessary things" — "we are free to have them or not"; "neither here nor there, neither evil nor good" — to be abolished "if they are going to be worshiped, otherwise not," and never by compulsion.

**World Meaning.** Images are "unnecessary, and we are free to have them or not, although it would be much better if we did not have them. I am not partial to them" (v2 15062–15065); they "ought to be abolished if they are going to be worshiped, otherwise not" (v2 15144–15145); "we would do better to give a poor man a gold-piece than to give God a golden image" (v2 15165–15166); "images are neither here nor there, neither evil nor good, we may have them or not, as we please" (v2 15204–15206) — all from the Third and Fourth Sermons, read at Doc_05 (§11 item 4). Among "the leaving of monasteries by monks, the marriage of priests, and the images… no compulsion or law must be made of them" (v2 15288–15292); those whose only Christianity is that they "break the images" are warned (v2 15906–15909, per Doc_03). The funeral preface mocks "costly shrines of gold and silver, and images set with gems and jewels; but within are dead men's bones" (Hy 1027–1029, Bacon's English). The Apology tells the same descent: "From invocation the next step was to images; these also were worshiped, and a virtue was supposed to exist in these" — with the story of a statue "which moved automatically by a trick" (Ap 7610–7616). The other side reaches the library only as the editor's quotation of Karlstadt ([R43], context; "Dr. Carlstadt" named once in the sermons among four names, v2 14997, per Doc_05).

**Ecological Function.** 7.5's "free" thing and the 1522 crisis's test case (Doc_05 §2.5 a); 8.7's next step; Doc_05 §7.1's boundary against the radicals. Promoted for its paired Distortion Risk ([144]); no [PV] — the Karlstadt divergence is context-only (R43).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* that this world smashed images, or forbade them. *World Hearing:* images indifferent — kept or removed freely, removed where worshipped — and iconoclasm by force a "must" made of a "free" thing.

**Key Texts.** v2 15062–15065, 15144–15145, 15165–15166, 15204–15206, 15288–15293; Hy 1027–1029; Ap 7605–7616. **Key Sources.** [R15] Sermons 3 and 4 (read at Doc_05); [R16]; [R28]; [R38] Ap XXI (grep-located). Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 7.5 must and free; 8.7 saints; 3.4 doctrines of men; 6.8 sects; 5.5 both kinds.

### 5.9 Comfort, trial, and death — the pastoral register

#### 9.1 comfort / consolation — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R3, R15, R25, R27, R28, R37, R38; R5 (unread). **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** What the doctrine of faith brings to anxious consciences — "the greatest consolation" — and what absolution, baptism, the Supper and the burial hymns are for.

**World Meaning.** "God-fearing and anxious consciences find by experience that it brings the greatest consolation, because consciences cannot be set at rest through any works, but only by faith" (AC 546–549); the Apology opens its chief article on the "necessary and most abundant consolation to devout consciences" (Ap 566–567, per Doc_03) and closes it: "this alone brings sure and firm consolation to pious minds" (Ap 1429–1430). The sermons' "many absolutions" are for "comfort or strengthening for our conscience" (v2 15709–15711); of private confession, "I know what comfort and strength it has given me" (v2 15679–15680); baptism is "a gracious covenant of comfort" (v1 2415–2416, per Doc_03); the Supper "a pure wholesome, comforting remedy" (LC 4389, per Doc_05). At burial, songs so that we "comfort one another with God's word" (Hy 946–949, per Doc_03); in the hymn, "His precious word assureth me; / My solace, my sure rock is he" (Hy 1675–1677, Bacon's English). Christ sends the Spirit "to rule, comfort, and quicken them" (AC 221–223). The *Fourteen of Consolation* (v1 3994 ff., [R5]) — the register's own treatise — is unread.

**Ecological Function.** G5's affective content, carried by 2.8; the purpose of 2.2 and the work of 5.10, 5.2, 5.3, 4.9; Doc_05 §8's "Hopes." Not a generator — Tier 2.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* comfort as reassurance or emotional support. *World Hearing:* the setting-at-rest of a terrified conscience by a promise — the thing every sacrament and absolution exists to give.

**Key Texts.** AC 546–553; Ap 566–567, 1427–1430; v2 15679–15683, 15709–15724; Hy 1675–1677. **Key Sources.** [R37] AC XX; [R38]; [R15]; [R5] unread. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 2.8 conscience; 9.3 assurance; 5.10 confession/absolution; 2.5 grace; 5.3 Sacrament of the Altar; 9.4 death; 9.2 temptation.

#### 9.2 temptation / trial — "the devil, the world, and the flesh" (*Bekoerunge*) — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices (Ap 3737–3738, 5133–5134, 5155, 5550; §4; Doc_03 had "Luther-only, cross-register"). **Registry:** R13, R15, R25, R26, R31, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The threefold assault the catechisms name — "of the flesh, of the world and of the devil" — which no Christian escapes and in which prayer and faith are learned: "Need teacheth to pray."

**World Meaning.** "Temptation, however, or (as our Saxons in olden times used to call it) Bekoerunge, is of three kinds, namely, of the flesh, of the world and of the devil" (LC 3632–3634) — the one original-language gloss the founder himself supplies for this word (Doc_05 §11 item 2; no vendored text glosses *Anfechtung*, which occurs once, in an unrelated footnote sense, v2 5418, per Doc_03). "No one can escape temptation and allurements… but we pray for this, that we may not fall and be drowned in them" (LC 3669–3672, per Doc_05); "the young suffer especially from the flesh, afterwards, they that attain to middle life and old age, from the world, but others who are occupied with spiritual matters, that is, strong Christians, from the devil" (LC 3677–3680). The household prays against "the Devil, the world and our bodily desires" (SC 331–335, per Doc_03). "Without trouble, trials, and vexations, prayer cannot rightly be made… the common saying is 'Need teacheth to pray'" (TT 3076–3079, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim). Faith "arises in repentance, and ought to be established and grow in the midst of good works, temptations, and dangers" (Ap 3736–3738); it "is nourished in a manifold way in temptations" (Ap 5155); the doctrine gives consciences "[in agony of death and temptation] sure comfort" (Ap 5550). "Every one must fight his own battle" (v2 14642, per Doc_03).

**Ecological Function.** G12's frame in the person and G5's school (Doc_05 §2.4 vii: "Need teaches; trial is the school"; §8's age-graded fears); anchors 9.5, 4.7, 1.7; presupposes 9.1.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* temptation as the urge to a specific sin. *World Hearing:* a lifelong threefold assault, expected and age-graded, in which faith and prayer are formed — and the world's own old Saxon word for it.

**Key Texts.** LC 3632–3634, 3669–3680; SC 331–335; TT 3074–3079 (Bell's English); Ap 3734–3741, 5152–5157 (read this pass). **Key Sources.** [R25] LC Sixth Petition (read at Doc_05); [R26]; [R31]; [R38]. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 4.7 the devil; 9.5 prayer; 9.1 comfort; 1.7 the cross; 9.4 death; 2.1 faith.

#### 9.3 assurance / "sure" / "certain" — against despair and "conditionally" — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 1 ↓)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none. **Registry:** R2, R4, R6, R15, R26, R27, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The certainty this world says its doctrine gives and the old way withheld — "I cannot doubt I have a gracious God" — against praying "conditionally, and therefore uncertainly," and against despair.

**World Meaning.** "The assurance of salvation by letters of pardon is vain" (v1 1348–1349; the file's own "53."). Works done by people who "do not know, or they doubt" whether they please God "are nothing and altogether dead" (v1 6845–6852, per Doc_03). The one who has taken the Sacrament "cannot doubt I have a gracious God" (v2 15723–15724); the household prays "That I should be certain that such prayers are acceptable" (SC 351–352); the confession makes remission "sure": "If the matter were to depend upon our merits, the promise would be uncertain and useless, because we never could determine when we would have sufficient merit" (Ap 1190–1193), and "the pious may receive the sure hope of salvation" where the adversaries "bid them doubt" (Ap 1434–1439). The old way is remembered as "conditionally": "we always prayed in Popedom *conditionaliter*, conditionally, and therefore uncertainly" (TT 3063–3064, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim); "at a venture" (LC 3083, per Doc_05); "all depends upon this, that we learn also to say Amen, that is, that we do not doubt that our prayer is surely heard" (LC 3738–3739, per Doc_05 §2.6). The antonym is despair, to which traditions drove men so that "many fell into despair" (AC 991–992, per Doc_03), and "Grief drove me to despair" (Hy 1288, Bacon's English).

**Ecological Function.** G5's goal-word (Doc_05 §9A pattern 5: "its purpose is certainty"); the promise-logic's yield (2.7); the Amen of 9.5; refuses 1.1's false assurance and 2.4's uncertainty. Not a generator — Tier 2.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* assurance as self-confidence, or as presumption; "certain" as proven. *World Hearing:* a certainty grounded outside the self, in a promise that "cannot fail or deceive me," against the self-torture of "one week trails another"; the whole doctrine's purpose.

**Key Texts.** v1 1348–1349, 6845–6852; v2 15722–15724; SC 351–352; Ap 1186–1209, 1430–1442; TT 3052–3064 (Bell's English); LC 3738–3749. **Key Sources.** [R2]; [R6]; [R15]; [R26]; [R38]; [R31]; [R25] LC Prayer (read at Doc_05). Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 2.8 conscience; 2.2 justification; 2.7 promise/testament; 9.1 comfort; 9.5 prayer; 1.1 indulgence; 2.1 faith; 1.2 repentance; 2.6 Law and Gospel.

#### 9.4 death — "a deep, sound, sweet sleep"; burial — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [DR] [RT]. **AG:** none for death as the terror faith overcomes (Ap 1439–1442, 5550, 8577–8579; §4); Luther-only for the sleep/burial register. **Registry:** R3, R15, R26, R28, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** The battle each must fight alone, which faith is to learn to despise "as a deep, sound, sweet sleep" — buried with "no dirges nor lamentations, but comforting songs," in a churchyard renamed a sleeping-place.

**World Meaning.** "The challenge of death comes to us all, and no one can die for another. Every one must fight his own battle with death by himself, alone… I will not be with you then, nor you with me" (v2 14641–14644). The deathbed is where the devil attacks (v2 14994–14995, per Doc_05) and where a "free" thing made a "must" terrifies (v2 14798–14801). The confession's question — "how will such persons sustain themselves in death who have heard nothing of this faith?" (Ap 1439–1441) — is answered by "the terrors of sin and death must be overcome by faith" (Ap 8577–8578). Baptism already begins it: "the life of a Christian, from baptism to the grave, is nothing else than the beginning of a blessed death" (v1 2319–2321, per Doc_03); the household prays for "a blessed death" (SC 345–347, per Doc_03). The founder's funeral preface: "we Christians… should exercise and wont ourselves in faith to despise death, to look on it as a deep, sound, sweet sleep, the coffin no other than the bosom of our Lord Christ, or paradise, the grave nought but a soft couch of rest" (Hy 957–962); "no dirges nor lamentations, but comforting songs" (Hy 983–984); churchyards "as the primitive Fathers called them, 'Cemeteries,' that is, resting and sleeping places" (Hy 980–981); yet "it is meet and right to give care and honor to the burial of the dead" (Hy 989–990, per Doc_05).

**Ecological Function.** A pastoral topic G5 organizes (Doc_04 §2.2's fold); Doc_05 §1.1 ("the dying" as the one participant every register reaches) and §1.2; anchors 9.1, 4.9 (the burial songs), 1.5 (what is abolished at the grave), 5.2. Claimed practice, Widely Accepted; no burial recorded.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* death as an end faced by inner resolve, and burial as private grief. *World Hearing:* the last battle, faced alone but on a text; a sleep and a couch; a burial sung, honoured and stripped of masses for the soul.

**Key Texts.** v2 14641–14644; Hy 957–990 (Bacon's English); v1 2319–2321; SC 345–347; Ap 1439–1442, 8577–8579. **Key Sources.** [R15] Sermon 1; [R28] the 1542 preface; [R3]; [R26]; [R38]. Author Gravity: the sleep register is Luther's — disclosed.

**Related Terms.** 9.1 comfort; 9.2 temptation; 4.7 the devil; 4.9 hymn; 5.2 baptism; 1.5 purgatory; 2.8 conscience.

#### 9.5 prayer — "Need teacheth to pray"; commandment, promise, form — Tier 2 (Doc_03: 2)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT]. **AG:** none — both voices (Ap 6510–6515, 6925–6927, 8712–8714; AC 575–579 per Doc_05; §4); and the one non-founder voice in the library is *about this practice* (Doc_05 §2.6). **Registry:** R14, R15, R25, R26, R31, R37, R38. **Living tradition:** 1517–1545 function only.

**Quick Meaning.** Commanded as strictly as any commandment, promised an answer, and given its form in the Lord's Prayer — the household's daily frame, the community's "wall of iron," a weapon and a sign, learned in need and never "at a venture."

**World Meaning.** "Prayer is therefore as strictly and earnestly commanded as all other commandments: to have no other God, not to kill, not to steal, etc." (LC 3015–3017, per Doc_05 §2.6); "It is our duty and obligation to pray if we would be Christians, as much as it is our duty and obligation to obey our parents and the government" (LC 3032–3034, per Doc_05). It has a promise: "Here I come, dear Father, and pray, not of my own purpose nor upon my own worthiness, but at Thy commandment and promise, which cannot fail or deceive me" (LC 3121–3123, per Doc_05). And a form: "God anticipates us, and Himself arranges the words and form of prayer for us, and places them upon our lips" (LC 3128–3130, per Doc_05) — "a great advantage indeed over all other prayers that we might compose ourselves. For in them the conscience would ever be in doubt" (LC 3133–3136, per Doc_05). It is the community's defense — "all our shelter and protection rest in prayer alone" (LC 3185–3186, per Doc_05); "the prayer of a few godly men intervened like a wall of iron on our side" (LC 3193–3194, per Doc_05) — and the household's rhythm (SC 543–607). Its old form was doubt: "we always prayed in Popedom *conditionaliter*" (TT 3063–3064, Bell's English); the monks' prayers "howl and growl day and night like fiends" (LC 3148–3149, per Doc_05). "Need teacheth to pray" (TT 3079, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim); "a strong wall, and a fort of the church" (TT 3088, per Doc_03). The confessional voice: "he that knows that he has a Father gracious to him through Christ… calls upon God" (AC 575–579, per Doc_05); prayer "most truly can be called a sacrament" (Ap 6512), and the sermons teach "that we should be fully confident that it is efficacious" (Ap 6926–6927). Before the mass was changed in 1522 the congregation should have "called upon God in earnest prayer" (v2 14764–14765). And the one sentence from the household's other side is about it: "Sir! how is it, that in Popedom they pray so often with great vehemence, but we are very cold and careless in praying?" (TT 3147–3148, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim).

**Ecological Function.** The fold Doc_04 §2.2 deferred and Doc_05 §2.6 tested on the Lord's Prayer read entire: "prayer reinforcing eight gravities and organizing no content of its own" — its logic G3's (command and promise), its argument G5's (against doubt), its location G4's, its enemy G12's; the fold's destination should add G3 and G5 (Doc_05 §13 item 4). Tier 2 at the richest Tier 2 depth. Anchors 4.8, 9.2, 9.3 (the Amen), 4.7, 7.4 (the "wall of iron"), 4.2. The vocabulary of coldness — "cold," "coldly," "ice cold" — is prayer's own in both the founder's and the household's mouths (Doc_05 §2.6), across two translators' English.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* prayer as spontaneous, private, optional devotion; set forms as lesser. *World Hearing:* a commanded duty with a promised answer and a given form, prayed with certainty ("Amen"), the household's frame and the community's wall — and the practice whose coldness the world reports of itself.

**Key Texts.** LC 3006–3141, 3148–3149, 3174–3196, 3738–3749 (read entire at Doc_05); SC 235–238, 351–352, 537–607; TT 3027–3034, 3054–3064, 3076–3090, 3147–3151 (Bell's English); AC 575–579; Ap 6510–6515, 6925–6927, 8712–8714; v2 14764–14766. **Key Sources.** [R25] LC Third Part, entire (Doc_05 §11); [R26]; [R31] (Contested as verbatim); [R37] AC XX; [R38] Ap XIII, XV, XXIV; [R14] the *Kurze Form*'s own Lord's Prayer (v2 13342–14402) unread. Author Gravity: none.

**Related Terms.** 4.8 daily; 4.2 household; 4.1 catechism; 2.7 promise/testament; 9.3 assurance; 9.2 temptation; 4.7 the devil; 7.4 insurrection; 6.9 the Turk; 9.7 patience.

#### 9.6 martyr — "the two youths" — Tier 3 (Doc_03: 3)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT]. **AG:** Luther-only, single-register (Hymn V), in a translator's English — the weakest evidentiary base of any entry (Doc_03 9.6). **Registry:** R30.

**Quick Meaning.** The library's one martyrology — two young monks, "John" and "Henry," "burnt at Brussels by the Sophists of Louvain" (Hy 1744–1745, heading), who "For God's dear Word… shed their blood" (Hy 1763) and, stripped of "monkish garb" (Hy 1789–1790), were made "True priests of God's own making" (Hy 1800); the hymn names no order.

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a martyr-cult, or a fact-record. *World Hearing:* a ballad in Bacon's English, in one register — the community's one statement of what a faithful life under ultimate pressure looks like (Doc_02 §9), and a Doc_09 story more than a lexicon term.

#### 9.7 patience — "my whole life is nothing but patience" — Tier 3 (Doc_03: 3)

**Tags:** [SC] [RT]. **AG:** none for the word (Ap 1524, 2545, 4506; §4); the household sentence is Luther's. **Registry:** R15, R25, R31, R38.

**Quick Meaning.** The fourth of the 1522 sermon's "chief things" — "patience works and produces hope" (v2 14696–14704, per Doc_03) — the strong's bearing with the weak ("have patience with him for a time," v2 14719–14720), a fruit of the Spirit in the confession's lists, and the founder's household confession: "the patiences are so many, that my whole life is nothing but patience" (TT 3014–3015, Bell's English, Contested as verbatim).

**Distortion Risk.** *Modern Hearing:* a temperament. *World Hearing:* the whole of a life under persecution, pace and marriage — "Patience is weighted as the whole of a life" (Doc_05 §8).

## 6. Master index — derived from the entries, not maintained beside them

The project's lexicon-index discipline asks that tier, tag, [CT] status and Author-Gravity risk be answerable without opening every entry, and that the index be *derived* from the entries rather than kept as a second list that can drift. The table below was generated by script from §5's own entry headers, Tags lines, AG lines, Registry lines and Related Terms lines after the draft was closed (§10 names the script), and every column is therefore a reading of the entries, not an assertion beside them. **Reading the index.** Tier 1 = 18, Tier 2 = 48, Tier 3 = 6 (§1.2). [AS] = 10, unchanged from Doc_03 (OG-3). [DR] = 60 (Doc_03's 57 plus the three disclosed additions at §1.5). [PV] = 3 (5.6, 6.7, 7.4), unchanged. [CT] = 2 (2.2, 7.1), both with Contest Type filled, both provisional (§2). RES = 3 (4.1, 4.2, 4.9; 4.1 added this revision, Round 1 finding C7). AG risk: 48 "No," 18 "Yes — single-voice/register," 5 "Weighted," 1 alias-problem note (1.4). No Excluded Registry row (R33, R55, R57, R58, R94, R95) is drawn on *as a source* in any Registry column: R94 appears once, at entry 7.4, transparently as the bar it is (see 7.4's Ecological Function), never as evidence, and the rest appear nowhere. Several rows are carried in an entry's `Registry:` line with a qualifier — "(context)," "as named absence," "as unread lead," "(bars)" — and the Registry-rows column strips those qualifiers to bare row codes (corrected this revision to state the fact without a stale enumeration, Round 3 finding R3-C4: an earlier form of this sentence named only R43, R44 and R48, when R56, R59 and R61 also carry a qualifier this way). A reader should check the entry's own `Registry:` header line for the marker before treating a row as a source (corrected to point at the field where the markers actually live, Round 2 finding R2-C7; an earlier form of this sentence pointed at Key Sources, which is where the same markers lived before Round 1's S1 fix moved them). This is the Gallic index's S5 defect the sentence exists to guard against. **The Registry column is not a complete list of an entry's sources**: it derives from the entry's own `Registry:` header line only, and a number of entries name additional rows inside Key Sources that their `Registry:` line omits — a reader who wants every source an entry draws on should read that entry's Key Sources directly, not stop at this index (Round 1 finding C5; the enumerated count first given here at Revision 1 did not match a script re-derivation and is not repeated, Round 2 finding R2-S2).

## 7. Cross-reference pass — Related-Terms reciprocity, computed

The Lexicon Framework's Development Workflow step 5 ([162]) asks for a cross-reference pass "checking that Related Terms references are reciprocal and that conceptual clusters identified during Conceptual Ecology work are visibly reflected in the lexicon's cross-reference structure." Computed from §5's Related Terms lines by script, not asserted:

- **565 directed Related-Terms edges across the 66 Tier 1 and Tier 2 entries; 274 reciprocal pairs; 17 one-directional edges — every one of them into a Tier 3 entry.** Tier 3 entries carry no Related Terms section in this document, on the reading taken of the Framework's own text: [144]'s Tier 3 shape ("should contain only: Quick Meaning, and… a single-line Distortion Risk note") supports omitting it, but the Framework is not univocal — [137] states for Tier 2 that "Related Terms should still be included, since cross-referencing remains cheap and valuable regardless of tier," and Part VI's Retrieval Front-Matter lists Related Terms for every chunk at every tier. The tension is named rather than resolved silently (Round 1 finding C8), and the reading taken here follows [144]'s explicit Tier 3 list over the cheaper-but-general principle [137] states for Tier 2. So an edge into a Tier 3 entry cannot be reciprocated here; the seventeen are: 4.1→4.3, 4.1→4.5, 4.4→4.5, 8.5→4.5, 5.1→5.7, 5.3→5.7, 4.7→6.9, 4.9→6.9, 6.6→6.9, 6.7→6.9, 9.5→6.9, 4.9→9.6, 6.2→9.6, 8.7→9.6, 7.5→9.7, 8.3→9.7, 9.5→9.7. They are listed rather than smoothed; Phase B's chunks, which carry Related-Terms front-matter at every tier, can close them.
- **How the reciprocal set was reached, disclosed:** the first assembled draft carried 507 directed edges with 75 one-directional, 58 of them into Tier 1 or Tier 2 entries. Each of the 58 was read by this drafting pass as a candidate return link (does the target entry's own content connect back to the source?) and all 58 were judged warranted and added — no link was added that the target entry's World Meaning or Ecological Function does not already presuppose. The Gallic Doc_06's reason for not reconciling its own gaps (a fix round adding links "no drafting pass actually wrote") does not apply here, because this is the drafting pass. An independent review should test a sample of the added links against the entries' content rather than take this sentence's word.
- **Clusters visible in the cross-reference structure.** Doc_05 §10B's chain G2 → G4 → G3 → G5 is a path in the graph (3.1 → 4.1 → 5.1/5.3 → 2.8, every step reciprocal); its two hubs are the two most-linked Tier 1 entries (3.1 the Word and 2.8 conscience, each named by more than a dozen entries); its tension points are edges (3.1 ↔ 7.5; 4.1 ↔ 5.11 and 4.1 ↔ 6.5 for G4–G13; 7.5 ↔ 5.3 for must/free against the Supper). Doc_03's nine clusters remain visible as the section structure; the cross-cluster edges (e.g., 1.2 ↔ 2.8, 5.10 ↔ 2.8, 6.2 ↔ 4.2, 8.3 ↔ 4.2, 7.5 ↔ 8.6) are where the ecology's own connections cut across the candidate-stage grouping, which is what the Framework says the pass exists to reveal.

## 8. Confidence review (Lexicon Framework Development Workflow step 6; Constitution Article 17; Doc_02 §14)

Every claim about how a term *functioned* was checked against the five-level vocabulary as Doc_02 §14 calibrated it. The pattern, stated once: the doctrinal and confessional content of every entry is **Documented** at its loci (verified by script, §10); the founder's own registers are **Documented** as his; the household program (4.1, 4.2, 4.8, 9.5, 5.11) is **Documented** as prescription and **Inferential/Thin** as any household's practice, with the reading of reception **Contested** (R70–R72); the movement's parish practice (5.6, 4.9, 5.3, 5.10) is **Widely Accepted** as claimed and **Inferential/Thin** as any parish's; every Table Talk saying (in 2.8, 4.4, 4.7, 7.3, 8.3, 8.7, 9.2, 9.3, 9.5, 9.7, 6.5, 6.7) is **Contested** as verbatim and Documented as Aurifaber's report; the 1522 outcome (7.5) is **Widely Accepted** at the editor's word; the apocalyptic reading of the adversary frame (4.7) is **Dominant Modern Reconstruction** at most, unread, and not built on; the organizing role of the presence against the Reformed (5.3) is **untagged**, as Doc_04 §3 G10 left it, pending [R56]; the two [CT] contests are **Contested** as to how the in-text wordings relate and **Documented** as to the wordings themselves; and the ten [AS] tags are **Inferential/Thin** as comparative claims. No entry grants a term certainty about how it was *held* because it is well attested as *existing* — the Confidence Inheritance Principle's own warning ([43]). Three entries carry Reported-Experience Status (4.1, 4.2, 4.9), each stating why.

## 10. Loci read this pass; verification run; corrections to prior documents

**Read directly this pass, in the vendored files, at the ranges given:** **Ap** 771–1457 (Article IV, Parts 2–5, to the heading of Part 6), 4935–5234 (Article XII, the condemned dogmas and the two-parts argument), 6415–6566 (Article XIII entire), 6613–6991 (Article XV entire), 8567–8806 (Article XXIV, the *opus operatum* statement and "What a Sacrifice Is"), 9561–10194 (Article XXVII entire); and, by grep with the hit's context read, Ap 1745–1752, 3734–3741, 4418–4424, 5011–5021, 5130–5137, 5152–5158, 5546–5553, 5606–5612, 5620–5632, 5735–5742, 6363–6378, 7605–7616, 7724–7730, 8716–8724, 9860–9868, 10406–10412 (§4's flag discharge). **AC** 166–191, 208–231 (Articles I and III entire). **LC** 521–680 (the First Commandment's remainder). **Every other locus** this document quotes was verified this pass by script at its line rather than re-read in context: after the draft was closed, a checker parsed every quotation followed by a file-and-line citation (759 quotations across the whole document, §0–§12) and tested each, whitespace-, quotation-mark- and italic-normalized and with footnote markers and bracketed scripture references removed, as a substring of the cited lines ±1. On the first run four did not match and each was repaired rather than exempted silently: two were false positives (a term name and a piece's title in quotation marks adjacent to a range citation; both now italicized instead), and two were the running marginal "The Two Kingdoms" (v3 12123–12127), whose text the file prints as "The Two King- ' doms" and which both citations now mark as OCR-broken. Quotations from the two OCR-degraded files (v3, Co) marked "OCR" were exempted from the exact match and compared by eye. **This drafting checker's "all 759 matched" claim was itself found overstated at Round 1 (S2) and is not repeated here.** An independent 831-pair check (a wider extraction than this checker's own 759 — it also tested this document's 112 quotations of Doc_03/04/05) found two genuine defects, of two different kinds this checker's substring test could not catch (corrected this revision to distinguish them, Round 2 finding R2-C5): 8.3's "a divine and blessed estate," a phrase merged under a second phrase's citation and so passing as a substring of *some* nearby text, now corrected to LC 1692 (Round 1 S2); and 6.6's condensation of "merely do not have their priests and bishops confirmed by Rome" down to "not confirmed by Rome" without ellipsis — a single quotation shortened, not a merged citation — now restored to its full verbatim text (Round 1 C1). Adjusting for the reviewer's own parser artefacts, the adjudicated rate was 99.75% (829 of 831) before these two fixes, not the 100% this checker had certified — the gap is the lesson: a substring test confirms a quotation appears near its citation, not that it is *the* quotation the citation names. **The checker is the build thread's own one-off script and is not retained as a repository artifact** (Doc_03 Round 3's R3-2 is the error class: a named script that does not exist); its method is described here so that a reviewer can re-run the same test. **No file was read beyond the ranges named above; no live research was used** (the leads at §2 are prior knowledge, marked as such, for the Registry owner).

**Corrections and findings for prior documents — none changing a prior classification, determination or confidence rating:**

1. **Doc_03 4.4 (LC loci).** Doc_03 cites "the confidence and faith of the heart alone make both God and an idol" at LC 431–433 and "the most common idol on earth" at LC 446–448. In the file the first sentence is at LC 411–412 (inside the 408–416 definition) and the second at LC 433; LC 447–448 carries "the chief explanation of this point is that to have a god is to have something in which the heart entirely trusts." The quotations are exact; the loci were one sentence off. Corrected in 4.4 here; logged for Doc_03's owner.
2. **Doc_03 8.5 / 8.1 (AC loci).** "a state of perfection" is at AC 1130–1131 (Doc_03 lists 1113–1115 first, which carries "evangelical counsels"); "wicked services, and, consequently, are void" is at AC 1204–1206 (Doc_03's 1146–1148 carries the vow-annuls-commandment sentence). Corrected here; logged.
3. **Doc_03 9.1 (v2 15709–15711).** The file reads "only one comfort or strengthening for our conscience," not "comfort and strengthening." Corrected at 9.1; logged.
4. **Doc_04 §3 G6 / Doc_05 §1.2 (v2 14765).** Both prior documents quote "the aid of the authorities"; a first draft of 5.3 here compressed it to "with the aid of the authorities," which the file does not say — caught by the checker and restored to "had obtained the aid of the authorities." Recorded so that Phase B does not inherit the compressed form.
5. **Doc_04 §3 G6 / R19 (v3 10670–10672).** The file prints "as the peasants! are threat- ening to do" — an OCR "!" and a line-break hyphen that R19's "verified verbatim" and Doc_04's quotation both silently smooth. The reading is not in doubt; 7.4 here quotes it with the OCR note. Proposed Verification-Note addition for the Registry owner (R19).
6. **Doc_03 §0 / Registry R87 — a lemma the text supplies.** *Bekoerunge* (LC 3632–3634), found at Doc_05, is now recorded at 9.2 as the founder's own gloss; the list of text-supplied original-language forms at Doc_03 Discipline 2 should gain it when Doc_03 is next revised.
7. **Registry R38 (Apology)** — proposed Verification-Note extension, under the append-only discipline: Ap 771–1457, 4935–5234, 6415–6566, 6613–6991, 8567–8806, 9561–10194 read in full this pass; every quotation this document draws from them verified at the line. **R37 (AC):** Articles I and III (166–191, 208–231) now read. **R25 (LC):** 521–680 now read — the First Commandment exposition is now read entire across Doc_03 and this pass.
8. **Doc_02 §12.3 / §7.1 boundary against the Jews.** Nothing in this lexicon carries a term for it, because no vendored text is licensed for anything but disclosure (R49, R94); the Representative's obligation to acknowledge the 1543 treatise is Doc_02 §12.3's and Doc_05 §7.1's, and it is not a lexicon term. Stated so the absence is not read as an oversight.
